Isometric group actions with vanishing rate of escape on CAT ⁡ ( 0 ) \mathrm{CAT}(0) spaces Thanks: This work was supported by JSPS Grants-in-Aid for Scientific Research Grant Number JP20H01802.
Hiroyasu Izeki

Abstract
. Let Y = ( Y , d ) Y=(Y,d) be a CAT ⁡ ( 0 ) \mathrm{CAT}(0) space which is either proper or of finite telescopic dimension, and Γ \Gamma a countable group equipped with a symmetric and nondegenerate probability measure μ \mu . Suppose that Γ \Gamma acts on Y Y via a homomorphism ρ : Γ → Isom ⁡ ( Y ) \rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y}) , where Isom ⁡ ( Y ) \mathrm{Isom}({Y}) denotes the isometry group of Y Y , and that the action given by ρ \rho has finite second moment with respect to μ \mu . We show that if ρ ⁡ ( Γ ) \rho(\Gamma) does not fix a point in the boundary at infinity ∂ Y \partial Y of Y Y and the rate of escape l ρ ​ ( Γ ) = l ρ ​ ( Γ , μ ) l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=l_{\rho}(\Gamma,\mu) associated to an action given by ρ \rho vanishes, then there exists a flat subspace in Y Y that is left invariant under the action of ρ ⁡ ( Γ ) \rho(\Gamma) . Note that if the rate of escape does not vanish, then we know that there exists an equivariant map from the Poisson boundary of ( Γ , μ ) (\Gamma,\mu) into the boundary at infinity of Y Y by a result of A. Karlsson and G. Margulis. The key ingredient of the proof is μ \mu -harmonic functions on Γ \Gamma and μ \mu -harmonic maps from Γ \Gamma into Y Y . We prove a result similar to the above for an isometric action of Γ \Gamma on a locally finite-dimensional CAT ⁡ ( 0 ) \mathrm{CAT}(0) space.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
53C23, 20F65

1.
Introduction

Let
\Gamma
be a countable group equipped with a probability
measure
\mu
.
We regard
\mu
as the law of a random walk; for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
we regard
\mu(\gamma)
as the transition probability of a move from the
identity element
e
to
\gamma
.
Then the
n
-fold convolution
\mu^{n}
of
\mu
tells us the distribution
of the position of the random walk, generated by
\mu
and starting from

e
, at time
n
.

Suppose that
\Gamma
acts on a metric space
Y=(Y,d_{Y})
via a
homomorphism
\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
, where
\mathrm{Isom}({Y})

denotes the isometry group of
Y
.
Take any point
p\in Y
and consider an orbit map

f_{p}\colon\gamma\mapsto\rho(\gamma)p
.
Then we can transplant the random walk generated by
\mu
to
the orbit
f_{p}(\Gamma)=\rho(\Gamma)p\subset Y
.
The behavior of this transplanted random walk depends on how

\Gamma
acts on
Y
. When
Y
is a nonpositively curved
metric space, one can ask the following convergence question:
for which action do almost all sample paths of the transplanted random
walk converge to the “boundary at infinity”
\partial Y
of
Y
?
If almost all sample paths converge to the boundary at infinity,
then this means that any orbit map induces a canonical equivariant map from
the Poisson boundary
\partial(\Gamma,\mu)
, a probability space that
describes the distribution of the position of a random walk on
\Gamma

generated by
\mu
at “time infinity”, into
\partial Y
.
Such a boundary map is known to be very useful tool in rigidity theory
as we can see, for example, in a pioneering work due to H. Furstenberg
discussed in
[
14
]
, and a superrigidity result due to
G. Margulis
[
34
]
,
[
35
]
.

There have been many works related to the convergence question above.
Lie groups and their subgroups acting on associated symmetric spaces are
classical and well-understood ones (see
[
4
]
).
V. Kaimanovich and H. Masur
[
27
]
treated mapping class
groups acting on Teichmüller spaces and showed that, under certain
conditions, the Poisson boundary of a mapping class group can be
realized on the Thurston boundary of corresponding Teichmüller space.
Also a theorem due to Kaimanovich
[
26
]
says that it is the
case for hyperbolic-type groups acting on themselves, and that the
Poisson boundary of these groups
can be realized on the boundary at infinity of themselves.
Then, for example, F. Gautero and F. Mathéus
[
15
]

treated certain groups acting on
{\mathbb{R}}
-trees, and J. Maher and G. Tiozzo

[
33
]
treated groups acting on separable Gromov hyperbolic
spaces. Also groups acting on
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
cubical complexes were
considered by T. Fernós, J. Lécureux and F. Mathéus

[
12
]
.
Recently Chois
[
9
]
treated groups containing elements with
bounded geodesic image property acting on various nonpositively curved
spaces. The reader can find more works on the question in the references
of papers cited above.

On the other hand, there has been a known criterion for the
convergence to the boundary for groups acting on general nonpositively
curved metric spaces due to A. Karlsson and G. Margulis

[
28
]
.
Consider the
rate of escape

l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=l_{\rho}(\Gamma,\mu)
of an action given by
\rho

which turns out to be equal to

l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\int_{\Gamma}d_{Y}(p,\rho(\gamma)p)d\mu^{n}(\gamma)}{n}

(see §
3
).
This limit is known to exist, and since the numerator of the right-hand
side is the average of the distance between the starting point and the
position at time
n
of the transplanted random walk, it measures the
speed of the transplanted random walk’s escape to the boundary at
infinity
\partial Y
of
Y
.
Indeed, for a nonpositively curved metric space
Y
,
A. Karlsson and G. Margulis proved in
[
28
]
that if

l_{\rho}(\Gamma)>0
, then
almost all sample paths of the transplanted random walk
leave every bounded subset fast enough and go almost straight toward

\partial Y
;
almost all sample paths converge to points in
\partial Y
.
However, it is usually difficult to check the positivity of

l_{\rho}(\Gamma)
. For example, in

[
12
]
and
[
33
]
, the positivity
of
l_{\rho}(\Gamma)
was obtained as a consequence of the convergence
to the boundary.

If
l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=0
, then the transplanted random
walk is rather wandering around in
Y
.
Thus one might think that information obtained from such a random
walk should contain lots of noise, and that it is hard to
understand.
Indeed, it seems that there has been no known efficient method to handle
this case in full generality.
However, under mild assumptions on
Y
and
\rho
, our approach using
harmonic functions and harmonic maps associated to
\mu
and
\rho

enables us to specify such an action as stated in
Theorem
A
below; an action given by
\rho
with

l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=0
turns out to be the simplest one in the sense that
it is reduced to that on a Euclidean space.

Theorem A
.

Let
Y
be a complete
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space which is either proper or
of finite telescopic dimension,
and
\Gamma
a countable group equipped with a symmetric and
nondegenerate probablity measure
\mu
.
Let
\Gamma
act on
Y
via a homomorphism

\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
, and
suppose that
\rho(\Gamma)
does not fix a point in

\partial Y
and that the action given by
\rho
has finite
second moment with respect to
\mu
.
If
l_{\rho}(\Gamma,\mu)=0
, then there exists a flat subspace
F
in

Y
, which is a convex subset of
Y
isometric to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

(n\geq 0)
,
left invariant under the action of
\rho(\Gamma)
.

The case
n=0
in the theorem above should be understood as that

\rho(\Gamma)
fixes a point in
Y
.
Let us briefly explain the assumptions in Theorem
A
.
(See §
2
for precise definitions and more
details.)
To obtain sufficient information of actions of
\Gamma
from the
random walk generated by
\mu
, we assume that

\mu
is symmetric and nondegenerate.
Namely,
\mu
satisfies
\mu(\gamma^{-1})=\mu(\gamma)
, and the support

\mathrm{supp}\,{\mu}
of
\mu
generates
\Gamma
.
We also assume that the action given by
\rho
has finite second
moment with respect to
\mu
:

\int_{\Gamma}d_{S}(p,\rho(\gamma)p)^{2}d\mu(\gamma)<\infty

holds for
p\in Y
;
it is easy to see that, by the triangle inequality
and the Schwarz inequality, this does not depend on the choice of

p\in Y
. If
\Gamma
is finitely generated and
\mu
has finite
second moment with respect to some (hence any) word metric on
\Gamma
,
then any
\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
gives the action with
finite second moment (see §
2
).
A metric space under consideration is a nonpositively curved metric
space called
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space.
An important feature of
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces on which our approach relies
is the convexity of distance functions.
We also assume that a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
is either proper or of finite
telescopic dimension in order to provide
Y
with a certain compactness
property.
Here a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
is called
proper
if every closed metric
ball
B(p,r)
,
p\in Y
,
r>0
, is compact, while a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y

is said to have
telescopic dimension
at most
n

if every asymptotic cone of
Y
has geometric dimension at most
n
;
the notion of geometric dimension and telescopic dimension was
introduced by B. Kleiner in
[
29
]
and by
P.-E. Caprace and A. Lytchak in
[
6
]
, respectively.
(See §
6.3
for more details.)
The assumption that
\rho(\Gamma)
does not fix a point in
\partial Y

guarantees the existance of a
\rho
-equiavariant
\mu
-harmonic map

f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
.
Here, a
\rho
-equivariant map
f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
is called

\mu
-
harmonic
if
f
minimizes the energy
E_{\mu}
defined by

E_{\mu}(f)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}d_{Y}(f(e),f(\gamma))^{2}d\mu(\gamma)

among all
\rho
-equivariant maps.
As equivariant maps from the Poisson boundary, equivariant harmonic
maps are also known to be useful tools in rigidity theory.
For example, K. Corlette
[
10
]
,
N. Mok, Y.-T. Siu and S.-K. Yeung
[
36
]
,
J. Jost and S.-T. Yau
[
24
]
,
[
25
]
, and
M. Gromov and R. Schoen
[
17
]
used the existence
of nonconstant equivariant harmonic maps from Riemannian symmetric
spaces in order to prove superrigidity results for lattices in Lie
groups, where the local geometric structure of the domains of harmonic
maps has key information to determine the images of these harmonic
maps and deduce superrigidity results.
Also discrete equivariant harmonic maps from the vertex set of a
simplicial complex or a finitely generated group itself were
treated by,
for example, M. Gromov
[
16
]
, M.-T. Wang
[
39
]
, and
H. Kondo, S. Nayatani and the author
[
22
]
,

[
20
]
,
[
21
]
,

[
19
]
, where various fixed-point theorems for isometric actions
of finitely generated groups on
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces were obtained from the
existence of constant equivariant harmonic maps.
While in the present paper, we prove Theorem
A
by using
the existence of a nonconstant equivariant harmonic map defined on a
countable group.
Typically, the image of our harmonic map is nowhere dense in the
target space or even in the convex hull of the image; the space we need
to specify its structure is far from being covered by the image.
In order to prove our theorem, however, we need to fill this huge
complement.
We achieve this by using the convexity of distance
functions of the target space effectively.

The outline of the proof of Theorem
A
is as follows.
Since
Y
is either proper or of finite telescopic dimension, under the
assumption that
\rho(\Gamma)
does not fix a point in
\partial Y
,
one sees that there exists a
\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map

f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
(see §
2
and
Proposition
6.11
).
A
\mu
-harmonic map has a property that the pull-back of a convex
function
u
on the target space
Y
becomes a
\mu
-subharmomnic
function on
\Gamma
, that is,
-\Delta f^{*}u(\gamma)\geq 0
holds for
any
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
Here, one may regard the value

|-\Delta f^{*}u(\gamma)|
as a quantity that measures the strength of
the convexity of a function
u
around
f(\gamma)
.
We note here that a distance function
p\mapsto d(p,p_{0})
is a convex
function on
Y
, since
Y
is a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space,
and that the strength of the convexity of a distance function should be
related to the strength of the nonpositivity (negativity) of the
“curvature” of
Y
.
Now, under the assumption that
l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=0
, we can
show that there exists a sequence
\{\gamma_{j}\}\subset\Gamma
such that
the pull-back of
u_{j}\colon p\mapsto d(p,f(\gamma_{j}))

approaches a
\mu
-harmonic function on
\Gamma
.
By our assumption that
Y
is either proper or of finite telescopic
dimension, after a suitable normalization, we obtain a limit function

u
of
\{u_{j}\}
which is either a distance function from a point in

Y
or a Busemann function associated to a point in
\partial Y

(see §
3
and
§
6.2
).
Then the pull-back
f^{*}u
of the limit function
u
is
\mu
-harmonic,
that is,
-\Delta f^{*}u(\gamma)=0
holds for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
and hence
u
has the weakest convexity on the convex hull

\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
of
f(\Gamma)
.
This suggests that the nonpositivity of the curvature of

\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
is the weakest one, namely,
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
must be
a flat subspace in
Y
.

Combining Theorem
A
with a theorem due to A. Karlsson
and G. Margulis mentioned above, we immediately obtain the following
theorem, which is a refinement of a part of a theorem due to U. Bader,
B. Duchesne, and J. Lécureux

[
1
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Theorem B
.

Let
Y
be a complete
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space which is either proper or
of finite telescopic dimension, and
\Gamma
a countable group
with a symmetric and nondegenerate probability measure
\mu
.
Let
\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
be a homomorphism and
suppose that
\rho(\Gamma)
does not fix a point in

\partial Y
and that the action given by
\rho
has finite
second moment with respect to
\mu
.
Then either of the following is true.

(1)
An orbit map induces a canonical
\rho
-equivariant map from the
Poisson boundary
\partial(\Gamma,\mu)
of
\Gamma
into the boundary

\partial Y
of
Y
.

(2)
There exists a flat subsapce of
Y
left invariant under the
action of
\rho(\Gamma)
.

For example, if
\Gamma
, equipped with the discrete topology,
has Kazhdan’s property
(T)
, then

\Gamma
is finitely generated and any
isometric action of
\Gamma
on a Hilbert space (a Euclidean space) has
a fixed point (see for example
[
3
]
).
Therefore we obtain the following.

Corollary
.

Let
\Gamma
be a discrete group with Kazhdan’s
property
(T)
, and
\mu
a symmetric and nondegenerate probability
measure
\mu
on
\Gamma
with finite second moment.
Let
Y
be a complete
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space which is either proper or
of finite telescopic dimension, and

\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
a homomorphism.
Suppose that
\rho(\Gamma)
does not fix a point in
Y\cup\partial Y
.
Then any orbit map induces a canonical
\rho
-equivariant map from the
Poisson boundary
\partial(\Gamma,\mu)
of
\Gamma
into the boundary

\partial Y
of
Y
.

Since a group
\Gamma
with Kazhdan’s property
(T)
is known to
have an “entropy gap”, the assertion of the corollary above says that
there is certain restrictions on isometric actions of
\Gamma
on

\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces as it has been suggested in many works from various
viewpoints.

If
Y
is locally finite-dimensional
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space, we can show a
slightly weaker result.
We assume that
\rho(\Gamma)
is
reductive in the sense of Jost
(see §
2
for the definition), which is stronger
than the assumption that there exists a point in
\partial Y
fixed by

\rho(\Gamma)
; we need this assumption to deduce the existence of a

\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map from
\Gamma
into
Y
.

Theorem C
.

Let
Y
be a complete, locally finite-dimensional
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space,
and
\Gamma
a countable group equipped with a symmetric and
nondegenerate probablity measure
\mu
.
Let
\Gamma
act on
Y
via a homomorphism

\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
, and
suppose that
\rho(\Gamma)
is
reductive in the sense of Jost and that the action given by

\rho
has finite second moment with respect to
\mu
.
If
l_{\rho}(\Gamma,\mu)=0
, then there exists a flat subspace
of
Y
left invariant under the action of
\rho(\Gamma)
.

It is clear that an analogue of Theorem
B
holds for a
locally finite-dimensional
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
.

Theorem D
.

Let
Y
be a complete, locally finite-dimensional
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space,
and
\Gamma
a countable group
with a symmetric and nondegenerate probability measure
\mu
.
Let
\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
be a homomorphism and
suppose that
\rho(\Gamma)
is
reductive in the sense of Jost and that the action given by

\rho
has finite second moment with respect to
\mu
.
Then either of the following is true.

(1)
An orbit map induces a canonical
\rho
-equivariant map from the
Poisson boundary
\partial(\Gamma,\mu)
of
\Gamma
into the boundary

\partial Y
of
Y
.

(2)
There exists a flat subsapce of
Y
left invariant under the
action of
\rho(\Gamma)
.

We also point out that, as a byproduct of our result, we obtain
a certain gap theorem on the energy growth of equivariant harmonic
maps.
For a
\rho
-equivariant map
f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
,

n
-step energy
E_{\mu^{n}}(f)
is defined by

E_{\mu^{n}}(f)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}d_{Y}(f(e),f(\gamma))^{2}d\mu^{n}(\gamma).

It is shown in
[
20
]
that,
for any
\rho
-equivariant map into a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
,

E_{\mu^{n}}(f)\leq Cn^{2}E_{\mu}(f)
holds for some
C>0
, and
that if
f
is a
\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map into a Hilbert
space (a Euclidean space), then

E_{\mu^{n}}(f)=nE_{\mu}(f)

holds.
On the other, if
l_{\rho}(\Gamma)>0
, then the Schwarz inequality
tells us that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{E_{\mu^{n}}(f)}{n^{2}}>0.

Therefore we obtain:

Theorem E
.

Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space which is either proper, of
finite telescopic dimension, or of locally finite dimension.
And let
\Gamma
be a countable group with a symmetric and
nondegenerate probability measure
\mu
.
Suppose that the action given by a homomorphism

\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
has finite second moment with
respect to
\mu
and that
there exists a nonconstant
\rho
-equivariant

\mu
-harmonic map
f
.
Then either
E_{\mu^{n}}(f)=nE_{\mu}(f)
or

E_{\mu^{n}}(f)\asymp n^{2}
holds.

We note here that it was pointed out and proved in
[
16
]
and

[
21
]
that if there exist
\varepsilon>0
and a
natural number
n
such that every
\rho
-equivariant map
f
satisfies

E_{\mu^{n}}(f)\leq(n-\varepsilon)E_{\mu}(f)
, then there exists a
constant
\rho
-equivariant harmonic map, and hence
\rho(\Gamma)
fixes
a point in
Y
, which is used in
[
21
]
and

[
19
]
to prove a fixed-point property for random groups.

This paper is organized as follows.
In §
2
, we recall fundamental definitions and
facts on
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces, and some facts on equivariant harmonic maps.
We first finish the proof of Theorem
A
for proper

\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces in §
3
,
§
4
, and
§
5
.
In §
3
, under the assumption

l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=0
together with the existence of an equivariant

\mu
-harmonic map
f
, we show that the pull-back of a distance
function or a Busemannn function by
f
becomes a (locally)

\mu
-harmonic function; they are obtained as a limit of distance
functions from a sequence of points.
The properness of a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space guarantees that the limit
function can be regarded as either a distance function or a Busemann
function.
Then, in §
4
, we prove two
inequalities reflecting the convexity of metric balls and horoballs
respectively; the equality case plays a key role in the recognition of a
flat subspace in Theorem
A
.
In §
5
, using these results, we prove
Theorem
A
for proper
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces.

The proofs of Theorem
A
for nonproper
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces
and that of Theorem
C
are given in
§
6
and §
7
.
In order to apply our arguments to nonproper
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces, we need a
sort of compactness theorem (Theorem
6.12
) for

\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces with certain dimension bounds.
In §
6
, we present a quick review of the notion of
ultralimit and consider ultralimits of
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces with certain
dimension bounds.
Assuming the compactness theorem mentioned above,
we finish the proof of Theorem
A
.
The proof of Theorem
C
is a straightforward variant of
that of Theorem
A

(see §
6.5
).
Then §
7
is devoted to the proof of
Theorem
6.12
.
In the course of the proof of Theorem
A
, we need to
replace a probability measure
\mu
on
\Gamma
with one whose support
covers all non-trivial elements of
\Gamma
.
It is achieved by taking a convex combination of the
n
-fold
convolution
\mu^{n}
’s of
\mu
.
In §
8
, we show that this replacement does not affect
our assumption
l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=0
in Theorem
A
.

The author would like to thank Uri Bader, Atsushi Katsuda, Shin
Nayatani, Takefumi Kondo, Ryokichi Tanaka, Masato Mimura, and Tomohiro
Fukaya for their comments and interests in this work.

2.
Equivariant harmonic maps into
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces

In this section, we give some fundamental definitions and
facts on
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces and equivariant harmonic maps from
countable groups into
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces.

Let
Y
be a metric space with metric
d_{Y}
, and
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
an
interval.
A map
c\colon I\rightarrow Y
is called a
geodesic
if
c
is
an isometric embedding, namely,
c
satisfies
d_{Y}(c(t),c(t^{\prime}))=|t-t^{\prime}|

for any
t,t^{\prime}\in I
.
If
I
is a bounded closed interval, we often call
c
a

geodesic segment
, while if
I=[0,\infty)
, we call
c
a

geodesic ray
.
We call a geodesic defined on whole
{\mathbb{R}}
a
geodesic line
.
If every pair of points in
Y
is joined by a geodesic segment, then
Y

is called a
geodesic space
.
In what follows, all metric spaces under consideration are assumed to be
complete as metric spaces.

A geodesic space
Y
is called
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space if any geodesic triangle
is thinner than that in the Euclidean plane in the following sense:
Let
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in Y
, and denote the image of geodesic joining
p_{i}

and
p_{j}
by
[p_{i},p_{j}]
.
We call
[p_{1},p_{2}]\cup[p_{2},p_{3}]\cup[p_{3},p_{1}]
a
geodesic
triangle
, and denote by
\triangle(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
.
Note that we can take
a triangle
\triangle(\overline{p_{1}},\overline{p_{2}},\overline{p_{3}})
in

{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
so that
d_{Y}(p_{i},p_{j})=d_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}(\overline{p_{i}},\overline{p_{j}})
,
since the side lengths of
\triangle(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
satisfy the triangle
inequality.
We call
\triangle(\overline{p_{1}},\overline{p_{2}},\overline{p_{3}})
a

comparison triangle
for
\triangle(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
.
And we call a point

\overline{q}\in[\overline{p_{i}},\overline{p_{j}}]

a
comparison point
for
q\in[p_{i},p_{j}]
if

d_{Y}(p_{i},q)=d_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}(\overline{p_{i}},\overline{q})
.
Then we say that
\triangle(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
satisfies the

\mathrm{CAT}(0)
inequality

if
d_{Y}(q_{1},q_{2})\leq d_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}(\overline{q_{1}},\overline{q_{2}})
holds
for any pair of points
q_{1},q_{2}
on the sides of
\triangle(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})

and their comparison points
\overline{q_{1}},\overline{q_{2}}
.
If every geodesic triangle in
Y
satisfies the
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
inequality,
then
Y
is called a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
.
A complete, simply
connected Riemannian manifold of nonpositive sectional curvature is a
typical example of
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space. Also trees and Hilbert spaces are

\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces.
If one replaces the Euclidean plane, the present location of the
comparison triangle, with the
2
-dimensional space form of curvature

\kappa
, we obtain the notion of
\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)
space, which can be
regarded as a metric space whose curvature is bounded from above by

\kappa
.
For a detailed exposition on
\mathrm{CAT}(\kappa)
spaces, we refer the reader
to
[
5
]
.

If
Y
is a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space, then it is clear that any pair of distinct
points is joined by a unique geodesic, and in particular, a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)

space turns out to be contractible.
Furthermore, it is not hard to see that the distance function on a

\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space becomes a convex function, an important feature of a

\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space:
For any geodesic
c_{i}\colon[0,1]\rightarrow Y
,
i=1,2
, parametrized
proportional to arc length (possibly a constant map), a function

t\mapsto d(c_{1}(t),c_{2}(t))
is a convex function, that is, for any

t\in[0,1]
,

d_{Y}(c_{1}(t),c_{2}((t))\leq(1-t)d_{Y}(c_{1}(0),c_{2}(0))+td_{Y}(c_{1}(1),c_{2}(1))

holds (see
[
5
, p. 176, 2.2 Proposition]
).
For a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
the following is a part of the list of its
properties more or less directly derived from the convexity
above and the definition of
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces.
We will frequently use them in what follows.

•

Recall that
C\subset Y
is called a
convex
subset if any
pair of points in
C
can be joined by a geodesic lying in
C
.
For a closed convex subset
C
of
Y
and
p\in Y
, there exists
a unique point
q\in C
such that
d_{Y}(p,C)=d_{Y}(p,q)
holds.
Thus we can define the
nearest point projection

\mathrm{proj}_{C}

that sends
p\in Y
to this
q\in C
;
\mathrm{proj}_{C}
turns out to
be a distance nonincreasing map.
(See (
[
5
, p. 176, 2.4 Proposition]
) for the
detail.)

•

Let
C
be a closed convex subset of
Y
.
If
\mathrm{proj}_{C}
restricted to
C^{\prime}
becomes an isometry onto its
image, then the convex hull of
\mathrm{proj}_{C}(C^{\prime})\cup C^{\prime}
is isometric
to a product
C^{\prime}\times[0,l]
.
(See
[
5
, p. 182, 2.12 Exercise (3)]
.)

•

If two geodesic lines
c_{i}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow Y
,
i=1,2
,
are parallel to each other, that is,
c_{1}({\mathbb{R}})
stays in a bounded
distance from
c_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
and vice versa, then the convex hull of

c_{1}({\mathbb{R}})\cup c_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
is isometric to
{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,l]
.
(See
[
5
, p. 182, 2.13 The Flat Strip Theorem]
.)

•

Let
c\colon{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow Y
be a geodesic line.
The union of the images of geodesic lines parallel to
c
forms a
closed convex subset
Y_{c}
of
Y
, and
Y_{c}
is isometric to a
product metric space
{\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{0}
for some convex subset

Y_{0}\subset Y_{c}
.
(See
[
5
, p. 183, 2.14 A Product Decomposition
Theorem]
. )

From the viewpoint of the convexity of the distance function,
the Euclidean space
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, whose nonpositivity of the curvature is the
least among the
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces, should be characterized as a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)

space with the “weakest” convexity.
In order to prove the existence of a flat subspace in
Theorem
A
, we need to develope a quantitative
approach to the recognition of the subset with the weakest convexity.
It is plausible that, for example, the existence of solutions to a sort
of “equation” leads us to the goal;
indeed, it is achieved by considering harmonic maps and harmonic
functions as we will see.
In order to realize this, a tool like Riemannian metric provides us with
a great help; it enables us to produce various quantitative expressions
of the convexity (see, for example,
Propositions
4.1
and

4.4
, and remarks following them).
An “inner product” defined below on the tangent cone
plays such a role in this paper.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space.

(1)
Let
c
and
c^{\prime}
be two non-trivial geodesics in
Y
starting
from
p\in Y
. The
angle

\angle_{p}(c,c^{\prime})
between
c
and
c^{\prime}

is defined by

\angle_{p}(c,c^{\prime})=\lim_{t,t^{\prime}\rightarrow 0}\angle_{\overline{p}}(\overline{c(t)},\overline{c^{\prime}(t^{\prime})}),

where

\angle_{\overline{p}}(\overline{c(t)},\overline{c^{\prime}(t^{\prime})})

denotes the angle between the sides

[\overline{p},\overline{c(t)}]
and
[\overline{p},\overline{c^{\prime}(t^{\prime})}]

of the comparison triangle

\triangle(\overline{p},\overline{c(t)},\overline{c^{\prime}(t^{\prime})})\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
For
p,q\in Y
, denote by
c_{p}^{q}
a geodesic starting from
p
and
teminating at
q
.
We often use an expression
\angle_{p}(q,q^{\prime})
for the angle

\angle_{p}(c_{p}^{q},c_{p}^{q^{\prime}})
between
c_{p}^{q}
and
c_{p}^{q^{\prime}}
.

(2)
Let
p\in Y
.
For a pair of non-trivial geodesics
c
,
c^{\prime}
starting from
p
, we
define a relation
\sim
by
c\sim c^{\prime}
if and only if

\angle_{p}(c,c^{\prime})=0
.
Then
\sim
becomes an equivalence relation on the set of
all non-trivial geodesics starting from
p
denoted by

\widetilde{(S_{p}Y)^{\circ}}
.
Then the angle
\angle_{p}
induces a metric on the quotient

(S_{p}Y)^{\circ}=\widetilde{(S_{p}Y)^{\circ}}/\sim
,
which we denote by the same symbol
\angle_{p}
. The completion

(S_{p}Y,\angle_{p})
of the metric space
((S_{p}Y)^{\circ},\angle_{p})
is
called the
space of directions
at
p
.

(3)
Let
TC_{p}Y
be the cone over
S_{p}Y
, namely,

TC_{p}Y=(S_{p}Y\times{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})/(S_{p}Y\times\{0\}).

Let
W,W^{\prime}\in TC_{p}Y
. We may write
W=(V,t)
and
W^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},t^{\prime})
, where

V,V^{\prime}\in S_{p}Y
and
t,t^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
. Then

d_{TC_{p}Y}(W,W^{\prime})=t^{2}+{t^{\prime}}^{2}-2tt^{\prime}\cos\angle_{p}(V,V^{\prime})

defines a metric on
TC_{p}Y
. The metric space
(TC_{p}Y,d_{TC_{p}Y})
is
known to be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space and is called the
tangent cone
at

p
.
We define an “inner product” on
TC_{p}Y
by

\left\langle W,\,W^{\prime}\right\rangle=tt^{\prime}\cos\angle_{p}(V,V^{\prime}).

We often denote the length
t
of
W
by
|W|
; thus we have

|W|=\sqrt{\langle W,W\rangle}=d_{TC_{p}Y}(O_{p},W)
, where
O_{p}
denotes
the origin of
TC_{p}Y
.
In what follows, we identify
S_{p}Y
with the set of elements
with the length equals one in
TC_{p}Y
:

S_{p}Y=\{W\in TC_{p}Y\mid|W|=1\}
.

(4)
Define a map
\log_{p}\colon Y\longrightarrow TC_{p}Y
by

\log_{p}(q)=([c],d_{Y}(p,q))
, where
c
is a geodesic starting from

p
and terminating at
q
, and
[c]\in S_{p}Y
is the equivalence class
of
c
. Then
\log_{p}
is distance nonincreasing.

Remark 2.2
.

For a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
, the space of direction
S_{p}Y
is known to be
a
\mathrm{CAT}(1)
space (
[
5
, p. 191, 3.19 Theorem]
).
Note that, by definition, any isometry
\Phi\colon Y\rightarrow Y

induces an isometry
\Phi_{*}\colon S_{p}Y\rightarrow S_{\Phi(p)}Y
and

\Phi_{*}\colon TC_{p}Y\rightarrow TC_{\Phi(p)}Y
.

While the tangent cone describes a local structure of a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)

space
Y
, the
boundary at infinity
defined below tells us an
asymptotic nature of
Y
; for example,
the boundary
\partial Y
of
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y

equipped with the angular metric (see below for the definition)
describes the distribution of the flat subspaces in
Y
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space.

(1)
Two geodesic rays
c_{i}\colon[0,\infty)\rightarrow Y
,

i=1,2
, are
asymptotic
to each other if there exists

M\in[0,\infty)
such that
d(c_{1}(t),c_{2}(t))\leq M
for all

t\in[0,\infty)
.
Being asymptotic is easily seen to be an equivalence relation on the set
of geodesic rays. The
boundary

\partial Y
of
Y
is defined to
be the set of equivalence classes (asymptotic classes) of geodesic
rays.
The topology on
Y\cup\partial Y
naturally induced from
the uniform convergence of geodesics and geodesic rays on each bounded
interval is called the
cone topology
of
Y\cup\partial Y
.

(2)
Let
\xi_{i}\in\partial Y
. Denote by
c_{p}^{\xi_{i}}
a geodesic
ray starting from
p
and representing
\xi_{i}
(we will say
“terminating at
\xi_{i}
” and express as

c_{p}^{\xi_{i}}(\infty)=\xi_{i}
).
Let us denote the angle between

c_{p}^{\xi_{1}}
and
c_{p}^{\xi_{2}}
by
\angle_{p}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
.
Then

d_{\angle}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\sup_{p\in Y}\angle_{p}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})

gives a metric on
\partial Y
called the
angular metric
.

(3)
Let
c\colon[0,\infty)\rightarrow Y
be a geodesic ray.
The
Busemann function

b_{c}
associated to
c
is defined by

b_{c}(x)=\lim_{t\to\infty}d(c(t),x)-t

The level set
S_{c}(r)=\{p\in Y\mid b_{c}(p)=r\}
is called a

horosphere
centered at
\xi=c(\infty)
(see
Remark
2.8
below).
The sublevel set
B_{c}(r)=\{p\in Y\mid b_{c}(p)\leq r\}
is
called a
horoball
centered at
\xi=c(\infty)
.

Remark 2.4
.

Since any isometry preserves the relation asymptotic, the isometry
group
\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
of
Y
acts on
\partial Y
.
The action is by homeomorphisms with respect to the cone topology.
On the other hand, it is clear, by definition, the action is by
isometry with respect to the angular metric on
\partial Y
.

Remark 2.5
.

A metric space
Y=(Y,d_{Y})
is called
proper
if every
closed metric ball is compact.
If a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
is proper, then one sees that, by using
Arzelà-Ascoli theorem,

Y\cup\partial Y
equipped with the cone topology is compact.

Remark 2.6
.

For a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
, the boundary
(\partial Y,d_{\angle})
with
the angular metric is a
\mathrm{CAT}(1)
spaces
(see
[
5
, p. 285, 9.13 Theorem]
).
The topology on
\partial Y
given by the angular metric is stronger
than the cone topology restricted to
\partial Y
in general.
For example, for a hyperbolic
n
-space
\mathbb{H}^{n}
,

\partial\mathbb{H}^{n}
with the cone topology is homeomorphic to

S^{n-1}
, while
(\partial\mathbb{H}^{n},d_{\angle})
is easily seen
to be a discrete metric space, since every pair of points in

\partial\mathbb{H}^{n}
can be joined by a geodesic line.

Remark 2.7
.

Let
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\partial Y
with
d_{\angle}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})<\pi
.
It is known that if there exists
p_{0}
such that

\angle_{p_{0}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})=d_{\angle}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})
, then the convex hull of

c_{p_{0}}^{\xi}([0,\infty))\cup c_{p_{0}}^{\xi^{\prime}}([0,\infty))
is
isometric to a flat sector, which is a sector in the Euclidean plane
bounded by a pair of half lines that meet at their endpoints with an
angle
\angle(\xi,\xi^{\prime})

(
[
5
, p. 283, 9.9 Corollary]
).
On the other hand,
by
[
5
, p. 281, 9.8 Proposition (2)]
, one sees that
if the convex hull of
[p,\xi)\cup[p,\xi^{\prime})
is isometric to a
flat sector, then
d_{\angle}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})=\angle_{p}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})
holds.

Remark 2.8
.

Let
\xi\in\partial Y
and
\{p_{n}\}\subset Y
any sequence that
converges to
\xi
in the cone topology. Fix
p_{0}\in Y
.
Consider a function
p\mapsto b_{\xi}(p,p_{0})
defined by

(2.1)

b_{\xi}(p,p_{0})=\lim_{n}d(p_{n},p)-d(p_{n},p_{0}).

The limit in the right-hand side exists and does not depend on the
choice of a sequence
\{p_{n}\}
converging to
\xi

(see
[
5
, p.269, 8.19 Corollary]
, see also
Lemma
6.6
).
Therefore we see that
b_{\xi}(p)=b_{c}(p)
for a geodesic ray
c
with

c(0)=p_{0}
and
c(\infty)=\xi
;
we treat (
2.1
) as the definition of the Busemann
function, and mainly use the symbol
b_{\xi}(\cdot,p_{0})
in what
follows.
Note that, by definition and the description above,

\lim_{t\to\infty}b_{\xi}(c(t),p_{0})\to-\infty
for any geodesic ray
terminating at
\xi
.
Note also that the definition (
2.1
) above shows that
the Busemann function has an additivity:

(2.2)

b_{\xi}(p,p_{0})=b_{\xi}(p,p_{0}^{\prime})+b_{\xi}(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{0})

for any
p,p_{0},p_{0}^{\prime}\in Y
.
In particular, the change of the basepoint
p_{0}
leaves the Busemann
function invariant up to a constant.
Thus we often denote the horosphere and the horoball centered at
\xi

by
H_{\xi}(r)
and
B_{\xi}(r)
without specifying the basepoint.
Since a Busemann function
b_{\xi}(\cdot,p_{0})
is the limit of a
sequence of convex functions,
b_{\xi}(\cdot,p_{0})
is also a convex
function.
As a consequnece, a horoball
B_{\xi}(r)
, as well as a metric ball,
becomes a convex subset of
Y
.

Let
\Gamma
be a countable group, and
\mu
a probability
measure on
\Gamma
.
We assume that the support of
\mu
generates
\Gamma
,
and that
\mu
is symmetric, that is,

\mu
satisfies
\mu(\gamma)=\mu(\gamma^{-1})
for any

\gamma\in\Gamma
.
Suppose that
\Gamma
acts on a metric space
Y
isometrically via a
homomorphism
\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
, where
\mathrm{Isom}({Y})

is the isometry group of
Y
.
We assume that the action given by
\rho
has

finite second moment
with respect to
\mu
, namely, for
p\in Y
,

\int_{\Gamma}d_{Y}(p,\rho(\gamma)p)^{2}d\mu(\gamma)<\infty

holds.
Since the action given by
\rho
is by isometry, using the triangle
inequality, we see that

\begin{split}d_{Y}(p^{\prime},\rho(\gamma)p^{\prime})^{2}&\leq\left(d_{Y}(p^{\prime},p)+d_{Y}(p,\rho(\gamma)p)+d_{Y}(\rho(\gamma)p,\rho(\gamma)p^{\prime})\right)^{2}\\
&=\left(2d_{Y}(p^{\prime},p)+d_{Y}(p,\rho(\gamma)p)\right)^{2}\\
&=4d_{Y}(p^{\prime},p)^{2}+4d_{Y}(p^{\prime},p)d_{Y}(p,\rho(\gamma)p)+d_{Y}(p,\rho(\gamma)p)^{2}.\end{split}

Applying the Schwarz inequality to the second term in the last
expression, we see that it is also integrable with respect to
\mu
; the
integrability does not depend on the choice of
p\in Y
.
A map
f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
is said to be

\rho
-
equivariant
if
f(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})=\rho(\gamma)f(\gamma^{\prime})

holds for any
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
.
The
\mu
-energy
E_{\mu}(f)
of a
\rho
-equivariant map
f
defined
as

E_{\mu}(f)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}d_{Y}(f(e),f(\gamma))^{2}d\mu(\gamma)

is well-defined by the assumption that the action given by
\rho
has
finite second moment with respect to
\mu
.

Now suppose that
\Gamma
is finitely generated.
Then we have a finite generating set
S
of
\Gamma
.
We assume that
S
is symmetric;
s\in S
implies that
s^{-1}\in S
.
The
word length

\mathrm{length}_{S}(\gamma)
of
\gamma\in\Gamma

is defined as the length of the shortest expression of
\gamma
by
generators belonging to
S
:

\mathrm{length}_{S}(\gamma)=\min\{k\in{\mathbb{N}}\mid\gamma=s_{1}\dots s_{k},\ s_{i}\in S\}.

Then
d_{S}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=\mathrm{length}_{S}(\gamma^{-1}\gamma^{\prime})

becomes a metric on
\Gamma
, and called the
word metric
on

\Gamma
with respect to a finite generating set
S
.
Note that, by definition,
d_{S}(\cdot,\cdot)
is a left-invariant metric:

d_{S}(\gamma\gamma^{\prime},\gamma\gamma^{\prime\prime})=d_{S}(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime})
.
For finite generating sets
S
and
S^{\prime}
, although
d_{S}
and
d_{S^{\prime}}
may
be different, it is easy to see that they are Lipschitz equivalent.
We say that
\mu
has finite second moment, if
\mu
satisfies

\int_{\Gamma}d_{S}(e,\gamma)^{2}d\mu(\gamma)<\infty,

where
e\in\Gamma
denotes the identity element in
\Gamma
, and

d_{S}
is a word metric on
\Gamma
defined above.
Note that the property of having finite second monent does not depend on
the choice of a finite generating set
S
(
S
may not be contained in

\mathrm{supp}\,\mu
).
Taking
u(\cdot)
to be
d(f(e),\cdot)
in the following lemma,
we see that if
\Gamma
is finitely generated and

\mu
has finite second moment, then any isometric action of
\Gamma
on
a metric space has finite second moment.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitely generated group with a probability measure

\mu
with finite second moment. Suppose
\Gamma
acts on a metric
space
Y
via a homomorphism
\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
.
Suppose further that a function
u\colon Y\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
satisfies

|u(p)|\leq C_{1}d(p_{0},p)^{2}+C_{2}
for some
C_{1},C_{2}>0
and

p_{0}\in Y
. Then, for any
\rho
-equivariant map

f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
, the pull-back
f^{*}u
of
u
by
f

belongs to
L^{1}(\Gamma,\mu)
.
In particular,
f^{*}u
is a
\mu
-integrable function on
\Gamma
.

Proof.

Fix a finite generating set
S
of
\Gamma
.
Then, by setting
C=\max\{d_{Y}(f(e),f(s))\mid s\in S\}
,
for
\gamma=s_{1}\dots s_{k}
with
s_{i}\in S

(
i=1,\dots,k
), we have

\begin{split}&d_{Y}(f(e),f(s_{1}\dots s_{k}))\\
\leq&d_{Y}(f(e),f(s_{1}))+d_{Y}(f(s_{1}),f(s_{1}s_{2}))+\dots+d_{Y}(f(s_{1}\dots s_{k-1}),f(s_{1}\dots s_{k}))\\
=&d_{Y}(f(e),f(s_{1}))+d_{Y}(f(e),f(s_{2}))+\dots+d_{Y}(f(e),f(s_{k}))\leq Ck,\end{split}

because the action given by
\rho
is isometric and
f
is

\rho
-equivariant.
This implies that
d_{Y}(f(e),f(\gamma))\leq Cd_{S}(e,\gamma)
.
Therefore
f^{*}u
satisfies

\begin{split}&|f^{*}u(\gamma)|=|u(f(\gamma))|\\
\leq&C_{1}\left(d(p_{0},f(e))+d(f(e),f(\gamma))\right)^{2}+C_{2}\\
=&C_{1}\left(d(f(e),f(\gamma))^{2}+2d(p_{0},f(e))d(f(e),f(\gamma))+d(p_{0},f(e))^{2}\right)+C_{2}\\
\leq&C_{1}^{\prime}d_{S}(e,\gamma)^{2}+C_{1}^{\prime\prime}d_{S}(e,\gamma)+C_{2}^{\prime\prime}\end{split}

for some
C_{1}^{\prime},C_{1}^{\prime\prime},C_{2}^{\prime\prime}>0
.
Thus, by applying the Schwarz inequality to the second term in
the last expression and by our assumption of
\mu
having
finite second moment, we see that

\int_{\Gamma}|f^{*}u(\gamma)|d\mu(\gamma)<\infty.

This completes the proof.
∎

A
\rho
-equivariant map
f
is called a
\mu
-
harmonic map
if
f

minimizes the
\mu
-energy
E_{\mu}(f)
:

E_{\mu}(g)\geq E_{\mu}(f)
holds for any
\rho
-equivariant map

g\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
.

Note that a
\rho
-equivariant map is completely determined by the image
of
e
; the set of
\rho
-equivariant map can be identified with
Y
.
We denote by
f_{p}
a
\rho
-equivariant map defined by

f_{p}(\gamma)=\rho(\gamma)p
.
Recall that a function
u\colon Y\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is said to be

convex
if, for any geodesic
c\colon[0,l]\rightarrow Y
,

t\mapsto u(c(t))
is a convex function on
[0,l]
.
Take any geodesic
c\colon[0,1]\rightarrow Y
parametrized
proportional to arc length.
Since the distance funtion of a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
is nonnegative and
convex, its square
t\mapsto d_{Y}(c(t),\rho(\gamma)c(t))^{2}
is also
convex, and hence

\begin{split}E_{\mu}(f_{c(t)})&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}d_{Y}(c(t),\rho(\gamma)c(t))^{2}d\mu(\gamma)\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}(1-t)d_{Y}(c(0),\rho(\gamma)c(0))^{2}+td_{Y}(c(1),\rho(\gamma)c(1))^{2}d\mu(\gamma)\\
&=(1-t)E_{\mu}(f_{c(0)})+tE_{\mu}(f_{c(1)}),\end{split}

which means that
p\mapsto E_{\mu}(f_{p})
is a convex function on

Y
. Then we have the following.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space and

\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
a homomorphism.
Let
f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
be a
\rho
-equivariant map.
Then
f
is
\mu
-harmonic if and only if
f
satisfies the following
inequality
\,:
For any
V\in S_{f(e)}Y
,

(2.3)

\int_{\Gamma}\left\langle V,\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle d\mu(\gamma)\leq 0.

We use the following lemmas in the proof of
Proposition
2.10
.

Lemma 2.11
.

(
[
5
, p. 185, 3.6 Corollary]
)

Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space and
p\in Y
. Take two non-trivial
geodesics
c
,
c^{\prime}
starting
from
p
, and fix a point
q
on
c^{\prime}
. Then we have

\cos\angle_{p}(c,c^{\prime})=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{d_{Y}(p,q)-d_{Y}(c(t),q)}{t}.

Lemma 2.12
.

(
[
22
, Lemma 1.4]
)

Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space and
p,q\in Y
. Let
c(t)

(
resp.
c^{\prime}(t)

)
be a non-trivial geodesic starting from
p

(
resp.
q

)
. Then we have

\cos\angle_{p}(c,c_{p}^{q})=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{d_{Y}(p,c^{\prime}(t))-d_{Y}(c(t),c^{\prime}(t))}{t},

where
c_{p}^{q}
denotes the geodesic starting from
p
and
terminating at
q
.

Proof of Proposition
2.10
.
Although the proof goes along the same line as that of

[
22
, Proposition 2.5]
, where the weights
corresoponding to our
\mu
has finite support, we give a proof here
for the sake of completeness.

Suppose that
f
is
\mu
-harmonic, that is,
f
minimizes
E_{\mu}
.
Let
c\colon[0,\varepsilon]\rightarrow Y
be a geodesic representing

V\in S_{f(e)}Y
, and
f_{t}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
a

\rho
-equivarinat map defined by

f_{t}(\gamma)=\rho(\gamma)c(t)
.
By Lemmas
2.11
and
2.12
above, we see that

\begin{split}&\lim_{t\to+0}\frac{1}{t}\left(d_{Y}(f_{t}(e),f_{t}(\gamma))-d_{Y}(f(e),f(\gamma))\right)\\
=&\lim_{t\to+0}\frac{1}{t}\left[\big(d_{Y}(c(t),\rho(\gamma)c(t))-d_{Y}(c(0),\rho(\gamma)c(t))\big)\right.\\
&\phantom{++}+\left.\big(d_{Y}(c(0),\rho(\gamma)c(t))-d_{Y}(c(0),\rho(\gamma)c(0))\big)\right]\\
=&-\cos\angle_{f(e)}(V,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma)))-\cos\angle_{f(\gamma)}(\log_{f(\gamma)}(f(e)),\rho(\gamma)V)\end{split}

holds. Noting that

\angle_{f(\gamma)}(\log_{f(\gamma)}(f(e)),\rho(\gamma)V)=\angle_{f(e)}(\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma^{-1})),V)
,
we get

\begin{split}&\lim_{t\to+0}\frac{1}{t}\left(d_{Y}(f_{t}(e),f_{t}(\gamma))^{2}-d_{Y}(f(e),f(\gamma))^{2}\right)\\
=&-2d_{Y}(f(e),f(\gamma))\left(\cos\angle_{f(e)}(V,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma)))+\cos\angle_{f(e)}(\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma^{-1})),V)\right)\\
=&-2\left(\left\langle V,\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle+\left\langle V,\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma^{-1})\right\rangle\right).\end{split}

Since

\begin{split}|d_{Y}(f_{t}(e),f_{t}(\gamma))-d_{Y}(f(e),f(\gamma))|&\leq d_{Y}(f(e),f_{t}(e))+d_{Y}(f(\gamma),f_{t}(\gamma))\\
&=2d_{Y}(f(e),f_{t}(e))=2t,\end{split}

and
\gamma\mapsto d_{Y}(f(e),f(\gamma))
is
\mu
-integrable by the
Schwarz inequality, we see that

\begin{split}&\int_{\Gamma}\frac{|d_{Y}(f_{t}(e),f_{t}(\gamma))^{2}-d_{Y}(f(e),f(\gamma))^{2}|}{t}d\mu(\gamma)\\
\leq&\int_{\Gamma}\frac{1}{t}2d_{Y}(f(e),f_{t}(e))\left(2d_{Y}(f(e),f(\gamma))+d_{Y}(f(e),f_{t}(e))+d_{Y}(f(\gamma),f_{t}(\gamma))\right)d\mu(\gamma)\\
=&4\int_{\Gamma}\left(d_{Y}(f(e),f(\gamma))+t\right)d\mu(\gamma)<\infty.\end{split}

Since
f
is
\mu
-harmonic, by Lebesgue’s dominated convergence
theorem and
\mu(\gamma)=\mu(\gamma^{-1})
, we obtain

(2.4)

\begin{split}0&\leq\lim_{t\to+0}\frac{E_{\mu}(f_{t})-E_{\mu}(f)}{t}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}\lim_{t\to+0}\frac{d_{Y}(f_{t}(e),f_{t}(\gamma))^{2}-d_{Y}(f(e),f(\gamma))^{2}}{t}d\mu(\gamma)\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}-2\left\langle V,\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle d\mu(\gamma).\end{split}

Now suppose
f
satisfies (
2.3
).
Suppose further that there exists a
\rho
-equivariant map
g
with

E_{\mu}(g)<E_{\mu}(f)
.
Let
d=d_{Y}(f(e),g(e))
and take a geodesic

c\colon[0,d]\rightarrow Y
joining
f(e)
to
g(e)
.
Then, since
t\mapsto E_{\mu}(f_{c(t)})
is a convex function,
together with the above computation, we see that

\begin{split}\int_{\Gamma}-2\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle d\mu(\gamma)&=\lim_{t\to+0}\frac{E_{\mu}(f_{c(dt)})-E_{\mu}(f)}{dt}\\
&\leq\lim_{t\to+0}\frac{\left((1-t)E_{\mu}(f)+tE_{\mu}(g)\right)-E_{\mu}(f)}{dt}\\
&=\frac{E_{\mu}(g)-E_{\mu}(f)}{d}<0,\end{split}

where
V_{c}\in S_{f(e)}Y
is the equivalence class of
c
.
A contradiction.
This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.13
.

If
Y
is isometric to a Riemannian manifold on a neighborhood of

f(e)
, then the tangent cone at
f(e)
is isometric to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
and
f_{t}
can be extended uniquely and smoothly to
t<0
with small

|t|
.
Then one sees that if
f
is
\mu
-harmonic,

\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{E_{\mu}(f_{t})-E_{\mu}(f)}{t}=0

must holds, and we have an equality in (
2.4
).
However we cannot replace the inequality with equality if there is a
singularity in
TC_{f(e)}Y
.

Proposition
2.15
below asserts that a

\mu
-harmonic map
f
is characterized as a map that locates
f(e)
at
the
barycenter
of
f_{*}\mu
defined as follows, where
f_{*}\mu
is
the push-forward of a measure
\mu
by
f
.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space and
\nu
a probability measure on
Y

with finite second moment, that is,

\int_{Y}d_{Y}(p_{0},p)^{2}d\nu(p)<\infty

for some (hence any)
p_{0}\in Y
. The
barycenter

\mathrm{bar}(\mu)
of

\nu
is defined to be a unique point that minimizes

q\mapsto\int_{Y}d_{Y}(q,p)^{2}d\nu(p).

The existence of the barycenter is a consequence of the convexity of a

\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
(see, for example,
[
30
, Lemma 2.5.1]
for the
proof).
Since
t\mapsto d_{Y}(c(t),p)
is a convex function (regard
p
as the
image of a geodesic with zero velocity), we see that

q\mapsto\int_{Y}d_{Y}(p,q)^{2}d\nu(p)
is a convex function as in the
case of
p\mapsto E_{\mu}(f_{p})
.
A slight simplification of the proof of
Proposition
2.10
leads us to the following
proposition; since the support of
\nu
is fixed, in contrast to

f_{t}(\Gamma)
in Proposition
2.10
, we only need
Lemma
2.11
.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space and
\nu
a probability measrure with
finite second moment. Then
q
is the barycenter of
\nu

if and only if
q
satisfies

\int_{Y}\left\langle V,\,\log_{q}(p)\right\rangle d\nu(p)\leq 0

for any
V\in S_{q}Y
.

Remark 2.16
.

Let
f
be a
\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map.
Then, since
f
is
\rho
-equivariant, we see that
f(\gamma)

is also the barycenter of
(f\circ\gamma)_{*}\mu
.

Let
\varphi\colon\Gamma\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
.
Suppose that

\int_{\Gamma}\varphi(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})d\mu(\gamma^{\prime})<\infty

holds for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
The
\mu
-Laplacian

\Delta=\Delta_{\mu}
with respect to

\mu
is an operator defined by

\Delta\varphi(\gamma)=\varphi(\gamma)-\int_{\Gamma}\varphi(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})d\mu(\gamma^{\prime}).

A function
\varphi\colon\Gamma\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is called a

\mu
-
subharmonic function
if
\Delta\varphi(\gamma)\leq 0
holds
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
If
\varphi
satisfies
\Delta\varphi(\gamma)=0
for any

\gamma\in\Gamma
, then
\varphi
is called a
\mu
-
harmonic
function
.

The following fact plays an important role in the proof of
Theorem
A
.

Proposition 2.17
.

Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space and

\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
a homomorphism.
Let
f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
be a
\rho
-equivariant

\mu
-harmonic map, and
u\colon Y\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
a lower
semicontinuous convex function on
Y
satisfying

|u(p)|\leq C_{1}d_{Y}(p_{0},p)^{2}+C_{2}
for some
p_{0}\in Y

and
C_{1},C_{2}>0
.
Then the pull-back
f^{*}u
of
u
defined by
f^{*}u(\gamma)=u(f(\gamma))

is a
\mu
-subharmonic function on
\Gamma
.

Proof.

By Jensen’s inequality for
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces
(
[
11
, p. 242, Proposition 12.3]
), for a lower
semicontinuous convex function
u

and a probability measure
\nu
on
Y
, we have

u(\mathrm{bar}(\nu))\leq\int_{Y}u(p)d\nu(p).

Note that, since
\rho(\gamma)
is an isometry,

\mathrm{bar}(\rho(\gamma)_{*}\nu)=\rho(\gamma)\mathrm{bar}(\nu)
holds by the
definition of the barycenter.
Therefore, by Propositions
2.10
and

2.15
, we see that a
\mu
-harmonic map
f
satisfies

f(\gamma)=\mathrm{bar}((\rho(\gamma)\circ f)_{*}\mu)
for any

\gamma\in\Gamma
.
Thus, substituting
(\rho(\gamma)\circ f)_{*}\mu=f_{*}(\gamma_{*}\mu)
to

\nu
, we get

u(f(\gamma))\leq\int_{\Gamma}u(p)d(f_{*}(\gamma_{*}\mu))(p)=\int_{\Gamma}f^{*}u(\gamma^{\prime})d\gamma_{*}\mu(\gamma^{\prime})=\int_{\Gamma}f^{*}u(\gamma\gamma^{\prime\prime})d\mu(\gamma^{\prime\prime})

for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
A computation similar to that in the proof of Lemma
2.9
,
using the triangle inequality and the Schwarz inequality,
we see that
\gamma_{*}\mu
also has finite second moment.
Thus, by our assumption on
u
and Lemma
2.9
,
integrals in the expression above make sense.
Thus we conclude that
f^{*}u
is a
\mu
-subharmonic function.
∎

Remark 2.18
.

According to Ishihara
[
18
, Theorem 3.4]
, a smooth map
between Riemannian manifolds is harmonic if and only if the pull-back
of any (local) convex function on the target manifold is a (local)
subharmonic function.
A similar characterization is proved for a harmonic map from
a
1
-dimensional Riemannian polyhedron into a nonpositively curved
metric space
[
11
, Proposition 12.4]
.
Although our situation is much simpler than that in

[
11
]
as the proof above shows, we do not know if the
converse of Proposition
2.17
is also true
or not.

Remark 2.19
.

It should be easy to imagine that, for a convex function
u
,
the quantity
|u(\mathrm{bar}(\nu))-\int_{Y}u(p)d\nu(p)|
measures the
strength of the convexity of
u
.
Therefore, if we have a
\mu
-harmonic map

f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
and a convex function

u\colon Y\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
that is
\mu
-harmonic, then
we may expect that
u
has the “weakest possible convexity”
on the convex hull of
f(\Gamma)
.

Remark 2.20
.

In terms of probablity theory,

\mu
-subharmonic functions correspond to submartingales associated to
a random walk generated by
\mu
. See
[
38
]

for more information on martingale theory and harmonic maps.

We list some known existence results of
\mu
-harmonic maps.
(See
[
16
]
,
[
31
]
,

[
23
]
, and
[
32
]
for related results.)
A homomorphism
\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
is called

reductive in the sense of Jost
[
23
]
if there exists
a closed, convex
\rho(\Gamma)
-invariant subset
C
of
Y
satsifying
either of the following:

•

C
is isometric to a finite-dimensional Euclidean space, or

•

for any unbounded sequence
\{p_{n}\}\subset C
, there exists

\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\{d(p_{n},\rho(\gamma)p_{n})\}
is
unbounded.

It is well-known that if
\rho(\Gamma)
is
reductive in the sense of Jost, then there exists a

\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map even when
Y
is not proper (see

[
23
]
,
[
32
]
, and Corollary
6.4
).
Note that if
\rho(\Gamma)
is not
reductive in the sense of Jost, then there exists an unbounded
sequence
\{p_{n}\}\subset Y
such that, for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

d(p_{n},\rho(\gamma)p_{n})
is bounded.
If
Y
is proper, by passing to a subsequence of
\{p_{n}\}
, we can find
a limit point
\xi\in\partial Y
, and see that
\xi
is fixed by

\rho(\Gamma)
; thus if
Y
is proper and
\rho(\Gamma)
does not fix a
point in
\partial Y
, then there exists a
\rho
-equivariant

\mu
-harmonic map.
We will show that this is also true for nonproper
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces with
certain dimension bound
(Proposition
6.11
).

3.
The rate of escape and equivariant harmonic maps

Let
\Gamma
be a countable group with a symmetric probability
measure
\mu
whose support generates
\Gamma
.
This measure
\mu
can be regarded as a transition probability of a move
starting from the identity element
e
in
\Gamma
, and hence generates
a random walk on
\Gamma
.
We define
(\Omega,\mathbb{P})
to be
the set of increments of this random walk:

(\Omega,\mathbb{P})=(\Gamma,\mu)^{{\mathbb{N}}}=(\Gamma,\mu)\times(\Gamma,\mu)\times\dots.

Then, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a walk
\omega\in\Omega
,
the position of
\omega
, starting from
e
, at time
n
is given by a
map

\gamma_{n}\colon\Omega\rightarrow\Gamma;\ \omega\mapsto\omega_{1}\dots\omega_{n},

where
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\dots,\omega_{n},\dots)\in\Omega
.
Let
\mu^{n}
be a probability measure on
\Gamma
obtained as the
push-forward of
\mathbb{P}
by a map
\gamma_{n}
, that is,

\mu^{n}={\gamma_{n}}_{*}\mathbb{P}
.
Then we see that
\mu^{n}
describes the distribution of the position of a
random walk generated by
\mu
at time
n
,
and, for
\mu^{n}
-integrable function

\varphi\colon\Gamma\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\begin{split}\int_{\Gamma}\varphi(\gamma)d\mu^{n}(\gamma)&=\int_{\Omega}\varphi(\gamma_{n}(\omega))d\mathbb{P}(\omega)\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}\dots\left(\int_{\Gamma}\left(\int_{\Gamma}\varphi(\omega_{1}\dots\omega_{n-1}\omega_{n})d\mu(\omega_{n})\right)d\mu(\omega_{n-1})\right)\dots d\mu(\omega_{1});\end{split}

\mu^{n}
is the
n
-fold convolution of
\mu
.
Suppose that
\Gamma
acts on a metric space
Y=(Y,d)
via a
homomorphism
\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
and that the
action given by
\rho
has finite second moment with respect to
\mu
.
Then the action also has finite second moment with respect to

\mu^{n}
, according to the following computation and induction:
for
p\in Y
,

\begin{split}&\int_{\Gamma}d(p,\rho(\gamma)p)^{2}d\mu^{n}(\gamma)\\
=&\int_{\Gamma}\left(\int_{\Gamma}d(p,\rho(\gamma^{\prime}\omega_{n})p)^{2}d\mu(\omega_{n})\right)d\mu^{n-1}(\gamma^{\prime})\\
\leq&\int_{\Gamma}\left(\int_{\Gamma}\left(d(p,\rho(\gamma^{\prime})p)+d_{S}(\rho(\gamma^{\prime})p,\rho(\gamma^{\prime}\omega_{n})p)\right)^{2}d\mu(\omega_{n})\right)d\mu^{n-1}(\gamma^{\prime})\\
=&\int_{\Gamma}\left(\int_{\Gamma}d(p,\rho(\gamma^{\prime})p)^{2}+2d(p,\rho(\gamma^{\prime})p)d(p,\rho(\omega_{n})p)+d(p,\rho(\omega_{n})p)^{2}d\mu(\omega_{n})\right)d\mu^{n-1}(\gamma^{\prime})\\
\leq&\left(1+2\left(\int_{\Gamma}1+d(p,\rho(\omega_{n})p)^{2}\ d\mu(\omega_{n})\right)\right)\int_{\Gamma}1+d(p,\rho(\gamma^{\prime})p)^{2}\ d\mu^{n-1}(\gamma^{\prime})\\
&\phantom{aaa}+\int_{\Gamma}d(p,\rho(\omega_{n})p)^{2}d\mu(\omega_{n}),\end{split}

where we use the invariance of
d
under
\rho(\gamma^{\prime})
to deduce the
second equality, and

d(p,q)\leq 1+d(p,q)^{2}
to deduce the last inequality.

Now we introduce the quantity called the rate of escape and see
what happens when it vanishes.
It is well-known that, for almost all
\omega\in\Omega
,

\lim_{n}\frac{d(p,\rho(\gamma_{n}(\omega))p)}{n}

exists and the function

\omega\mapsto\lim_{n}\frac{d(p,\rho(\gamma_{n}(\omega))p)}{n}

converges to a constant function in
L^{1}(\Omega,\mathbb{P})

(see, for example,
[
40
, §8, A]
).
The value of this constant function is called the
rate of escape

(or the
drift
) of
\rho
and denoted by

l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=l_{\rho}(\Gamma,\mu)
.
It is easy to check, by using the triangle inequality,

l_{\rho}(\Gamma)
does not depend on the choice of
p\in Y
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
Y=(Y,d)
be a proper
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space, and
\Gamma
a
countable group equipped with a symmetric probability measure
\mu

whose support generates
\Gamma
.
Suppose that
\Gamma
acts on
Y
via a homomorphism

\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
and that
there exists a
\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map

f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
.

(
Note that the existence of a
\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic
map means that the action given by
\rho
has finite second moment.
)

If
l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=0
, then, for almost all
\omega\in\Omega
,
there exists

\xi(\omega)\in Y\cup\partial Y
such that a function

\varphi_{\omega}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
defined by

\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}d(\xi(\omega),f(\gamma))-d(\xi(\omega),f(e))&\text{if }\xi(\omega)\in Y\\
b_{\xi(\omega)}(f(\gamma),f(e))&\text{if }\xi(\omega)\in\partial Y\end{cases}

satisfies
\Delta\varphi_{\omega}(e)=0
.

Remark 3.2
.

If one assumes that
\mu(\gamma)\not=0
for any

\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
, then one can show that there exists

\omega\in\Omega
such that
\varphi_{\omega}
in the proposition
is
\mu
-harmonic, that is,
\Delta\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma)=0
for any

\gamma\in\Gamma
.
This is true even if
Y
is not proper, although
\xi(\omega)
may not
be a point in
Y\cup\partial Y

(Proposition
6.9
).
We will prove this in §
6.2
.

In order to prove Proposition
3.1
,
let us consider a function
L^{n}(f)
of a
\rho
-equivariant map

f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
defined as

L^{n}(f)=\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma))d\mu^{n}(\gamma)=\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),\rho(\gamma_{n}(\omega))f(e))d\mathbb{P}(\omega).

Take
\varphi
to be a function

\gamma\mapsto d(f(e),f(\gamma))
and set

\alpha_{n}=\int_{\Gamma}-\Delta\varphi(\gamma)d\mu^{n}(\gamma)=\int_{\Omega}-\Delta\varphi(\gamma_{n}(\omega))d\mathbb{P}(\omega).

Then there is the following relation between this
\alpha_{n}
and
the rate of escape
l_{\rho}(\Gamma)
:

Lemma 3.3
.

If
\liminf_{n}\alpha_{n}>0
, then
l_{\rho}(\Gamma)>0
.

Proof.

Notice that

\begin{split}&L^{n}(f)+\alpha_{n}=L^{n}(f)-\int_{\Gamma}\Delta\varphi(\gamma)d\mu^{n}(\gamma)\\
=&\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma))d\mu^{n}(\gamma)-\int_{\Gamma}\left(d(f(e),f(\gamma))-\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma\gamma^{\prime}))d\mu(\gamma^{\prime})\right)d\mu^{n}(\gamma)\\
=&\int_{\Gamma}\left(\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma\gamma^{\prime}))d\mu(\gamma^{\prime})\right)d\mu^{n}(\gamma)\\
=&L^{n+1}(f).\end{split}

Now suppose that
\liminf_{n}\alpha_{n}=c>0
.
Then there exists
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\inf_{n\geq n_{0}}\alpha_{n}\geq c/2.

Therefore, by taking
c^{\prime}=\min\{L^{n_{0}}(f)/n_{0},c/2\}
, we see that

L^{n}(f)\geq L^{n_{0}}(f)+\frac{c(n-n_{0})}{2}\geq c^{\prime}n

for any
n\geq n_{0}
.
Since

\omega\mapsto\frac{d(f(e),f(\gamma_{n}(\omega)))}{n}

converges to
l_{\rho}(\Gamma)
in
L^{1}(\Omega,\mathbb{P})
,
we have

l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\frac{d(f(e),f(\gamma_{n}(\omega)))}{n}\ d\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{L^{n}(f)}{n}\geq c^{\prime}>0.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.
Suppose that there exists a

\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map
f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
.
Since
f
is harmonic and
y\mapsto d(f(e),y)
is a continuous convex
function, by Proposition
2.17
, we see that

\varphi(\gamma)=d(f(e),f(\gamma))
becomes a

\mu
-subharmonic function, that is,

\Delta\varphi(\gamma)=\varphi(\gamma)-\int_{\Gamma}\varphi(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})d\mu(\gamma^{\prime})\leq 0

holds for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

Since we are assuming
l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=0
, we see that

\liminf_{n}\alpha_{n}\leq 0
by Lemma
3.3
.
On the other hand, since
\varphi
is subharmonic, we have

-\Delta\varphi(\gamma)\geq 0
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
and hence we conclude that

\liminf_{n}\int_{\Omega}-\Delta\varphi(\gamma_{n}(\omega))d\mathbb{P}(\omega)=0.

Therefore, by taking a subsequence
\{n_{j}\}\subset{\mathbb{N}}
, we have

\lim_{j}\int_{\Omega}-\Delta\varphi(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega))d\mathbb{P}(\omega)=0.

Since
-\Delta\varphi(\gamma)\geq 0
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
, this means
that

\{-\Delta\varphi(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\cdot))\colon\Omega\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}\}_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}

converges to
0
in
L^{1}(\Omega,\mathbb{P})
.
Thus, by taking a subsequence if necessary, we may assume that

-\Delta\varphi(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\cdot))\to 0
almost everywhere on

(\Omega,\mathbb{P})
.
Take
\omega\in\Omega
so that

-\Delta\varphi(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega))\to 0
.
Letting

\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}(\gamma)=d(f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{-1}),f(\gamma))-d(f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{-1}),f(e)),

we have

\Delta\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}(\gamma)=d(f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{-1}),f(\gamma))-\int_{\Gamma}d(f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{-1}),f(\gamma\gamma^{\prime}))d\mu(\gamma^{\prime}).

In particular, we see that

\begin{split}\Delta\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}(e)&=d(f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{{-1}}),f(e))-\int_{\Gamma}d(f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{-1}),f(\gamma^{\prime}))d\mu(\gamma^{\prime})\\
&=d(f(e),f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)))-\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega))\gamma^{\prime})d\mu(\gamma^{\prime})\\
&=\Delta\varphi(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)).\end{split}

Passing to a subsequence if necessary, since
Y
is proper, we may
assume that the sequence
\{\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{-1}\}
converges to a
point
\xi(\omega)\in Y\cup\partial Y
.
If
\xi(\omega)\in Y
, we have

\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma):=\lim_{j}\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}(\gamma)=d(\xi(\omega),f(\gamma))-d(\xi(\omega),f(e)),

while in the case
\xi(\omega)\in\partial Y
we get

\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma):=\lim_{j}\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}(\gamma)=b_{\xi(\omega)}(f(\gamma),f(e)),

where
b_{\xi(\omega)}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the
Busemann function associated to
\xi(\omega)

(see Remark
2.8
).
Note that

|\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}(\gamma)|=|d(f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{-1}),f(\gamma))-d(f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{-1}),f(e))|\leq d(f(e),f(\gamma))

and that
the action given by
\rho
has finite second moment with respect
to
\mu
.
Then we see that, by Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem,

\lim_{j}\int_{\Gamma}\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}(\gamma)d\mu(\gamma)=\int_{\Gamma}\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma)d\mu(\gamma),

and hence we get
\Delta\varphi_{\omega}(e)=0
.
This completes the proof of Proposition
3.1
.
∎

4.
Some consequences of the convexity of distance functions

In this section, we show two propositions which reflect the
convexity of distance functions on
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces.
These propositions, together with Proposition
3.1

above, play a key role in the proof of Theorem
A
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space, and
p,q\in Y
.
Let
c\colon[0,\infty)\longrightarrow Y
be a geodesic ray starting
from
p
.
Take
r_{0}>0
and let
p_{0}=c(r_{0})
.
Denote by
V_{c}
the element in
S_{p}Y
corresponding to the geodesic
segment
t\mapsto c(t)
.
Then we have

\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle\geq d(p_{0},p)-d(p_{0},q).

Proof.

Suppose that
\angle(\log_{p}(p_{0}),\log_{p}(q))=0
.
Then
\log_{p}(q)
lies in a geodesic ray
\{tV_{c}\mid t\geq 0\}
in

TC_{p}Y
and

\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle=d_{T_{p}}(O_{p},\log_{p}(q))=d(p,q)\geq d(p_{0},p)-d(p_{0},q)

by the triangle inequality.

Suppose
\angle(\log_{p}(p_{0}),\log_{p}(q))\not=0
and

\angle(\log_{p}(p_{0}),\log_{p}(q))<\pi
.
Set
W_{q}=\log_{p}(q)/\|\log_{p}(q)\|\in S_{p}Y
.
Since
0<\angle(V_{c},W_{q})=\angle(\log_{p}(p_{0}),\log_{p}(q))<\pi
,
there is a non-trivial geodesic segment
\bar{c}
in
S_{p}Y
starting
from
V_{c}
and terminating at
W_{q}
.
By attaching a segment of length
\pi-\angle(V_{c},W_{q})
to the
endpoint
W_{q}
, we obtain a
\mathrm{CAT}(1)
space
S_{p}
, containing

S_{p}Y
as a convex subset in which
\bar{c}
can be extended up to
length
\pi

(
[
5
, p. 347, 11.1 Basic Glueing Theorem]
).
Denote by
-V_{c}\in S_{p}
the endpoint of this extended
geodesic
\bar{c}
. Then we have

\angle(V_{c},W_{q})+\angle(W_{q},-V_{c})=\pi,

in particular,

(4.1)

-\left\langle-V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle=\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle.

Let
T_{p}
be the Euclidean cone over
S_{p}
.
Then
T_{p}
contains
TC_{p}Y
as a convex subset.
Note that
\{tV_{c}\mid t\geq 0\}\cup\{t(-V_{c})\mid t\geq 0\}

forms a geodesic line in
T_{p}
since
\angle(V_{c},-V_{c})=\pi
.
Let
\tilde{c}
be a geodesic defined by

\tilde{c}(t)=\begin{cases}tV_{c}&t\geq 0,\\
|t|(-V_{c})&t\leq 0.\end{cases}

Then
\tilde{c}
satisfies

\tilde{c}({\mathbb{R}})=\{tV_{c}\mid t\geq 0\}\cup\{t(-V_{c})\mid t\geq 0\}
,

\tilde{c}(0)=O_{p}
and
\left\langle V_{c},\,\tilde{c}(t)\right\rangle=t
for

t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Set
d_{o}=d_{T_{p}}(\log_{p}(p_{0}),O_{p})
and

d_{q}=d_{T_{p}}(\log_{p}(p_{0}),\log_{p}(q))
, and let

q^{\prime}=\tilde{c}(d_{o}-d_{q})
.
Then
q^{\prime}
is a point on
\tilde{c}({\mathbb{R}})
satisfying

\left\langle V_{c},\,q^{\prime}\right\rangle=d_{o}-d_{q}\geq d(p_{0},p)-d(p_{0},q),

where the last inequality holds because

d_{q}\leq d(p_{0},q)
while
d_{o}=d(p_{0},p)
.
Let
\bar{q}
be the image of
\log_{p}(q)
by the nearest point
projection onto the image
\tilde{c}({\mathbb{R}})
of
\tilde{c}
in

T_{p}
. Since the nearest point projection does not increase the
distance,

d_{T_{p}}(\log_{p}(p_{0}),\bar{q})\leq d_{q}
,
and hence

\bar{q}\in\tilde{c}\left(\left[d_{o}-d_{q},\ d_{o}+d_{q}\right]\right).

Therefore

\left\langle V_{c},\,\bar{q}\right\rangle\geq d_{o}-d_{q}=\left\langle V_{c},\,q^{\prime}\right\rangle\geq d(p_{0},p)-d(p_{0},q).

By the definition of the distance of a Euclidean cone, we have

(4.2)

\begin{split}&d_{T_{p}}(\tilde{c}(t),\log_{p}(q))^{2}\\
&=\begin{cases}t^{2}+|\log_{p}(q)|^{2}-2t|\log_{p}(q)|\cos\angle(V_{c},\log_{p}(q))&t\geq 0,\\
t^{2}+|\log_{p}(q)|^{2}-2|t||\log_{p}(q)|\cos\angle(-V_{c},\log_{p}(q))&t\leq 0.\end{cases}\end{split}

Take
t_{0}
to be
t_{0}V_{c}=\tilde{c}(t_{0})=\bar{q}
, and note that the
left-hand side of (
4.2
) is minimized at

t=t_{0}
.
If
t_{0}\not=0
, then the differentiation of

d_{T_{p}}(\tilde{c}(t),\log_{p}(q))^{2}
at
t=t_{0}
exists and it
must be equal to
0
, that is,

\begin{cases}t_{0}-|\log_{p}(q)|\cos\angle(V_{c},\log_{p}(q))=0&t_{0}>0,\\
t_{0}+|\log_{p}(q)|\cos\angle(-V_{c},\log_{p}(q))=0&t_{0}<0.\\
\end{cases}

Therefore

\begin{cases}\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle=|\log_{p}(q)|\cos\angle(V_{c},\log_{p}(q))=t_{0}=\left\langle V_{c},\,\bar{q}\right\rangle.&t_{0}>0,\\
\left\langle-V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle=|\log_{p}(q)|\cos\angle(-V_{c},\log_{p}(q))=-t_{0}=-\left\langle V_{c},\,\bar{q}\right\rangle.&t_{0}<0.\\
\end{cases}

Since
q^{\prime}
lies in
\tilde{c}({\mathbb{R}})
, we have

\left\langle-V_{c},\,q^{\prime}\right\rangle=-\left\langle V_{c},\,q^{\prime}\right\rangle
.
Together with (
4.1
), we get

\begin{cases}\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle=\left\langle V_{c},\,\bar{q}\right\rangle\geq d(p_{0},p)-d(p_{0},q)&t_{0}>0\\
\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle=-\left\langle-V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle=\left\langle V_{c},\,\bar{q}\right\rangle\geq d(p_{0},p)-d(p_{0},q)&t_{0}<0.\end{cases}

Thus we obtain the desired inequality in the case
t_{0}\not=0
.

If
t_{0}=0
, then, from (
4.2
),
the right (resp. the left) differentiation of

d_{T_{p}}(\tilde{c}(t),\log_{p}(q))^{2}
at
t=0
exists and it is
nonnegative (resp. nonpositive). Noting (
4.1
) again,
we have

\begin{cases}-|\log_{p}(q)|\cos\angle(V_{c},\log_{p}(q))=-\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle\geq 0\\
-|\log_{p}(q)|\cos\angle(-V_{c},\log_{p}(q))=-\left\langle-V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle=\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle\geq 0,\\
\end{cases}

which implies
\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle=0
.
On the other hand, since the nearest point projection does not increase
the distance,
t_{0}=0
means that
d_{q}\geq d_{o}
;
therefore

\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle\geq d_{o}-d_{q}\geq d(p_{0},p)-d(p_{0},q)

again.

Now suppose
\angle(V_{c},\log_{p}(q))=\pi
. In this case,

V_{c}
and
\log_{p}(q)
can be joined by a geodesic segment in
T_{p}

passing through
O_{p}
, hence

\begin{split}\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle&=d_{T_{p}}(O_{p},\log_{p}(q))\cos\angle(V_{c},\log_{p}(q))\\
&=-d_{T_{p}}(O_{p},\log_{p}(q))\\
&=d_{o}-d_{q}.\end{split}

Since
d_{o}=d(p_{0},p)
and
d_{q}\leq d(p_{0},q)
, we get

\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle\geq d(p_{0},p)-d(p_{0},q).

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

Note that, according to the proof above, we have

\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle=\left\langle V_{c},\,\bar{q}\right\rangle\geq\left\langle V_{c},\,q^{\prime}\right\rangle

in any case.
This can be regarded as an expression of
the convexity of a metric ball centered at
\log_{p}(p_{0})
(or
p_{0}
).
And if the equality in the inequality above holds,
then it should be understood that the points in the statement are
arranged in a convex set with the “weakest” convexity, which is
an interval.
See the next remark.

Remark 4.3
.

In the proof above, when
\angle(V_{c},\log_{p}(q))
is equal to either

0
or
\pi
, the geodesic
segment joining
V_{c}
and
\log_{p}(q)
extends or passes through the
origin
O_{p}
, and hence
\log_{p}(q)=\bar{q}
.
If
\angle(V_{c},\log_{p}(q))\not=0,\pi
, then the equality in the
proposition holds if and only if
\bar{q}=q^{\prime}
.
In this case, since

d(\log_{p}(p_{0}),\log_{p}(q))=d(\log_{p}(p_{0}),q^{\prime})=d(\log_{p}(p_{0}),\bar{q})
,
the nearest point projection from
[\log_{p}(p_{0}),\log_{p}(q)]
to

\tilde{c}({\mathbb{R}})
turns out to be an isometry.
By
[
5
, p. 182, 2.12(3)]
,
the distance between
\tilde{c}({\mathbb{R}})
and each point of

[\log_{p}(p_{0}),\log_{p}(q)]
must be constant.
Both sets share the same point
\log_{p}(p_{0})
, the distance between these
set must be
0
. Therefore
\log_{p}(q)=\bar{q}
.

Letting
p_{0}\to\xi=c(\infty)
in
Proposition
4.1
, we obtain the
following proposition, which can be viewed as a sort of
quantitative expression of the convexity of the horoball.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space, and
p,q\in Y
.
Let
c\colon[0,\infty)\longrightarrow Y
be a geodesic ray starting
from
p
and terminating at
\xi\in\partial Y
.
Denoting the element corresponding to
t\mapsto c(t)
in
S_{p}Y
by

V_{c}
, we have

\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle\geq b_{\xi}(p,q).

If the equality holds in the inequality above, then the convex hull of

[p,\xi)\cup[q,\xi)\cup[p,q]
is
isometric to a half-infinite parallelogram in the Euclidean plane.

Proof.

Let
p_{0}=c(t_{0})
and recall
Proposition
4.1
. Then we have

\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle\geq d(c(t_{0}),p)-d(c(t_{0}),q)

for any
t_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Letting
t_{0}\to\infty
yields the desired
inequality.

Take
S_{p}
and
T_{p}
as in the proof of
Proposition
4.1
.
Note that

O_{p}
and
q^{\prime}
in
\tilde{c}({\mathbb{R}})
are located on the same side of

\tilde{c}({\mathbb{R}})
with respect to
\log_{p}(p_{0})=\tilde{c}(t_{0})
for
sufficiently large
t_{0}
. Recalling the definition of
q^{\prime}
and
Remark
4.2
, although
q^{\prime}
may depend on
t_{0}
, we have

\begin{split}b_{\tilde{c}(\infty)}(O_{p},\log_{p}(q))&=\lim_{t_{0}\to\infty}d_{T_{p}}(\tilde{c}(t_{0}),O_{p})-d_{T_{p}}(\tilde{c}(t_{0}),\log_{p}(q))\\
&=\lim_{t_{0}\to\infty}d_{T_{p}}(\tilde{c}(t_{0}),O_{p})-d_{T_{p}}(\tilde{c}(t_{0}),q^{\prime})\\
&=\lim_{t_{0}\to\infty}\left\langle V_{c},\,q^{\prime}\right\rangle\\
&\leq\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle.\end{split}

On the other hand, since
\log_{p}
does not increase the distance while

d(c(t_{0}),p)=d_{TC_{p}Y}(\tilde{c}(t_{0}),O_{p})
and

\log_{p}(c(t_{0}))=\tilde{c}(t_{0})
for
t_{0}\geq 0
, we have

\begin{split}b_{c(\infty)}(p,q)&=\lim_{t_{0}\to\infty}d(c(t_{0}),p)-d(c(t_{0}),q)\\
&\leq\lim_{t_{0}\to\infty}d_{TC_{p}Y}(\tilde{c}(t_{0}),O_{p})-d_{TC_{p}Y}(\tilde{c}(t_{0}),\log_{p}(q))\\
&=b_{\tilde{c}(\infty)}(O_{p},\log_{p}(q)).\end{split}

Therefore the equality

\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{p}(q)\right\rangle=b_{c(\infty)}(p,q)
means that

b_{\tilde{c}(\infty)}(O_{p},\log_{p}(q))=b_{c(\infty)}(p,q)

which implies that

\lim_{r_{0}\to\infty}\left(d(c(t_{0}),q)-d_{TC_{p}Y}(\tilde{c}(t_{0}),\log_{p}(q))\right)=b_{\tilde{c}(\infty)}(O_{p},\log_{p}(q))-b_{c(\infty)}(p,q)=0.

Then, by Lemma
4.5
below, we see that

d(c(t_{0}),q)=d_{TC_{p}Y}(\tilde{c}(t_{0}),\log_{p}(q))
for any

t_{0}\in[0,\infty)
. Note that, by the definition of the distance on

TC_{p}Y
, any triangle in
TC_{p}Y
having a vertex
O_{p}
is isometric to
a triangle in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. Thus

\triangle(\tilde{c}(t_{0}),O_{p},\log_{p}(q))
can be regarded as the
comparison triangle for
\triangle(c(t_{0}),p,q)

t_{0}\in[0,\infty)
.
Since
\angle c(t_{0})pq=\angle c(t_{0})O_{p}\log_{p}(q)
by definition,

[
5
, p. 180, 2.9 Proposition]
tells us that

\triangle(c(t_{0}),p,q)
is a flat triangle for any
t_{0}\in[0,\infty)
.
Therefore the convex hull of
[p,\xi)\cup[q,\xi)\cup[p,q]
is
isometric to a region obtained as the limit of a increasing sequence of
flat triangles isometric to
\triangle(c(t_{0}),p,q)
,
t_{0}\in[0,\infty)
,
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, which gives us a half-infinite parallelogram.
This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
c\colon[0,\infty)\rightarrow Y
be a geodesic ray and
q\in Y
.
Set
\tilde{c}(t):=\log_{c(0)}(c(t))
. If

\lim_{t\to\infty}d(c(t),q)-d_{TC_{c(0)}Y}(\tilde{c}(t),\log_{c(0)}(q))=0

holds, then, for any
t\in[0,\infty)
,

d(c(t),q)=d_{TC_{c(0)}Y}(\tilde{c}(t),\log_{c(0)}(q))

holds.

Proof.

Let
\triangle(\overline{c(0)},\overline{c(t)},\overline{q})
be a
comparison triangle of
\triangle(c(0),c(t),q)
, and denote the
angle
\angle_{\overline{c(0)}}(\overline{c(t)},\overline{q})

by
\theta(t)
. Let

\tilde{\theta}=\angle_{O_{c(0)}}(\tilde{c}(t),\log_{c(0)}(q))
.
Note that
\tilde{\theta}
is a constant independent of
t
, and

\theta(t)\to\tilde{\theta}
as
t\to 0
by the definition of the
angle, and that

\theta(t)
is nondecreasing as
t
getting larger
by the definition of
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
(or see, for example,
[
5
, p. 161, 1.7(3)]
).
Set

d(t)=d(c(t),q),\quad\tilde{d}(t)=d_{TC_{c(0)}Y}(\tilde{c}(t),\log_{c(0)}(q)),\quad P=d(c(0),q),

then by the cosine rule,
we see that

\cos\theta(t)=\frac{t^{2}+P^{2}-d(t)^{2}}{2tP},\quad\cos\tilde{\theta}=\frac{t^{2}+P^{2}-\tilde{d}(t)^{2}}{2tP}.

Since
\theta(t)
is nondecreasing, if
\theta(t_{0})>\tilde{\theta}

for some
t_{0}
, then by putting
C=\theta(t_{0})-\tilde{\theta}
we have

\theta(t)-\tilde{\theta}\geq C
for any
t\geq t_{0}
.
Therefore, there exists
C^{\prime}>0
such that

\cos\tilde{\theta}-\cos\theta(t)=\frac{d(t)^{2}-\tilde{d}(t)^{2}}{2tP}\geq C^{\prime}

for any
t\geq t_{0}
.
Since

d(t)=\sqrt{t^{2}+P^{2}-2tP\cos\theta(t)},\quad\tilde{d}(t)=\sqrt{t^{2}+P^{2}-2tP\cos\tilde{\theta}},

we get

\frac{d(t)^{2}-\tilde{d}(t)^{2}}{2tP}=\frac{d(t)-\tilde{d}(t)}{2P}\left(\sqrt{1+\frac{P^{2}}{t^{2}}-2\frac{P\cos\theta(t)}{t}}+\sqrt{1+\frac{P^{2}}{t^{2}}-2\frac{P\cos\tilde{\theta}}{t}}\right).

On the other hand, for
t\geq t_{0}
, we have

\sqrt{1+\frac{P^{2}}{t^{2}}-2\frac{P\cos\theta(t)}{t}},\sqrt{1+\frac{P^{2}}{t^{2}}-2\frac{P\cos\tilde{\theta}}{t}}\leq\sqrt{\left(1+\frac{P}{t}\right)^{2}}=1+\frac{P}{t}\leq 1+\frac{P}{t_{0}},

hence

C^{\prime}\leq\frac{d(t)^{2}-\tilde{d}(t)^{2}}{2tP}\leq 2\left(1+\frac{P}{t_{0}}\right)\frac{d(t)-\tilde{d}(t)}{2P}.

Thus, for
t\geq t_{0}
,

\frac{t_{0}C^{\prime}P}{(t_{0}+P)}\leq d(t)-\tilde{d}(t).

Thus
\lim_{t\to\infty}d(t)-\tilde{d}(t)=0
means that
C^{\prime}=0
, that
is,
\theta(t)=\tilde{\theta}
for any
t\geq 0
.
Therefore we get
d(t)=\tilde{d}(t)
for any
t\geq 0
.
∎

5.
Proof of Theorem
A
: when
Y
is proper

In this section, we give the proof of Theorem
A
in the
case that a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
is proper.
Suppose that a countable group
\Gamma
with a random walk

\mu
acts on
Y
via
\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})

with finite second moment,
and we are assuming that
\rho(\Gamma)
does not fix a point in

\partial Y
.
Then as we mentioned in §
2
, there exists

\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map
f
.
If
l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=0
, then by Proposition
3.1
,
for almost all
\omega\in\Omega
, there exists

\xi(\omega)\in Y\cup\partial Y
such that

\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}d(\xi(\omega),f(\gamma))-d(\xi(\omega),f(e))&\xi(\omega)\in Y\\
b_{\xi(\omega)}(f(\gamma),f(e))&\xi(\omega)\in\partial Y\end{cases}

satisfies
\Delta\varphi_{\omega}(e)=0
.

First we note that we may assume, by replacing
\mu
with a convex
combination of
\mu^{n}
, that the support
\mathrm{supp}\,\mu
of
a probability measure
\mu
contains
\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
.
Indeed, since we are assuming that the support of our original
\mu

generates
\Gamma
, we see that, for any
\gamma
, there exists
n
such
that
\mu^{n}(\gamma)\not=0
.
Thus we may assume that
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}\mu^{n}(\gamma)\not=0

for any
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
, where
a_{n}\geq 0
and

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}=1
.
It is easy to see that, by taking
a_{n}
suitably,
the action given by
\rho
has finite second moment with respect to

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}\mu^{n}
, since it is true for each

\mu^{n}
as we have seen in §
3
.
Furthermore, according to
[
13
, Theorem 4.5]
,

l_{\rho}(\Gamma,\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}\mu^{n})=\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}na_{n}\right)l_{\rho}(\Gamma,\mu)

holds, and hence we may assume

l_{\rho}(\Gamma,\sum_{i=n}^{\infty}a_{n}\mu^{n})=0
, which is our
assumption on
\mu
in Theorems
A
and
C
.
(Although the outline of the proof of
[
13
, Theorem 4.5]
is
suggested there, for the sake of completeness, we will give a proof of a
weaker result in §
8
, which is sufficient for our
purpose.)
Note also that, since we are assuming that
\rho(\Gamma)

fixes no point in
\partial Y
and
Y
is proper,
the existence of
\mu
-harmonic map is not
affected by this change of a probability measure
\mu
.

The following lemma summarizes the core argument used in the
proof of Theorem
A
and Theorem
C
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
(
we do not assume
Y
to be any one of proper,
of finite telescopic dimension, or of locally finite dimension here
)
,
and
\Gamma
a countable group with
symmetric and nondegenerate probability measure
\mu
.
Further assume that
\mathrm{supp}\,\mu\supset\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
.
Suppose that
\Gamma
acts on
Y
via a homomorphism

\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
and that there exists a

\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map
f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
.

(1)
Let
\xi\in Y
and

\varphi\colon\gamma\mapsto d(\xi,f(\gamma))-d(\xi,f(e))
.
If
\Delta\varphi(e)=0
holds, then there exists a point in
Y
fixed
by
\rho(\Gamma)
.

(2)
Let
\xi\in\partial Y
and

\varphi\colon\gamma\mapsto b_{\xi}(f(\gamma),f(e))
.
If
\Delta\varphi(e)=0
holds, then, for any

\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
, the convex hull of

[f(e),\xi)\cup[f(e),f(\gamma)]\cup[f(\gamma),\xi)

is isometric to a half-infinite parallelogram in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

(3)
Suppose that the assumption of
(2)
is satisfied and that there
exists a sequence
\{\gamma_{j}\}\subset\Gamma
such that

\{f(\gamma_{j})\}
converges to
\xi
in
(2)
in the cone topology.
If further
\{\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi\}
and
\{f(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\}

converge to
\xi^{+},\xi^{-}\in\partial Y
in the cone topology
respectively,
then, for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
, there exists a geodesic

c_{\gamma}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow Y
such that
c_{\gamma}(0)=f(\gamma)

and
c_{\gamma}(\pm\infty)=\xi^{\pm}
.

(4)
Suppose that

l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=0
.
Under the assumptions of
(2)
and
(3)
,
the convex hull
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
splits as
H\times Z
, where

H
is a complete Hilbert space and
Z
does not admit any splitting

{\mathbb{R}}\times Z^{\prime}
.
Furthermore,
\rho
splits along this splitting
as
\rho=\rho_{1}\times\rho_{2}
, where

\rho_{1}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({H})
and

\rho_{2}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Z})
.
There also exists
\rho_{2}
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map

f_{2}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Z
, and
l_{\rho_{2}}(\Gamma)=0

holds.

Proof.

(1)
We show that
f
is a constant map under the assumption.
Then we see that
f(\Gamma)=\rho(\Gamma)f(e)
is a point, and this point
is fixed by
\rho(\Gamma)
.

If
\\
xi=f(e)
