, then

\varphi(\gamma)=d(f(\gamma),f(e))
,
and
\Delta\varphi(e)=0
means that

\Delta\varphi(e)=\varphi(e)-\int_{\Gamma}\varphi(\gamma)d\mu(\gamma)=-\int d(f(\gamma),f(e))d\mu(\gamma)=0,

namely
f(\Gamma)=f(e)
;
f
is a constant map.

Now assume
\xi\not=f(e)
.
Take any
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
, and note that

\mu(\gamma)\not=0
by our assumption.
Let
p=f(e)
,
q=f(\gamma)
and
p_{0}=\xi
in
Proposition
4.1
,
and denote by
V_{c}\in S_{f(e)}Y
the element corresponding to the
geodesic
c_{f(e)}^{\xi}
joining
f(e)
and
\xi
.
Then we get

\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle\geq d(\xi,f(e))-d(\xi,f(\gamma))=-\varphi(\gamma).

By Proposition
2.10
and

\varphi(e)=0
, we see that

\begin{split}0&\geq\int_{\Gamma}\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle d\mu(\gamma)\geq\int_{\Gamma}-\varphi(\gamma)d\mu(\gamma)\\
&=\left(\varphi(e)-\int_{\Gamma}\varphi(\gamma)d\mu(\gamma)\right)\\
&=\Delta(\varphi(e))=0.\end{split}

Therefore we get

\int_{\Gamma}\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle d\mu(\gamma)=0.

Moreover the equality in
Propostion
4.1
must hold
for each
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
:

(5.1)

\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle=d(\xi,f(e))-d(\xi,f(\gamma)).

By Remark
4.3
this implies that

\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\in\tilde{c}({\mathbb{R}})
for every

\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
, where
\tilde{c}
is a geodesic in

T_{f(e)}
passing through
O_{f(e)}
and
\log_{f(e)}(\xi)
.
Hence we have

\begin{split}d(\xi,f(\gamma))&=d(\xi,f(e))-\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle\\
&=d_{T_{f(e)}}(\log_{f(e)}(\xi),\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))).\end{split}

Therefore

\triangle(O_{f(e)},\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma)),\log_{f(e)}(\xi))
,
which becomes a comparison triangle of
\triangle(f(e),f(\gamma),\xi)
,
degenerates to a segment and contained in
\tilde{c}({\mathbb{R}})
.
This means that
f(\gamma)
is contained in a geodesic segment passing through

f(e)
and
\xi
for every
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
.
Note that, although such a geodesic on
Y
may not be unique, all such
geodesics share a geodesic segment
[\xi,f(e)]
in common.

Set

a(\gamma):=\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle=d(\xi,f(e))-d(\xi,f(\gamma)),

and
b:=d(\xi,f(e))
.
Then we have

a(\gamma)=\begin{cases}d(f(e),f(\gamma))&\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle\geq 0\\
-d(f(e),f(\gamma))&\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle\leq 0,\end{cases}

and, by definition, we get
b\geq a(\gamma)

for all
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
.
Suppose that, for
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
f(\gamma)
lies on a
geodesic segment starting from
f(e)
and passing through
\xi
.
Then we have
\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle\geq 0
,
and
b\geq a(\gamma)
means that

d(\xi,f(e))\geq d(f(\gamma),f(e))
, in other words,

f(\gamma)\in[\xi,f(e)]
.
Therefore, for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

f(\gamma)
must lie on a geodesic segment starting from
\xi

and passing through
f(e)
; it cannot lie somewhere beyond
\xi
.

Let
\xi^{\prime}
be the point in the closure of
f(\Gamma)
closest to
\xi
.
Thus, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a
\gamma_{j}\in\Gamma

with
d(f(\gamma_{j}),\xi^{\prime})<\varepsilon
.
Suppose that there is
\gamma_{0}\in\Gamma
such
that
f(\gamma_{0})=\xi^{\prime}
.
If
\gamma_{0}=e
, then we have
d(f(\gamma),\xi)\geq d(f(e),\xi)
for
any
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
Hence
\Delta\varphi(e)=0
implies that

f(\Gamma)
must become the point
f(e)
, that is,
f
is a constant
map.
Suppose that
\gamma_{0}\not=e
.
For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

f(\gamma)
lies in a geodesic segment starting from

\xi^{\prime}=f(\gamma_{0})
and passing through
f(e)\not=\xi^{\prime}
.
Then there is
V_{0}\in S_{f(\gamma_{0})}Y
representing these geodesics,
and we get

\begin{split}\int_{\Gamma}\left\langle V_{0},\,\log_{f(\gamma_{0})}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle\ d{\gamma_{0}}_{*}\mu(\gamma)=&\int_{\Gamma}d(f(\gamma_{0}),f(\gamma_{0}(\gamma_{0}^{-1}\gamma)))\ d\mu(\gamma_{0}^{-1}\gamma)\\
\geq&d(f(\gamma_{0}),f(e))\mu(\gamma_{0}^{-1})>0.\end{split}

Recalling Remark
2.16
, we see that this
contradicts the harmonicity of
f
.
Therefore
f(\gamma)\not=\xi^{\prime}
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
Note that, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
,
f(\gamma_{j})
must lie
in the interior of a geodesic segment
[\xi^{\prime},f(e)]
.
Set

a_{j}(\gamma)=\left\langle V_{c,f(\gamma_{j})},\,\log_{f(\gamma_{j})}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle
,
where
V_{c,f(\gamma_{j})}
denotes the element in
S_{f(\gamma_{j})}Y

corresponding to the geodesic
c_{f(\gamma_{j})}^{\xi^{\prime}}
.

Note that, since
f(\Gamma)
lies in a union of geodesic
having
[\xi^{\prime},f(e)]
in common, we may regard that
f(\Gamma)

lies in a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space locally isometric to
{\mathbb{R}}
around

f(\gamma_{j})\in(\xi^{\prime},f(e))
, and hence

\log_{f(\gamma_{j})}(f(\Gamma))
lies in a geodesic line in

TC_{f(\gamma_{j})}Y
.
Then, by Remark
2.13
and Remark
2.16
,
we see that
f
satisfies

(5.2)

\int_{\Gamma}a_{j}(\gamma)d{\gamma_{j}}_{*}\mu(\gamma)=0.

Since each
f(\gamma)
lies in a geodesic segment starting from

\xi^{\prime}
and passing through
f(\gamma_{j})
, we have

a_{j}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}d(f(\gamma_{j}),f(\gamma))&a_{j}(\gamma)\geq 0\\
-d(f(\gamma_{j}),f(\gamma))&a_{j}(\gamma)<0,\end{cases}

and
a_{j}(\gamma)\leq d(f(\gamma_{j}),\xi^{\prime})<\varepsilon
.
Thus we have

\int_{\Gamma}\max\{a_{j}(\gamma),0\}d{\gamma_{j}}_{*}\mu(\gamma)<\varepsilon.

Therefore, by (
5.2
),

\begin{split}\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma^{\prime}))d\mu(\gamma^{\prime})&=\int_{\Gamma}d(f(\gamma_{j}),f(\gamma_{j}\gamma^{\prime}))d{\gamma_{j}}_{*}\mu(\gamma_{j}\gamma^{\prime})\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}|a_{j}(\gamma)|d{\gamma_{j}}_{*}\mu(\gamma)<2\varepsilon.\end{split}

Since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, this means that
f
is a constant
map.

(2)

Take any
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
.
Let
p=f(e)
,
q=f(\gamma)
in Proposition
4.4
.
Then we get

\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle\geq b_{\xi}(f(e),f(\gamma))=-\varphi(\gamma).

As in the proof of
(1)
above, by
Proposition
2.10
and
\varphi(e)=0
, we see that

\begin{split}0&\geq\int_{\Gamma}\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle d\mu(\gamma)\geq\int_{\Gamma}-\varphi(\gamma)d\mu(\gamma)\\
&=\left(\varphi(e)-\int_{\Gamma}\varphi(\gamma)d\mu(\gamma)\right)\\
&=\Delta(\varphi(e))=0.\end{split}

Therefore we get

(5.3)

\int_{\Gamma}\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle d\mu(\gamma)=0.

Moreover the equality in
Propostion
4.4
must hold
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
:

\left\langle V_{c},\,\log_{f(e)}(f(\gamma))\right\rangle=b_{\xi}(f(e),f(\gamma)).

By Proposition
4.4
, for all

\gamma\in\Gamma
, the convex hull of

[f(e),\xi(\omega))\cup[f(e),f(\gamma)]\cup[f(\gamma),\xi(\omega))

is isometric to a half-infinite parallelogram in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

(3)

Denote by
F_{\gamma}
a half-infinite parallelogram bounded by

[f(e),\xi)\cup[f(e),f(\gamma)]\cup[f(\gamma),\xi)

obtained in
(2)
.
Take any
\gamma\in\Gamma
and fix it for a while. Note that

\{f(\gamma_{j}\gamma)\}=\{\rho(\gamma_{j})f(\gamma)\}
also converges to

\xi
by our assumption.
Since
F_{\gamma_{j}\gamma}
’s are half-infinite parallelograms,

[f(e),\xi)
and
[f(\gamma_{j}\gamma),\xi)
are
parallel to each other.
Thus by taking
q_{j}\in[f(e),\xi)
so that

d([f(e),\xi),f(\gamma_{j}\gamma))=d(q_{j},f(\gamma_{j}\gamma))
, for
sufficiently large
j
, we may assume

d(q_{j},f(\gamma_{j}\gamma))=d([f(e),\xi),[f(\gamma_{j}\gamma),\xi)),

and

(5.4)

\begin{split}&\angle_{f(\gamma_{j}\gamma)}(\xi,f(e))=\angle_{f(\gamma_{j}\gamma)}(\xi,q_{j})+\angle_{f(\gamma_{j}\gamma)}(q_{j},f(e)),\\
&\angle_{f(\gamma_{j}\gamma)}(\xi,q_{j})=\angle_{q_{j}}(\xi,f(\gamma_{j}\gamma))=\frac{\pi}{2}.\end{split}

Put

\theta_{\gamma_{j}\gamma}=\angle_{f(\gamma_{j}\gamma)}(q_{j},f(e))
.
Again noting that
F_{\gamma_{j}\gamma}
is flat, we get

\begin{split}\cos\theta_{\gamma_{j}\gamma}&=\frac{d(q_{j},f(\gamma_{j}\gamma))}{d(f(e),f(\gamma_{j}\gamma))}=\frac{d([f(e),\xi),f(\gamma_{j}\gamma))}{d(f(e),f(\gamma_{j}\gamma))}\\
&=\sin\angle_{f(e)}(\xi,f(\gamma_{j}\gamma))\to 0,\end{split}

since
f(\gamma_{j}\gamma)\to\xi
.
Therefore
\theta_{\gamma_{j}\gamma}\to\pi/2
.
By (
5.4
), we see that the angle

\angle_{f(\gamma_{j}\gamma)}(\xi(\omega),f(e))
tends to
\pi
.

Consider the union of a geodesic segment and a geodesic ray

\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\left([f(e),f(\gamma_{j}\gamma)]\cup[f(\gamma_{j}\gamma),\xi)\right)=[f(\gamma_{j}^{-1}),f(\gamma)]\cup[f(\gamma),\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi),

for each
j
.
Recall that

\{\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi\}
and
\{f(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\}
converge
to points
\xi^{+},\xi^{-}\in\partial Y
respectively by our
assumption.
Therefore we may assume that a sequence of geodesic rays

\{c_{f(\gamma)}^{\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi}\}
converges to

c_{f(\gamma)}^{\xi^{+}}
unifomly on each bounded interval,
where
c_{f(\gamma)}^{\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi}
denotes the
geodesic starting from
f(\gamma)
and terminating at

\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi
as before.
On the other hand, setting

\tilde{c}_{j}\colon[0,\infty)\rightarrow Y
to be

\tilde{c}_{j}(t)=\begin{cases}c_{f(\gamma)}^{f(\gamma_{j}^{-1})}(t)&t\leq d(f(\gamma),f(\gamma_{j}^{-1}))\\
f(\gamma_{j}^{-1})&t\geq d(f(\gamma),f(\gamma_{j}^{-1})),\end{cases}

we see that
\{\tilde{c}_{j}\}
converges to
c_{f(\gamma)}^{\xi^{-}}

uniformly on each bounded interval.
Recalling that letting
j\to\infty
gives

\angle_{f(\gamma)}(\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi,f(\gamma_{j}^{-1}))=\angle_{f(\gamma_{j}\gamma)}(\xi,f(e))\to\pi,

we see that the images of
c_{f(\gamma)}^{\xi^{+}}
and

c_{f(\gamma)}^{\xi^{-}}
form a straight geodesic line passing through

f(\gamma)
whose endpoints in
\partial Y
are
\xi^{+}
and
\xi^{-}
.

(4)
Now let
\tilde{S}
be the union of the images of all geodesics
parallel to
c({\mathbb{R}})
, where
c
is a geodesic satisfying
c(0)=f(e)
,

c(-\infty)=\xi^{-}
,
c(\infty)=\xi^{+}
.
Then, by the discussion above, there exists a geodesic
joining
\xi^{+}
and
\xi^{-}
passing through
f(\gamma)
for each

\gamma\in\Gamma
, and thus

f(\Gamma)\subset\tilde{S}
.
By
[
5
, p. 183 2.14]
, we see that

\tilde{S}
is convex, and isometric to a product metric
space
{\mathbb{R}}\times S
.
We may take
S
to be a
convex subset of the form
\mathrm{proj}_{c}^{-1}(c(0))
,
where
\mathrm{proj}_{c}\colon Y\rightarrow c({\mathbb{R}})
is the nearest point
projection onto
c({\mathbb{R}})
and
c
is a geodesic satisfying
c(0)=f(e)
,

c(-\infty)=\xi^{-}
, and
c(\infty)=\xi^{+}
as above.
Let
\{q_{j}\}\subset S
and
q_{j}\to q
in
Y
. Then
there is a geodesic
c_{j}
satisfying
c_{j}(0)=q_{j}
,
c_{j}(-\infty)=\xi^{-}
,
and
c_{j}(\infty)=\xi^{+}
for each
j
. Since
c_{j}
’s are parallel to each
other,
d(c_{j}(t),c_{j^{\prime}}(t))=d(c_{j}(0),c_{j^{\prime}}(0))
for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and

j,j^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and hence
\{c_{j}(t)\}
is a Cauchy sequence for each
t
.
Since
Y
is complete,
\{c_{j}(t)\}
converges and forms a geodesic
joining
\xi^{-}
and
\xi^{+}
and passing through
q
. Therefore
S
is
closed, and
\tilde{S}={\mathbb{R}}\times S
is also closed.

Now we show that the convex hull
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
of
f(\Gamma)

splits as
{\mathbb{R}}\times S_{0}
.
By definition,
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
is the closure of the minimal convex
subset of
Y
containing
f(\Gamma)
.
Since
\tilde{S}
is a closed convex subset and

f(\Gamma)\subset\tilde{S}
,
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
is contained in

\tilde{S}
.
Note that
f(\Gamma)
is invariant under the action of
\rho(\Gamma)
,
hence so is
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
.
Recall that
\xi(\omega)=\lim f(\gamma_{j})
.
Then for any
q\in\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
, we have

[q,f(\gamma_{j})]\subset\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
and, as a set

[q,f(\gamma_{j})]
converges to
[q,\xi(\omega))
. Since

\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
is closed, we see that

[q,\xi(\omega))\subset\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
. The same is true for

\xi^{-}=\lim f(\gamma_{j}^{-1})
. Since
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
is

\rho(\Gamma)
-invariant,

[q,\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi(\omega))\subset\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
for
any
q\in\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
and
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Since

[q,\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi(\omega))
converges to
[q,\xi^{+})
by the
definition of
\xi^{+}
, we see that
[q,\xi^{+})
is also contained in

\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
.

Let
S_{0}=\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))\cap S
. Then
S_{0}
is a closed convex
subset of
Y
.
As we have seen above, for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

[f(\gamma),\xi^{+})
(resp.
[f(\gamma),\xi^{-})
) is contained in

\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
.
Since there is a geodesic
c_{\gamma}
joining
\xi^{+}
and
\xi^{-}
passing
through
f(\gamma)
,
[f(\gamma),\xi^{+})\cup[f(\gamma),\xi^{-})
must
coincide with the image
c_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}})
of
c_{\gamma}
.
Therefore
c_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}})\subset\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
and,
by reparametrizing
c_{\gamma}
so that
c_{\gamma}(0)\in S
, we see
that
c_{\gamma}(0)\in S\cap\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))=S_{0}
.
Hence
f(\gamma)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times S_{0}\subset\tilde{S}
for every

\gamma\in\Gamma
.
Since
S_{0}
is closed and convex, so is
{\mathbb{R}}\times S_{0}
, and we see that

\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))\subset{\mathbb{R}}\times S_{0}
.
On the other hand, for any
s\in S_{0}
, since

s\in\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
,

[s,\xi^{+})
and
[s,\xi^{-})
are contained in
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
as we
have seen above.
Since there is a geodesic line joining
\xi^{+}
and
\xi^{-}
passing through

s
,
[s,\xi^{+})\cup[s,\xi^{-})
must coincide with the image

{\mathbb{R}}\times\{s\}
of this geodesic line.
Therefore
{\mathbb{R}}\times S_{0}\subset\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
.
We conclude that
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))={\mathbb{R}}\times S_{0}
.

Note that there is an element
g_{a}\in\mathrm{Isom}({\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))})
given by

g_{a}\colon(r,s)\mapsto(r+a,s),\ (r,s)\in\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))={\mathbb{R}}\times S_{0},\ a\in{\mathbb{R}},

and recall that an isometry satisfying

d(gp,p)=d(gq,q)

for any
p,q\in Y
is called a
Clifford tranlation
;
g_{a}
is a
Clifford translation.
Let
H\subset\mathrm{Isom}({\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))})
be the set of all Clifford
translation on
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
.
Then, by

[
5
, p. 235 6.15 Theorem (3), (4), (5), (6)]
,

H
is an abelian group and it can be identified with a Hilbert space,
and
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
splits as
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))=H\times Z
;
each fiber isometric to
H
is given as the
H
-orbit
Hx
of

x\in\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
.
Since we have
g_{a}
,
H
is a non-trivial Hilbert space.
Note that there is no Clifford translation in
\mathrm{Isom}({Z})
, and hence
Z

does not admit any splitting
Z={\mathbb{R}}\times Z^{\prime}
.

According to the proof of

[
5
, p. 235, 6.15 Theorem (6)]
,
for any
x\in\mathrm{CH}((f(\Gamma)))
and
g\in\mathrm{Isom}({\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))})
,

g
maps
Hx
to
Hgx
.
Setting
x=(h,y)\in H\times Z
and
y^{\prime}=\mathrm{proj}_{Z}(gx)
, we see that

g(H\times\{y\})=H\times\{y^{\prime}\}
.
Thus, by
[
5
, p. 56, 5.3 Proposition (4)]
,
we see that
g
splits as
g=g_{1}\times g_{2}
, where

g_{1}\in\mathrm{Isom}({H})
and
g_{2}\in\mathrm{Isom}({Z})
.
Then it is easy to see that, along this splitting,

\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({f(\Gamma)})
splits
as
\rho=\rho_{1}\times\rho_{2}
, where

\rho_{1}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({H})
and

\rho_{2}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Z})
.

Note that, along the splitting
\rho=\rho_{1}\times\rho_{2}
, a

\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map
f
also splits as

f=f_{1}\times f_{2}
; setting
f(e)=(f_{1}(e),f_{2}(e))
and

f_{i}(\gamma)=\rho_{i}(\gamma)f_{i}(e)
,
i=1,2
, we see that

f(\gamma)=(f_{1}(\gamma),f_{2}(\gamma))
.
In particular, each
f_{i}

is a
\rho_{i}
-equivariant map for
i=1,2
.
If there is a
\rho_{i}
-equivariant map
f^{\prime}_{i}
with strictly smaller

\mu
-energy than
f_{i}
, then replacing
f_{i}
by
f_{i}^{\prime}
and taking

f^{\prime}=(f_{1}^{\prime},f_{2}^{\prime})
decreases the energy of
f
strictly.
This contradicts the fact that
f
is
\mu
-harmonic.
Hence
f_{i}
is a
\rho_{i}
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map.
Since the projection onto
Z
does not increase the distance,

l_{\rho_{2}}(\Gamma)=0
follows from the assumption

l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=0
.
∎

Now we finish the proof of Theorem
A
for proper
Y
.

Proof of Theorem
A
.

Under our assumption, by Proposition
3.1
,
there exists
\omega\in\Omega
such that
a subsequence
\{f(\gamma_{j})\}
of
\{f(\gamma_{n}(\omega)^{-1})\}

converges to
\xi(\omega)\in\partial Y
, and

\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}d(\xi(\omega),f(\gamma))-d(\xi(\omega),f(e))&\text{if }\xi(\omega)\in Y\\
b_{\xi(\omega)}(f(\gamma),f(e))&\text{if }\xi(\omega)\in\partial Y\end{cases}

satisfies
\Delta\varphi_{\omega}(e)=0
.
If
\xi(\omega)\in Y
, then Lemma
5.1

(1)
tells us that

\rho(\Gamma)
fixes a point in
Y
.

Suppose
\xi(\omega)\in\partial Y
.
Since
Y
is proper, taking further subsequence if necessary, we may
assume that
\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi(\omega)
and
\{f(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\}

converge to points
\xi^{+},\xi^{-}\in Y\cup\partial Y

respectively.
Since
d(f(e),f(\gamma^{-1}))=d(f(\gamma),f(e))\to\infty
,

\xi^{-}\in\partial Y
as well as
\xi^{+}
.
Then by Lemma
5.1

(4)
, we see that we have
a splitting
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))=H\times Z
,

\rho_{2}
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map

f_{2}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Z
and
l_{\rho_{2}}(\Gamma)=0
.
Note that
H={\mathbb{R}}^{k}
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and that
Z
is proper, since

Y
, and hence
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
, is proper.
Therefore, by Proposition
3.1
, we have

\xi\in Z\cup\partial Z
such that

\varphi(\gamma)=\begin{cases}d(\xi,f(\gamma))-d(\xi,f(e))&\text{if }\xi\in Z\\
b_{\xi}(f(\gamma),f(e))&\text{if }\xi\in\partial Z\end{cases}

satisfies
\Delta\varphi(e)=0
again.
If
\xi\in\partial Z
, then Lemma
5.1

(4)
tells us that

Z
must admit further splitting
Z={\mathbb{R}}^{k^{\prime}}\times Z^{\prime}
.
However, by our choice of
Z
in Lemma
5.1

(4)
, this is
impossible.
Thus
\xi
must lie in
Z
, and Lemma
5.1

(1)
implies that
there exists a point
p\in Z
fixed by
\rho_{2}(\Gamma)
.
Then we see that
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times\{p\}
is left invariant under the action
of
\rho(\Gamma)=\rho_{1}(\Gamma)\times\rho_{2}(\Gamma)
, which means the
existence of
\rho(\Gamma)
-invariant flat subspace.
This completes the proof of Theorem
A
for proper
Y
.
∎

6.
Proofs of
Theorem
A
for nonproper
\mathrm{CAT}(0)

spaces and Thorem
C

In what follows, we consider the case that
Y
is not proper.
Before proceed, recall that we have used the properness of
Y
to
deduce:

•

\mu
-harmonic map exists when
\rho(\Gamma)
does not fixes a
point in
\partial Y
,

•

\{f(\gamma_{j}(\omega)^{-1})\}
has a convergent subsequence

\{f(\gamma_{j})\}
,

•

\{f(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\}
has a convergent subsequence

\{f(\gamma_{j}^{\prime})\}
,

•

\{\rho(\gamma_{j}^{\prime})\xi(\omega)\}
has a convergent subsequence.

We have to make a detour or find a substitute for these arguments.
It is certainly possible when
Y
is of finite telescopic dimension, and
almost possible when
Y
is locally finite-dimensional as we will see in
§
6
and §
7
.

6.1.
A quick review of ultralimits

In what follows, we will frequently use the ultralimit of a sequence of
metric spaces. We start with a quick review of ultralimits.

Let
\lambda
be a nonprincipal ultrafilter on
{\mathbb{N}}
, that is, a finitely
additive probablity measure on
{\mathbb{N}}
satisfying

•

every
S\subset{\mathbb{N}}
is
\lambda
-measurable,

•

for any
S\subset{\mathbb{N}}
we have
\lambda(S)\in\{0,1\}
,

•

if
\#S<\infty
, then
\lambda(S)=0
.

See, for example,
[
5
, 5.48 Exercise]
for the
existence of a nonpricipal ultrafilter.
For any bounded sequence
\{a_{j}\}
of real numbers, there exists

l\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\lambda(\{j\in{\mathbb{N}}\mid|a_{j}-l|<\varepsilon\})=1
, which we express
as
l=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}a_{j}
.

Let
Y_{j}
be a metric space, and
p_{j}\in Y_{j}
,
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Let
Y^{\infty}
be a set of sequences
\{x_{j}\}
,
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that

d_{Y_{j}}(p_{j},x_{j})
is bounded independent of
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
We set
\{x_{j}\}\sim\{y_{j}\}
if
\{x_{j}\}
and
\{y_{j}\}
satisfy

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d(x_{j},y_{j})=0
. Then
\sim
becomes an equivalence relation on

Y^{\infty}
, and we denote the set of equivalence classes by

Y_{\infty}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}(Y_{j},p_{j})
.
We also denote
x=\{x_{j}\}\in Y_{\infty}
by
x=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}x_{j}
.
It is obvious that
d_{\lambda}(\{x_{j}\},\{y_{j}\})=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d(x_{j},y_{j})

becomes a metric on
Y_{\infty}
.
Let
q_{j}
be a point in
Y_{j}
whose distance from
p_{j}
is bounded
independently of
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
c_{j}\colon[0,d_{j}]\rightarrow Y_{j}
a
geodesic with
c_{j}(0)=q_{j}
.
Then, for each
t\in[0,\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d_{j}]
,
d(c_{j}(t),p_{j})
is
bounded independently of
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and hence we get

c_{\infty}(t)=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}c_{j}(t)
.
Since
d(c_{j}(t),c_{j}(t^{\prime}))=|t-t^{\prime}|
for any
j\in N
, we see that

c_{\infty}\colon[0,\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d_{j}]\rightarrow Y_{\infty}
turns out to
be a geodesic.
Then it is easy to see that if each
Y_{j}
is a complete
\mathrm{CAT}(0)

space, then
Y_{\infty}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}(Y_{j},p_{j})
becomes a complete
\mathrm{CAT}(0)

space.
Note that, even when
d_{j}\nearrow\infty
, we can define a geodesic ray

c_{\infty}\colon[0,\infty)\rightarrow Y_{\infty}
as an
ultralimit of a sequence of geodesics

\{c_{j}\colon[0,d_{j}]\rightarrow Y_{j}\}
.
In this case, we have a point

\xi=c_{\infty}(\infty)\in\partial Y_{\infty}
.
We often express this
\xi
as
\xi=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}c_{j}(d_{j})
.

In what follows, we mainly consider an ultralimit of the form

Y_{\infty}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim(Y,o)
for fixed
Y=(Y,d)
and
o\in Y
, and
we use the same symbol
d
for the metric on
Y_{\infty}
instead of

d_{\lambda}
for the sake of simplicity.
It can be shown that if
Y=(Y,d)
is proper, then

Y_{\infty}=(Y_{\infty},d)
is isometric to
Y=(Y,d)
.
However, this is no longer true for nonproper metric space
Y
.
In that case,
Y
becomes a convex closed proper subset of

Y_{\infty}
.

Example 6.1
.

Let
\lambda
be a nonprincipal ultrafilter.
Consider a metric space
Y
obtained by attaching a half line
L_{n}
to

1/n\in[0,\infty)
for
n=1,2,\dots
.
Then
Y
becomes a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space.
Let
t>0
and take a point
p_{n}^{t}\in L_{n}
so that

d(p_{n}^{t},[0,\infty))=t
.
Choose a basepoint

o
to be the endpoint
0
of
[0,\infty)
to which we
attach
L_{n}
’s, and consider an ultralimit

Y_{\infty}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{n}(Y,o)
.
Then there must be a point
p^{t}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{n}p_{n}^{t}
in
Y_{\infty}
.
However, since, for any
q\in Y
,
d(p_{n}^{t},q)\geq t
holds unless

q\in L_{n}
, we see that

\lambda(\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}\mid d(p_{n}^{t},q)\geq t\})=1
, and hence
p^{t}

cannot be a point in
Y
.
It is clear that, for each
t>0
,
d(p^{t},o)
must be equal to

t
, and, for
t\not=t^{\prime}
,
d(p^{t},p^{t^{\prime}})=|t-t^{\prime}|
must hold.
Therefore
\{p^{t}\mid t\in[0,\infty)\}
is a half line
L

attached to
o
, the endpoint of
[0,\infty)
, and
L

does not exist in
Y
.
Thus we see
Y\not=Y_{\infty}
.
Take an ultralimit one more time and consider

(Y_{\infty})_{\infty}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{n}(Y_{\infty},o)
.
Looking at
\{p_{n}^{t}\}
again, we see that, since

d(p_{n}^{t},p^{t})\geq 2t
for any
n
, an ultralimit of this sequence
cannot be
p^{t}
.
Indeed, we see that
\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{n}p_{n}^{t}
cannot be any point in
Y_{\infty}

as before, and there must be another half line
L^{\prime}
containing

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{n}p_{n}^{t}
and attached to
o
, which does not
exist in
Y_{\infty}
.
Therefore we get
(Y_{\infty})_{\infty}\not=Y_{\infty}
again.
Repeating this, we get larger and larger metric spaces.
This observation leads us to an argument often used in the proof of
our theorems in what follows.

Remark 6.2
.

Although
Y_{\infty}
can be larger than
Y
as the example above
shows,
Y_{\infty}
inherits a certain dimension bound as we will see
in Proposition
6.10
,
which plays an important role in the proof of Theorem

A
.

Let
\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
be a homomorphism.
For
p=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}p_{j}\in Y_{\infty}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim(Y,o)
, we see that

d(\rho(\gamma)p_{j},o)\leq d(\rho(\gamma)p_{j},\rho(\gamma)o)+d(\rho(\gamma)o,o)=d(p_{j},o)+d(\rho(\gamma)o,o)

is bounded independent of
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and hence we have

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\rho(\gamma)p_{j}\in Y_{\infty}
.
Therefore, the action of
\Gamma
on
Y
given by

\rho
can be extended to that on
Y_{\infty}
by setting

\rho(\gamma)(\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}p_{j})=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\rho(\gamma)p_{j}
.
It is clear that this gives a homomorphism from
\Gamma
into

\mathrm{Isom}({Y_{\infty}})
, which we denote by the same symbol
\rho
.

Also, as one easily imagine,
\mu
-energy
E_{\mu}
behaves well under
taking ultralimit.
Although the proof is standard for experts, we give a proof of the
following proposition for the sake of completeness.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space,
o\in Y
, and

\lambda
a nonprincipal ultrafilter.
Suppose that a countable group
\Gamma
acts on
Y
via a
homomorphism
\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
, and that the
action given by
\rho
has finite second moment with respect to
\mu
.
Let
f_{q}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
be a
\rho
-equivariant map
defined by
f_{q}(\gamma)=\rho(\gamma)q
, and

Y_{\infty}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim(Y,o)
.
Suppose that
p=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}p_{j}
for a bounded sequence

\{p_{j}\}\subset Y
. Then we have

E_{\mu}(f_{p})=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}E_{\mu}(f_{p_{j}}).

Proof.

Since

\begin{split}d(p_{j},\rho(\gamma)p_{j})&\leq d(o,p_{j})+d(o,\rho(\gamma)o)+d(\rho(\gamma)o,\rho(\gamma)p_{j})\\
&=2d(o,p_{j})+d(o,\rho(\gamma)o),\end{split}

and
\{p_{j}\}
is bounded, we have

d(p_{j},\rho(\gamma)p_{j})^{2}\leq C_{1}d(o,\rho(\gamma)o)^{2}+C_{2}

for some positive constant
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
.
Since
\Gamma
is countable, we can take an increasing sequence

D_{1}\subset D_{2}\subset\dots\subset D_{n}\subset\dots
of finite
subsets of
\Gamma
so that
\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}D_{n}=\mathrm{supp}\,\mu
.
Then, by the inequality above, we have

\int_{\Gamma\setminus D_{n}}d(p_{j},\rho(\gamma)p_{j})^{2}d\mu(\gamma)\leq\int_{\Gamma\setminus D_{n}}C_{1}d(o,\rho(\gamma)o)^{2}+C_{2}\ d\mu(\gamma)

for any
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and also

\int_{\Gamma\setminus D_{n}}d(p,\rho(\gamma)p)^{2}d\mu(\gamma)\leq\int_{\Gamma\setminus D_{n}}C_{1}d(o,\rho(\gamma)o)^{2}+C_{2}\ d\mu(\gamma).

Note that the right-hand side tends to
0
as

n\to\infty
since the action given by
\rho
has finite second
moment with respect to
\mu
.
Therefore, for any
\varepsilon>0
,
there exists
n
such that

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma\setminus D_{n}}d(p_{j},\rho(\gamma)p_{j})^{2}d\mu(\gamma)<\frac{\varepsilon}{4}

for any
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma\setminus D_{n}}d(p,\rho(\gamma)p)^{2}d\mu(\gamma)<\frac{\varepsilon}{4}

hold.
On the other hand, since

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d(p_{j},\rho(\gamma)p_{j})^{2}=d(p,\rho(\gamma)p)^{2}
and
\lambda
is
a nonprincipal ultrafilter, letting

A_{\gamma}=\left\{k\in{\mathbb{N}}\mid|d(p_{k},\rho(\gamma)p_{k})^{2}-d(p,\rho(\gamma)p)^{2}|<\varepsilon\right\},

we have
\lambda(A_{\gamma})=1
for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
Since
D_{n}
is a finite set, we obtain

\lambda(\bigcap_{\gamma\in D_{n}}A_{\gamma})=1
.
Note that, for any
j\in\bigcap_{\gamma\in D_{n}}A_{\gamma}
,

\left|\frac{1}{2}\int_{D_{n}}d(p_{j},\rho(\gamma)p_{j})^{2}d\mu(\gamma)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{D_{n}}d(p,\rho(\gamma)p)^{2}d\mu(\gamma)\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}

holds. Therefore, for any
\varepsilon>0
, by taking
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
as
above, we see that

\begin{split}\left|E_{\mu}(f_{p_{j}})-E_{\mu}(f_{p})\right|&=\frac{1}{2}\left|\int_{\Gamma\setminus D_{n}}d(p_{j},\rho(\gamma)p_{j})^{2}d\mu(\gamma)+\int_{D_{n}}d(p_{j},\rho(\gamma)p_{j})^{2}d\mu(\gamma)\right.\\
&\phantom{==}\left.-\int_{\Gamma\setminus D_{n}}d(p,\rho(\gamma)p)^{2}d\mu(\gamma)-\int_{D_{n}}d(p,\rho(\gamma)p)^{2}d\mu(\gamma)\right|\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma\setminus D_{n}}d(p_{j},\rho(\gamma)p_{j})^{2}d\mu(\gamma)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma\setminus D_{n}}d(p,\rho(\gamma)p)^{2}d\mu(\gamma)\\
&\phantom{==}+\frac{1}{2}\left|\int_{D_{n}}d(p_{j},\rho(\gamma)p_{j})^{2}d\mu(\gamma)-\int_{D_{n}}d(p,\rho(\gamma)p)^{2}d\mu(\gamma)\right|\\
&<\frac{\varepsilon}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}=\varepsilon\end{split}

holds for any
j\in\bigcap_{\gamma\in D_{n}}A_{\gamma}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Recall that
\rho(\Gamma)\subset\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
is said to be

reductive in the sense of Jost

if there exists a closed, convex
\rho(\Gamma)
-invariant subset
C
of

Y
satisfying either

•

C
is isometric to a finite-dimensional Euclidean space, or

•

for any unbounded sequence
\{p_{n}\}\subset C
, there exists

\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\{d(p_{n},\rho(\gamma)p_{n})\}
is
unbounded.

If
C
is isometric to a finite-dimensional Euclidean space, then we can
regard
C
as
Y
.
The existence of
\rho
-equivariant harmonic map in this case follows
from, for example, an argument similar to that in
[
32
]
.
Suppose that we have the second case in the definition of reductiveness
in the sense of Jost.
Take any sequence
\{f_{p_{j}}\}
that minimizes
\mu
-energy.
Suppose that
\{p_{j}\}
is unbounded.
Then there exists
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that

\{d(p_{j},\rho(\gamma)p_{j})\}
is unbounded.
Since
\mathrm{supp}\,\mu
generates
\Gamma
, there exists

s_{1},\dots,s_{k}\in\mathrm{supp}\,\mu
such that
\gamma=s_{1}\dots s_{k}
.
Since

d(p_{j},\rho(\gamma)p_{j})\leq d(p_{j},\rho(s_{1})p_{j})+\dots+d(p_{j},\rho(s_{k})p_{j})

as we have seen in the proof of Lemma
2.9
,

\{d(p,\rho(s_{j})p)\}
is unbounded for some
j
, and we conclude that

E_{\mu}(f_{p_{j}})
is unbounded.
A contradiction.
Now since
\{p_{j}\}
is bounded, by the proposition above, we see that

f_{p}
for
p=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim p_{j}
minimizes
\mu
-energy, and hence it is
a
\mu
-harmonic map into
Y_{\infty}
.
Note that
Y
is a convex closed subset in
Y_{\infty}
.
Then the nearest point projection
\mathrm{proj}_{Y}
onto
Y
is

\rho
-equivariant, and does not increase
the distance, and hence the energy.
Therefore
\mathrm{proj}\circ f_{p}
is also

\mu
-harmonic; we may assume
f_{p}
is a map into
Y
.
Summarizing this gives the following.

Corollary 6.4
.

If
\rho(\Gamma)
is
reductive in the sense of Jost, then there is a
\rho
-equivariant
harmonic map
f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
.

Remark 6.5
.

The corollary above also follows from

[
31
, Proposition 1.2]
without using ultralimits.

The following lemma tells us that Busemann functions also behave
well under taking ultralimits.

Lemma 6.6
.

Take
o\in Y
.
Suppose that a sequence
\{x_{j}\}\subset Y
satisfies

d(o,x_{j})\to\infty
.
Then we have
\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}x_{j}=\xi\in\partial Y_{\infty}
, where

Y_{\infty}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim(Y,o)
.
Furthermore, we have, for any
z\in Y
,

b_{\xi}(z,o)=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim\left(d(x_{j},z)-d(x_{j},o)\right).

Proof.

Take
z\in Y
.
Consider a geodesic ray
c
with
c(0)=o
.
Applying
[
5
, p. 269, 8.21 Lemma]
by setting

\rho=d(o,z)
,
we see that, for any
\varepsilon/3>0
, there exists
r>0
such that

d(c(r),z)-d(c(t),z)+(t-r)<\varepsilon/3

holds for any geodesic
c
starting from
o
and
t\geq r
.
This inequality can be rewritten as

\left(d(c(r),z)-r\right)-\left(d(c(t),z)-t\right)<\varepsilon/3.

Since
t\geq r
is arbitrary and
d(c(t),z)-t
is easily seen to be
nonincreasing by the triangle inequality
(
[
5
, p. 268, 8.18 Lemma (1)]
),
we see that

|\left(d(c(r),z)-r\right)-b_{c(\infty)}(z,o)|\leq\varepsilon/3

for any geodesic ray
c
starting from
o
.
Let
c_{j}
be a geodesic joining
o
and
x_{j}
with
c_{j}(d_{j})=x_{j}
, and
set

D(r)=\{j\in{\mathbb{N}}\mid d_{j}\geq r\}.

By our assumption,
d_{j}\to\infty
, and hence
\lambda(D(r))=1
.
Note that, as we have seen before, we have an ultralimit

c_{\infty}\colon[0,\infty)\rightarrow Y_{\infty}
of
\{c_{j}\}
,
and
\xi=c_{\infty}(\infty)=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}x_{j}\in\partial Y_{\infty}
.
Set

C(r,\varepsilon)=\{j\in{\mathbb{N}}\mid|d(c_{j}(r),z)-d(c_{\infty}(r),z)|<\varepsilon/3\}.

Then
\lambda(C(r,\varepsilon))=1
for any
r
and
\varepsilon
.
Thus
\lambda(D(r)\cap C(r,\varepsilon))=1
,
and for any
j\in D(r)\cap C(r,\varepsilon)
,

\begin{split}&|d(c_{j}(d_{j}),z)-d_{j}-b_{\xi}(z,o)|\\
\leq&|\left(d(c_{j}(d_{j}),z)-d_{j}\right)-\left(d(c_{j}(r),z)-r\right)|\\
&+|\left(d(c_{j}(r),z)-r\right)-\left(d(c_{\infty}(r),z)-r\right)|\\
&+|\left(d(c_{\infty}(r),z)-r\right)-b_{\xi}(z,o)|\\
<&\varepsilon/3+|d(c_{j}(r),z)-d(c_{\infty}(r),z)|+\varepsilon/3<\varepsilon\end{split}

holds. Since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, we get

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim\left(d(c_{j}(d_{j}),z)-d_{j}\right)=b_{\xi}(z,o),

and this completes the proof.
∎

6.2.
Existence of a
\mu
-harmonic function

We have seen that there is a function
\varphi_{\omega}
which
satisfies
\Delta\varphi_{\omega}(e)=0
in
§
3
when
Y
is proper.
In this section, we show that, even when
Y
is not proper,
there does exist a function which is
\mu
-harmonic on whole
\Gamma

under the assumption that
\mu(\gamma)\not=0
for any

\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
.
We will use this fact in §
7
.

Suppose that
\Gamma
acts on
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
via a homomorphism

\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
and that the action given by

\rho
has finite second moment.
Fix
o\in Y
.
Let
\varphi\colon\Gamma\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a function satisfying

\varphi(\gamma)\leq C_{1}d(o,\rho(\gamma)o)^{2}+C_{2}
for some

C_{1},C_{2}>0
.
Since the action give by
\rho
has finite second moment with respect
to
\mu
, it is also true for
\mu^{2}
as we have seen
in §
3
.
Noting that
d(p,q)\leq 1+d(p,q)^{2}
holds for any
p,q\in Y
, we see
that

\begin{split}|\varphi(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})|&\leq C_{1}d(o,\rho(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})o)^{2}+C_{2}\\
&\leq C_{1}\left(d(o,\rho(\gamma)o)+d(\rho(\gamma)o,\rho(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})o)\right)^{2}+C_{2}\\
&\leq C_{1}^{\prime}d(o,\rho(\gamma^{\prime})o)^{2}+C_{2}^{\prime}\end{split}

for some positive constant
C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime}
which depend on
\gamma
.
Thus, fixing
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
we see that
\gamma^{\prime}\mapsto\varphi(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})
is

\mu^{2}
-integrable by our assumption.
In the rest of this section, a function
\varphi
under consideration
is always assumed to satisfy

|\varphi(\gamma)|\leq C_{1}d(o,\rho(\gamma)o)^{2}+C_{2}
for some

C_{1},C_{2}>0
.

Let us denote by
\Delta_{2}
the
\mu^{2}
-Laplacian, which is defined by

\Delta_{2}\varphi(\gamma)=\varphi(\gamma)-\int_{\Gamma}\varphi(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})d\mu^{2}(\gamma^{\prime}).

Then we have

\begin{split}-\Delta_{2}\varphi(\gamma)&=\int_{\Gamma}\varphi(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})-\varphi(\gamma)d\mu^{2}(\gamma^{\prime})\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}\left(\int_{\Gamma}\varphi(\gamma\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})-\varphi(\gamma)d\mu(\gamma_{2})\right)d\mu(\gamma_{1})\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}\left(\int_{\Gamma}\left(\varphi(\gamma\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})-\varphi(\gamma\gamma_{1})\right)+\varphi(\gamma\gamma_{1})-\varphi(\gamma)d\mu(\gamma_{2})\right)d\mu(\gamma_{1})\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}\left(-\Delta\varphi(\gamma\gamma_{1})+\varphi(\gamma\gamma_{1})-\varphi(\gamma)\right)d\mu(\gamma_{1})\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}-\Delta\varphi(\gamma\gamma_{1})d\mu(\gamma_{1})-\Delta\varphi(\gamma),\end{split}

and hence we get the following:

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\varphi\colon\Gamma\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a
\mu
-subharmonic
function.

(1)

\varphi
is
\mu^{2}
-subharmonic
;
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

-\Delta_{2}\varphi(\gamma)\geq 0
holds.

(2)

-\Delta_{2}\varphi(\gamma)\geq-\Delta\varphi(\gamma)\geq 0

holds.

(3)
If
-\Delta_{2}\varphi(\gamma)=0
holds for
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
then
-\Delta\varphi(\gamma)=0
holds.

Proposition 6.8
.

Suppose that
\mu(\gamma)\not=0
holds for any

\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
.
Let
\varphi\colon\Gamma\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a
\mu
-subharmonic
function.
If
-\Delta_{2}\varphi(e)=0
holds, then
\varphi
is a
\mu
-harmonic
function, that is,
-\Delta\varphi(\gamma)=0
holds for any

\gamma\in\Gamma
.

Proof.

By the computation above and
-\Delta_{2}\varphi(e)=0
, we get

0=-\Delta_{2}\varphi(e)=-\Delta\varphi(e)+\int_{\Gamma}-\Delta\varphi(\gamma)d\mu(\gamma).

On the other hand, since
\varphi
is
\mu
-subharmonic, we have

-\Delta\varphi(\gamma)\geq 0
.
Therefore, by our assumption that
\mu(\gamma)\not=0
for any

\gamma\in\Gamma
, we see that
-\Delta\varphi(\gamma)=0
for any

\gamma\in\Gamma
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Now take a
\mu
-harmonic map
f
and let

\varphi(\gamma)=d(f(e),f(\gamma))
.
Then
\varphi
is a
\mu
-subharmonic function by
Proposition
2.17
.
Set

\alpha_{2,n}:=\int_{\Omega}-\Delta_{2}\varphi(\gamma_{2n}(\omega))\ d\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\int_{\Gamma}-\Delta_{2}\varphi(\gamma)d\mu^{2n}(\gamma).

Then, by the same computation as in
§
3
, we see that

L^{2(n+1)}(f)=L^{2n}(f)+\alpha_{2,n}

holds.
Thus if
l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=0
, then we have

\liminf_{n}\alpha_{2,n}=0
, and by Lebesgue’s convergence theorem and

-\Delta_{2}\varphi(\gamma)\geq 0
which follows from
Lemma
6.7

(1)
, we get

(6.1)

\liminf_{n}-\Delta_{2}\varphi(\gamma_{2n}(\omega))=0\quad\text{a.e.}

Set

A=\{\omega\in\Omega\mid\ \liminf_{n}-\Delta_{2}\varphi(\gamma_{2n}(\omega))=0\},

then we see that
\mathbb{P}(A)=1
.
Take
\omega\in A
.
Then, for any
j
, we can find
n_{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

-\Delta_{2}\varphi(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega))\leq 1/j.

By Lemma
6.7

(2)
, we also have

-\Delta\varphi(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega))\leq 1/j.

Letting
j\to\infty
, we see that

(6.2)

\lim_{j\to\infty}-\Delta_{k}\varphi(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega))=0

for
k=1,2
. Let us define

\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}(\gamma)=d(f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{-1}),f(\gamma))-d(f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{-1}),f(e)).

Note that, by the triangle inequality,
\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}
satisfies

|\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}(\gamma)|\leq d(f(e),f(\gamma))
for each

j\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
Thus
\{\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}\}
can be viewed as a sequence in the
product space

\prod_{\gamma\in\Gamma}[-d(f(e),f(\gamma)),d(f(e),f(\gamma))]
,
which is sequentially compact.
Since the topology of this product space agrees with that given
by the pointwise convergence of real valued functions on
\Gamma
,
by taking a subsequence if necessary, we may
assume that
\{\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}\}
converges pointwise to

\varphi_{\omega}
.
Since, by fixing
j
, the second term in the right-hand side of the
definition of
\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}
is independent of

\gamma\in\Gamma
, we have

\begin{split}\Delta_{2}\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}(\gamma)&=d(f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{-1}),f(\gamma))-\int_{\Gamma}d(f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{-1}),f(\gamma\gamma^{\prime}))d\mu^{2}(\gamma^{\prime})\\
&=d(f(e),f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)\gamma))-\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)\gamma\gamma^{\prime}))d\mu^{2}(\gamma^{\prime})\\
&=\Delta_{2}\varphi(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)\gamma),\end{split}

in particular, we get

\Delta_{2}\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}(e)=\Delta_{2}\varphi(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)).

Since

|\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}(\gamma)|=|d(f(\gamma_{j}),f(\gamma))-d(f(\gamma_{j}),f(e))|\leq d(f(e),f(\gamma)),

where
\gamma_{j}=\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{-1}
,
and
\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma))d\mu^{2}(\gamma)<\infty
,
we see that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\Gamma}\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}(\gamma)d\mu^{2}(\gamma)=\int_{\Gamma}\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma)d\mu^{2}(\gamma)

by Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem.
Combining with (
6.2
), we get

\Delta_{2}\varphi_{\omega}(e)=\lim_{j}\Delta_{2}\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}(e)=0.

Then, by Proposition
6.8
, we see
that
\Delta\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma)=0
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
Now let us denote
\xi(\omega)=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}f(\gamma_{j})
.
If
\xi(\omega)\in Y_{\infty}
, then, since
\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}

converges pointwise to
\varphi_{\omega}
while

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\varphi_{\omega,n_{j}}(\gamma)=d(\xi(\omega),f(\gamma))-d(\xi(\omega),f(e))
,
we see that

\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma)=d(\xi(\omega),f(\gamma))-d(\xi(\omega),f(e))
.
On the other hand, if
\xi(\omega)\in\partial Y_{\infty}
, then
Lemma
6.6
tells us that

\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma)=b_{\xi(\omega)}(f(\gamma),f(e))
.
Thus we get a
\mu
-harmonic function of the form

\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}d(f(\gamma),\xi(\omega))-d(f(e),\xi(\omega))&\text{if }\xi(\omega)\in Y_{\infty},\\
b_{\xi(\omega)}(f(\gamma),f(e))&\text{if }\xi(\omega)\in\partial Y_{\infty}.\end{cases}

What we have shown is the following generalization of
Proposition
3.1
.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
Y=(Y,d)
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space.
Take
o\in Y
and set
Y_{\infty}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim(Y,o)
.
Let
\Gamma
be a countable group equipped with a symmetric
probability measure
\mu
whose support generates
\Gamma
.
Suppose that
\Gamma
acts on
Y
via a homomorphism

\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
and that
there exists a
\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map

f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
.
If
l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=0
, then, for almost all
\omega\in\Omega
,
there exists

\xi(\omega)\in Y_{\infty}\cup\partial Y_{\infty}
such that a
function

\varphi_{\omega}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
defined by

\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}d(\xi(\omega),f(\gamma))-d(\xi(\omega),f(e))&\text{if }\xi(\omega)\in Y_{\infty}\\
b_{\xi(\omega)}(f(\gamma),f(e))&\text{if }\xi(\omega)\in\partial Y_{\infty}\end{cases}

is
\mu
-harmonic
;
the function
\varphi_{\omega}
satisfies

\Delta\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma)=0
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

6.3.
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces with certain dimension bounds

Let us denote the metric ball of radius
r
centered at
p
by

B(p,r)
.
The
radius

\mathrm{rad}_{Y}({B})
of
B\subset Y
is defined to be the
infimum of
r
such that
B\subset B(p,r)
for some
p\in Y
.
It is known that, for any bounded subset
B
of a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
,
there exists a unique point
p_{0}
such that

B\subset B(p_{0},\mathrm{rad}_{Y}({B}))

(see
[
5
, p. 179, 2.7 Proposition]
).
Such a point
p_{0}
is called the
circumcenter
of
B
.

The
geometric dimension
of a metric space with curvature bounded
from above is defined in an inductive manner in
[
29
]
and
studied there in detail.
For a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
,
according to
[
6
, Theorem 1.3]
,
Y

has geometric dimension at most
n
if and only if any subset
B
of
finite diameter satisfies

(6.3)

\mathrm{rad}_{Y}({B})\leq\sqrt{\frac{n}{2(n+1)}}\mathrm{diam}(B),

where
\mathrm{diam}(B)
denotes the diameter of
B
.
Note that the equality is achieved by a regular Euclidean
n
-simplex.
We say
Y
is
locally finite-dimensional
if, for each
R>0
and

p\in Y
, there exists
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
B(p,R)\subset Y
has
geometric dimension at most
n
. (Note that a convex subset of a

\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space is again a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space, and that it inherits the
upper bound on the geometric dimension from the ambient

\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space. Thus the choice of a point
p
is irrelevant
here.)
A
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
is said to have
telescopic dimension
at most

n
if every asymptotic cone of
Y
has geometric dimension at most

n
.
For example, a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
has telescopic
dimension at most
1

if and only if
Y
is hyperbolic in the sense of Gromov
(
[
6
]
).
Again
[
6
, Theorem 1.3]
tells us that
Y
has telescopic
dimension at most
n
if and only if, for any
\delta>0
, there exists

D>0
such that every bounded subset
B
with
\mathrm{diam}(B)>D

satisfies

(6.4)

\mathrm{rad}_{Y}({B})\leq\left(\delta+\sqrt{\frac{n}{2(n+1)}}\right)\mathrm{diam}(B).

The first part of the following proposition has already been pointed out
in
[
7
, Lemma A.2]
; P.-E. Caprace and A. Lytchack used the
term
ultracompletion
for the ultralmit of a constant sequence of
pointed metric space
(Y,p_{0})
there.
We include, however, the proof of the result here for the sake of
completeness.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space and
o\in Y
. Denote by

Y_{\infty}
an ultralimit
\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim(Y,o)
with respect to a
nonprincipal ultrafilter
\lambda
.

(1)
If
Y
has telescopic dimension at most
n
, so does

Y_{\infty}
.

(2)
Suppose that, for any
R>0
, there exsits
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
any
B\subset B(o,R)
has geometric dimensin at most
n
.
Then
Y_{\infty}
has the same property.

Proof.

(1)
Assume that
Y
has telescopic dimension at most
n
.
We first show that, for any
\delta>0
, there exists
D>0
such that any
finite subset
B\subset Y_{\infty}
with
\mathrm{diam}(B)>D
satisfies
(
6.4
).

Take any finite subset
B=\{p^{(1)},\dots,p^{(k)}\}
in

Y_{\infty}
with
\mathrm{diam}(B)>D
,
and fix an expression
p^{(i)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}p^{(i)}_{j}
with
p^{(i)}_{j}\in Y

for each
p^{(i)}\in B
.
Let
B_{j}=\{p^{(1)}_{j},\dots,p^{(k)}_{j}\}
be the set of
j
th elements
in the expression of each
p^{(i)}\in B
.
Since
B_{j}
is a finite set, there is
1\leq j(0)<j(1)\leq k
such
that
d(p^{(j(0))}_{j},p^{(j(1))}_{j})=\mathrm{diam}(B_{j})
.
For
m=0,1
there exists unique
l_{m}\in\{1,\dots,k\}
such that

\lambda(\{i\in{\mathbb{N}}\mid j(m)=l_{m}\})=1
,
since
\lambda
is an ultrafilter.
Then we see that
\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{i}p^{(j(m))}_{i}=p^{(l_{m})}
and

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim\mathrm{diam}(B_{j})=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim d(p^{(j(0))}_{i},p^{(j(1))}_{i})=d(p^{(l_{0})},p^{(l_{1})})\leq\mathrm{diam}(B).

On the other hand, since, for any

p=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}p_{j},\ p^{\prime}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}p_{j}^{\prime}\in B
,
we have

d(p,p^{\prime})=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d(p_{j},p_{j}^{\prime})\leq\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\mathrm{diam}(B_{j}),

which implies
\mathrm{diam}(B)\leq\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\mathrm{diam}(B_{j})
.
Hence we get
\mathrm{diam}(B)=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\mathrm{diam}(B_{j})
.
In particular, we may assume that
\mathrm{diam}(B_{j})>D
for
\lambda
-almost
all
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
p^{(0)}_{j}
be a circumcenter of
B_{j}
.
Take
R>0
so that
B(o,R)
contains
B
. Then we may assume that

B_{j}\subset B(o,R+1)
, and that
p^{(0)}_{j}
’s lie in
B(o,R+1)
.
Thus we obtain a point

p^{(0)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}p^{(0)}_{j}\in Y_{\infty}
.
Since, for any
p_{j}\in B_{j}
, we have

d(p^{(0)}_{j},p_{j})\leq\mathrm{rad}_{Y}({B_{j}})
, we see that

d(p^{(0)},p)\leq\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\mathrm{rad}_{Y}({B_{j}})
for any

p\in B
, which implies that

\mathrm{rad}_{Y_{\infty}}({B})\leq\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\mathrm{rad}_{Y}({B_{j}})
.
Since each
B_{j}
is a subset of
Y
with
\mathrm{diam}(B_{j})>D
, we
have

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim\mathrm{rad}_{Y}({B_{j}})\leq\left(\delta+\sqrt{\frac{n}{2(n+1)}}\right)\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim\mathrm{diam}(B_{j})

by (
6.4
), hence we get

(6.5)

\mathrm{rad}_{Y_{\infty}}({B})\leq\left(\delta+\sqrt{\frac{n}{2(n+1)}}\right)\mathrm{diam}(B).

Now suppose that
Y_{\infty}
has telescopic
dimension greater than
n
. Then, by
[
29
, Theorem 7.1]
,
there exsits a sequence of pointed subsets
\{(Y_{j},p_{j})\}
of

Y_{\infty}
such that
\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}(Y_{j},p_{j})
contains
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
.
Therefore we can find

S=\{q^{(0)},\dots,q^{(n+1)}\}\subset\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}(Y_{j},p_{j})
that forms a
regular Euclidean
(n+1)
-simplex with arbitrarily large diameter.
Take
D>0
for
\delta
satisfying

(6.6)

0<\delta<\sqrt{\frac{n+1}{2(n+2)}}-\sqrt{\frac{n}{2(n+1)}},

and choose
S
so that
\mathrm{diam}(S)>D
.
Expressing
q^{(i)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}q^{(i)}_{j}

with
q^{(i)}_{j}\in Y_{j}\subset Y_{\infty}
, and setting

S_{j}=\{q^{(0)}_{j},\dots,q^{(n+1)}_{j}\}
, we see that

\mathrm{rad}_{\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}(Y_{j},p_{j})}({S})\leq\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim\mathrm{rad}_{Y_{j}}({S_{j}}),\quad\mathrm{diam}(S)=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\mathrm{diam}(S_{j}),

and we may assume
\mathrm{diam}(S_{j})>D
as before.
Together with (
6.5
) and (
6.6
), we get

\mathrm{rad}_{\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}(Y_{j},p_{j})}({S})\leq\left(\delta+\sqrt{\frac{n}{2(n+1)}}\right)\mathrm{diam}(S)<\sqrt{\frac{n+1}{2(n+2)}}\mathrm{diam}(S).

This contradicts our assumption that
S
is a regular Euclidean

(n+1)
-simplex.
Therefore we conclude that
Y_{\infty}
has telescopic
dimension at most
n
.

(2)
Now assume that
Y
is locally finite-dimensional.
Fix
R>0
and take any finite subset

B=\{p^{(1)},\dots,p^{(k)}\}\subset Y_{\infty}
so
that
B\subset B(o,R)
.
Fix an expression
p^{(i)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}p^{(i)}_{j}
with
p^{(i)}_{j}\in Y
,
and define
B_{j}
as before.
We may assume
p^{(i)}_{j}\in B(o,R+1)\subset Y
, and hence
there exists
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
B_{j}
satisfies (
6.3
)
with
n
. Thus
B
also satisfies (
6.3
) with the same

n
as we have seen above.

Suppose that
B(o,R)\subset Y_{\infty}
has geometric dimension greater
than
n
.
Then, by
[
29
, Theorem A]
, there are sequences

r_{j}\to\infty
,
S_{j}\subset B(o,R)
, and
p\in B(o,R)
such that

d(S_{j},p)\to 0
and
r_{j}S_{j}
converges to the unit ball in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}

in the Gromov-Hausdorff topology.
Then we can find a sequence of
n+2
points in
B(o,R)
which converges
to the vertices of a regular Euclidean
(n+1)
-simplex by scaling them
with
r_{j}
’s.
Then this contradicts the fact that any finite set of

B(o,R)
satisfies (
6.3
). Thus
B(o,R)
has
geometric dimension at most
n
.
∎

If
Y
is proper and
\rho(\Gamma)
does not fix a point in

\partial Y
, then

\rho(\Gamma)
is reductive in the sense of Jost as we have seen
in the end of §
2
,
and hence
\{f_{j}(e)\}
is bounded for any minimizing sequence
\{f_{j}\}
.
In particular, we can find a harmonic map, by passing to a subsequence,
as a limit map of
\{f_{j}\}
(see also Corollary
6.4
).
This is not true in general for nonproper
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space; we cannot
conclude that
\rho(\Gamma)
is
reductive in the sense of Jost,
assuming that
\rho(\Gamma)
does not fix a point in
\partial Y
.
However we can show the following, which is related to

[
7
, Lemma A.7]
.

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
\Gamma
be a countable group equipped with a symmetric
and nondegenerate probability measure
\mu
.
Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space with finite telescopic dimension
and
\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
a homomorphism.
Assume that the action given by
\rho
has finite second
moment with respect to
\mu
.
If
\rho(\Gamma)
does not fix a point in
\partial Y
, then there
exists a
\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map.

Proof.

Denote by
f_{p}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
a
\rho
-equivariant map
with
f(e)=p
.
First note that, since
p\mapsto E_{\mu}(f_{p})
is a continuous convex
function on
Y
as we have seen in §
2
, for any
positive real number
c
, the sublevel set

E_{c}:=\{p\in Y\mid E_{\mu}(f_{p})\leq c\}

is a (possibly empty) closed convex subset of
Y
.
Take a decreasing sequence
\{c_{j}\}
of real numbers so that

\lim_{j}c_{j}=\inf_{p\in Y}E_{\mu}(f_{p})
.
Then we obtain a descending sequence of closed convex subset

\{E_{j}=E_{c_{j}}\}_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.
If
\bigcap_{j}E_{j}\not=\emptyset
, then, for any

q\in\bigcap_{j}E_{j}
,

E_{\mu}(f_{q})=\inf_{p\in Y}E_{\mu}(f_{p})
, and hece
f_{q}
is

\mu
-harmonic.

Suppose that
\bigcap_{j}E_{j}=\emptyset
.
Then, since
Y
has finite telescopic dimension,

[
6
, Lemma 5.5]
tells us that

\bigcap_{j}\partial E_{j}
is a nonempty set whose intrinsic
radius is at most
\pi/2
.
Since
Y
has finite telescopic dimension,
\partial Y
is finite
dimensional
\mathrm{CAT}(1)
space, and hence by

[
2
, Proposition 1.4]
, the set of intrinsic
circumcenters of

\bigcap_{j}\partial E_{j}\subset\partial Y
has a unique
circumcenter
\xi_{0}
.
We will show that
\bigcap_{j}\partial E_{j}
is
\rho(\Gamma)
-invariant
in what follows.
Then the unique circumcenter
\xi_{0}
of

\bigcap_{j}\partial E_{j}
must be fixed by
\rho(\Gamma)
.
And this completes the proof of the proposition.

Take any
\gamma\in\Gamma
and fix it for a while.
Then by our assumption on
\mu
, there exists

s_{1},\dots,s_{k}\in\mathrm{supp}\,\mu
such that
\gamma=s_{1}\dots s_{k}
.
Fix
c_{j}
, and take a point
p\in E_{j}
.
Then, since
E_{\mu}(f_{p})\leq c_{j}
, we see that

d(p,\rho(s_{i})p)\leq\sqrt{\frac{2E_{\mu}(f_{p})}{\mu(s_{i})}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2c_{j}}{\min_{i}\mu(s_{i})}}=:C

for any
p\in E_{j}
.
Therefore we obtain

d(p,\rho(\gamma)p)\leq d(p,\rho(s_{1})p)+\dots+d(p,\rho(s_{k})p)\leq Ck,

and hence we have

d(\rho(\gamma)E_{j},E_{j})\leq Ck
.
This shows that

\rho(\gamma)\partial E_{j}=\partial\rho(\gamma)E_{j}\subset\partial E_{j}
,
and hence

\rho(\gamma)\bigcap_{j}\partial E_{j}=\bigcap_{j}\rho(\gamma)\partial E_{j}\subset\bigcap_{j}\partial E_{j}.

In the same way, we have

\rho(\gamma^{-1})\bigcap_{j}\partial E_{j}\subset\bigcap_{j}\partial E_{j}

and applying
\rho(\gamma)
to both sides of this inclusion gives us

\bigcap_{j}\partial E_{j}\subset\rho(\gamma)\bigcap_{j}\partial E_{j}
.
Thus we obtain

\rho(\gamma)\bigcap_{j}\partial E_{j}=\bigcap_{j}\partial E_{j}
.
Since this is true for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we see that

\bigcap_{j}\partial E_{j}
is left invariant under the action of

\rho(\Gamma)
.
∎

6.4.
Proof of Theorem
A

This subsection is devoted to the proof of Theorem
A
.

Assume that

Y
has finite telescopic dimension and that

\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})

is a homomorphism with
l_{\rho}(\Gamma)=0
.
We may also assume that
\mathrm{supp}\,\mu\supset\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
as
we have explained in the
beginning of
§
4
.
Since we are assuming that
\rho(\Gamma)
fixes no point in

\partial Y
, by Proposition
6.11
, we have a

\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map
f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
.
Then, by Proposition
6.9
, for almost
every
\omega\in\Omega
, there exists a subsequence
\{n_{j}\}

such that
\xi(\omega)=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}f(\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{-1})
gives us
a
\mu
-harmonic function of the form

\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}d(f(\gamma),\xi(\omega))-d(f(e),\xi(\omega))&\text{if }\xi(\omega)\in Y_{\infty},\\
b_{\xi(\omega)}(f(\gamma),f(e))&\text{if }\xi(\omega)\in\partial Y_{\infty}.\end{cases}

Let us denote
\gamma_{n_{j}}(\omega)^{-1}
by
\gamma_{j}
.
If
d(f(e),f(\gamma_{j}))
is bounded, then we have

\xi(\omega)=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim f(\gamma_{j})\in Y_{\infty}
.
While if
d(f(e),f(\gamma_{j}))\to\infty
, then we have

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim f(\gamma_{j})=\xi(\omega)\in\partial Y_{\infty}
.

Assume first that
\xi(\omega)\in Y_{\infty}
.
Then, by Lemma
5.1

(1)
,
we see that extended action of
\rho(\Gamma)
on
Y_{\infty}
has
a fixed point.
Hence every
\rho(\Gamma)
-orbit in
Y_{\infty}
(and hence in
Y
) is
bounded.
Take any bounded orbit in
Y
.
Then the circumcenter of this orbit must be fixed by
\rho(\Gamma)
.
Thus
\rho(\Gamma)
fixes a point in
Y
.

Now assume that
\xi(\omega)\in\partial Y_{\infty}
.
Under our assuption that
Y

is of finite telescopic dimension, we can
show that sequences
\{f(\gamma_{j})\}
,
\{f(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\}
and

\{\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi(\omega)\}
actually converges in the cone
topology by passing to subsequences.
Indeed, we will show the following theorem, which claims a certain
compactness property of
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces of finite telescopic dimension
or of locally finite dimension.

Theorem 6.12
.

Let
Y
be a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space either of finite telescopic dimension
or locally finite-dimensional, and
\Gamma
a countable
group with a symmetric probability measure
\mu

with
\mathrm{supp}\,\mu\supset\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
.
Let
\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
be a homomorphism, and
suppose that there exists a
\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map

f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
.
Let
\{\gamma_{j}\}\subset\Gamma
satisfy

d(f(e),f(\gamma_{j}))\to\infty
, and
\xi=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}f(\gamma_{j})
.
If a function
\gamma\mapsto b_{\xi}(f(\gamma),f(e))
is

\mu
-harmonic, then
\xi\in\partial Y
, and there exists a
subsequence
\{\gamma_{j_{k}}\}
of
\{\gamma_{j}\}
such that

\{f(\gamma_{j_{k}})\}
converges to
\xi
in the cone topology.

Remark 6.13
.

The theorem above can be regarded as a sort of compactness theorem.
As was pointed out in
[
1
]
, a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
with finite telescopic dimension has a certain compactness property;
if one considers the weakest topology that makes any convex closed
subset (w.r.t. usual topology) is closed, then, for a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space

Y
with finite telescopic dimension,
Y\cup\partial Y
becomes
compact (see also
[
37
]
).
Although we do not use this compactificaiton in the present
paper, there might be some relation between our compactness theorem
above.

Now we proceed to the proof of Theorem
A
, assuming
Theorem
6.12
.

By Lemma
6.6
and
Proposition
6.9
, we see that a function defined by

\varphi_{\omega}(\gamma)=b_{\xi(\omega)}(f(\gamma),f(e))

is
\mu
-harmonic on whole
\Gamma
.
Take
\gamma\in\Gamma
, and let

\rho_{\gamma}\colon\gamma^{\prime}\mapsto\rho(\gamma\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{-1})
.
Then, for any
\rho
-equivariant map
f^{\prime}
, a new map
f_{\gamma}^{\prime}

defined by
f_{\gamma}^{\prime}\colon\gamma^{\prime}\mapsto f(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})

satisfies

\rho_{\gamma}(\gamma^{\prime})f_{\gamma}^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime\prime})=\rho(\gamma\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{-1})f^{\prime}(\gamma\gamma^{\prime\prime})=f^{\prime}(\gamma\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime})=f_{\gamma}^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}),

that is,
f_{\gamma}^{\prime}
is
\rho_{\gamma}
-equivariant. Also we
have

\begin{split}E_{\mu}(f_{\gamma}^{\prime})&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}d(f_{\gamma}^{\prime}(e),f_{\gamma}^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}))^{2}d\mu(\gamma^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}d(f^{\prime}(\gamma e),f^{\prime}(\gamma\gamma^{\prime}))^{2}d\mu(\gamma^{\prime})\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}d(f^{\prime}(e),f^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime}))^{2}d\mu(\gamma^{\prime})=E_{\mu}(f^{\prime}).\end{split}

Therefore we see that
f_{\gamma}
defined from a
\rho
-equivariant

\mu
-harmonic map
f
becomes a
\rho_{\gamma}
-equivariant

\mu
-harmonic map.
Note also that

\varphi\colon\gamma^{\prime}\mapsto b_{\xi(\omega)}(f_{\gamma}(\gamma^{\prime}),f_{\gamma}(e))=b_{\xi(\omega)}(f(\gamma\gamma^{\prime}),f(e))+b_{\xi(\omega)}(f(e),f(\gamma))

is
\mu
-harmonic, since

\varphi_{\omega}\colon\gamma\mapsto b_{\xi(\omega)}(f(\gamma),f(e))

is
\mu
-harmonic on whole
\Gamma
and

b_{\xi(\omega)}(f(e),f(\gamma))
is a constant independent of

\gamma^{\prime}
.
Then we see that,
by applying Lemma
5.1

(2)
to

f_{\gamma}
and
\varphi
, the convex hull of

[f(\gamma),\xi(\omega))\cup[f(\gamma),f(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})]\cup[f(\gamma\gamma^{\prime}),\xi(\omega))

is a half-infinite parallelogram for any

\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
.

Now we show that
\xi(\omega)=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}f(\gamma_{j})
and

\xi^{-}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}f(\gamma_{j}^{-1})

satisfy the assumption of Theorem
6.12
, and also that
there exists a sequence
\{f(\tilde{\gamma}_{j})\}
such that

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}f(\tilde{\gamma}_{j})=\xi^{+}
and
\{f(\tilde{\gamma}_{j})\}

satisfies the assumption of Theorem
6.12
.
Once we accomplish this, the proof completes as follows:
We see that each sequence converges to its limit in the cone topology.
Hence we can apply Lemma
5.1
(3), (4) and
obtain a splitting
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))=H\times Z
.
Furthermore we have a homomorphism

\rho_{2}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Z})
with
l_{\rho_{2}}(\Gamma)=0

and
\rho_{2}
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map

f_{2}\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
.
Since
\mathrm{CH}(f(\Gamma))
has finite telescopic dimension, so does
Z
.
Now we can apply the argument we have applied to
Y
to
Z
.
Then we see that either
Z
splits or
\rho_{2}(\Gamma)
in
Lemma
5.1
(4) fixes a point
p
in
Z
.
However, by our choice of
Z
, the former cannot occur.
Thus we conclude that

\rho(\Gamma)=\rho_{1}(\Gamma)\times\rho_{2}(\Gamma)
leaves
a flat subspace
H\times\{p\}
invariant.

By our choice of
\{f(\gamma_{j})\}
and
Lemma
6.6
, it is clear that

\{f(\gamma_{j})\}
satisfies the assumption of
Theorem
6.12
, and hence, by passing to a subsequence
if necessary, we may assume that
\{f(\gamma_{j})\}
converges to

\xi(\omega)
in the cone topology.
By Lemma
5.1

(2)
, we know that, for any

\gamma\in\Gamma
, the convex hull of

[f(e),\xi(\omega))\cup[f(e),f(\gamma)]\cup[f(\gamma),\xi(\omega))

is isometric to a half-infinite parallelogram in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Now we show that Lemma
5.1

(3)
is true even if the
convergences to
\xi^{\pm}
is given by ultralimits.
Since
\{f(\gamma_{j})\}
converges to
\xi(\omega)
in the cone
topology, by the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
5.1

(3)
,
we see that, for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

\angle_{f(\gamma_{j}\gamma)}(\xi(\omega),f(e))\to\pi
.
Consider a geodesic

c_{j}=c_{f(\gamma_{j}\gamma)}^{\xi(\omega)}
.
Fix
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
take a geodesic
\bar{c}_{j}
with
\bar{c}_{j}(0)=f(\gamma_{j}\gamma)
and

\bar{c}_{j}(d(f(e),f(\gamma_{j}\gamma)))=f(e)
.
Since
\angle_{f(\gamma_{j}\gamma)}(\xi(\omega),f(e))\to\pi
,

d(c_{j}(t),\bar{c}_{j}(t))\to 2t
for any
t>0
.
Consider geodesic rays
c=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})c_{j}
and

\bar{c}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\bar{c}_{j}
in
Y_{\infty}
.
Then
c(0)=\bar{c}(0)=f(\gamma)
and
d(c(t),\bar{c}(t))=2t
for any

t>0
, and hence
c
and
\bar{c}
form a straight geodesic line in

Y_{\infty}
, which we denote by
c_{\gamma}
.
This geodesic line
c_{\gamma}
can be parametrized so that

c_{\gamma}(\infty)=\xi^{+}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi(\omega)
and

c_{\gamma}(-\infty)=\xi^{-}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})f(e)
, where

\xi^{+}
and
\xi^{-}
are points in
\partial Y_{\infty}
.
Note that both
\xi^{+}
and
\xi^{-}
do not depend on
\gamma
by
definition, and we get a family of geodesic lines joining
\xi^{+}
and

\xi^{-}
in
Y_{\infty}
passing through each
f(\gamma)
.

Then, by

[
5
, p. 182, 2.13 The Flat Strip Theorem]
,
we see that, for any
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
,
the convex hull of
c_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}})\cup c_{\gamma^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}})
is a flat strip

\tilde{F}_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}
.
In particular, the intersection of the horosphere centered at
\xi^{+}

and
\tilde{F}_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}
is a geodesic segment perpendicular to

c_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}})
and
c_{e}({\mathbb{R}})
, and the same is true for
\xi^{-}
.
Therefore we obtain

b_{\xi^{-}}(f(\gamma),f(e))=-b_{\xi^{+}}(f(\gamma),f(e)).

Since both
\gamma\mapsto b_{\xi^{\pm}}(f(\gamma),f(e))
are

\mu
-subharmonic, both of them must be
\mu
-harmonic.
By Lemma
6.6
, we see that

\{f(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\}
satisfies the assumption of
Theorem
6.12
.
Therefore, by passing to a subsequence if necessary, we see that

\{f(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\}
converges to
\xi^{-}
in the cone topology.

Now consider
\xi^{+}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi(\omega)
.
Since we know that
f(\gamma_{j})
converges to
\xi(\omega)
in the cone
topology, for any
j
, we can find
n(j)\in{\mathbb{N}}
so that

\angle_{f(\gamma_{j})}(f(\gamma_{n(j)}),\xi(\omega))\leq 1/j,\ \text{ and }d(f(\gamma_{j}),f(\gamma_{n(j)}))\geq j,

which implies

\angle_{f(e)}(f(\gamma_{j}^{-1}\gamma_{n(j)}),\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi(\omega))\leq 1/j,\ \text{and }\ d(f(e),f(\gamma_{j}^{-1}\gamma_{n(j)}))\geq j.

By applying Lemma
5.1

(2)
to

\rho_{\gamma_{j}}
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map
f_{\gamma_{j}}
, we see
that the convex hull of

[f(\gamma_{j}),\xi(\omega))\cup[f(\gamma_{j}),f(\gamma_{n(j)})]\cup[f(\gamma_{n(j)}),\xi(\omega))

is a half-infinite prallelogram, hence
the same is true for the convex hull of

[f(e),\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi(\omega))\cup[f(e),f(\gamma_{j}^{-1}\gamma_{n(j)})]\cup[f(\gamma_{j}^{-1}\gamma_{n(j)}),\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi(\omega))
.
Therefore, setting

\tilde{\gamma}_{j}=\gamma_{j}^{-1}\gamma_{n(j)}
and

\xi_{j}=\rho(\gamma_{j}^{-1})\xi(\omega)
, we get

d(c_{f(e)}^{f(\tilde{\gamma}_{j})}(t),c_{f(e)}^{\xi_{j}}(t))\leq 2t\sin\frac{1}{2j}

for
t\in[0,j]
, and hence

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}c_{f(e)}^{f(\tilde{\gamma}_{j})}(t)=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}c_{f(e)}^{\xi_{j}}(t)=c_{f(e)}^{\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\xi_{j}}(t)

for each
t
. In particular, we see that

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}f(\tilde{\gamma}_{j})=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\xi_{j}=\xi^{+}
.
Since we already know that
\gamma\mapsto b_{\xi^{+}}(f(\gamma),f(e))

is
\mu
-harmonic,
we see that

\{f(\tilde{\gamma}_{j})\}
satisfies the assumption of
Theorem
6.12
, and in particular

\xi^{+}\in\partial Y
.
This completes the proof of Theorem
A
. ∎

6.5.
Proof of Theorem
C

Let
Y
be a locally finite-dimensional
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space, and

\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
a homomorphism.
If
\rho(\Gamma)
is
reductive in the sense of Jost,
then we know that there exists a
\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map
by Corollary
6.4
.
Once the existence of a
\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map is
achieved,
since our compactness theorem (Theorem
6.12
)
is also valid for locally finite-dimensional
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces,
the rest of the proof of Theorem
C
is
completely the same as the proof of Theorem
A
we have
seen above.

7.
Proof of a compactness theorem

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
6.12
.
We start with some lemmas on flat subsets in
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
spaces.

7.1.
Some lemmas on flat subsets

Lemma 7.1
.

Suppose that
c_{i}\colon[0,a]\rightarrow Y
is a geodesic
(i=1,2)
,
and that
t\mapsto d(c_{1}(t),c_{2}(t))
is constant. Then the convex hull
of
c_{1}([0,a])\cup c_{2}([0,a])
is isometric to a parallelogram in

{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Proof.

Take comparison triangles of

\triangle(c_{1}(0),c_{1}(a),c_{2}(a))
and

\triangle(c_{1}(0),c_{2}(0),c_{2}(a))
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, and
identify two edges corresponing to
[c_{1}(0),c_{2}(a)]
.
Since
d(c_{1}(0),c_{1}(a))=d(c_{2}(0),c_{2}(a))
and

d(c_{1}(0),c_{2}(0))=d(c_{1}(a),c_{2}(a))
,
we get a parallelogram in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Take any
t\in(0,a)
and fix it for a while.
Let
\overline{y}
be a point in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
given by

\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.13078pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$y$}}=[\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.60129pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$c_{1}(t)$}},\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.60129pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$c_{2}(t)$}}]\cap[\overline{c_{1}(0)},\overline{c_{2}(a)}]
,
and
y\in[c_{1}(0),c_{2}(a)]
a corresponding point in
Y
.
Then we have
d(c_{i}(t),y)\leq d(\overline{c_{i}(t)},\overline{y})
for

i=1,2
, and hence

d(c_{1}(t),c_{2}(t))\leq d(c_{1}(t),y)+d(c_{2}(t),y)\leq d(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.60129pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$c_{1}(t)$}},\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.13078pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$y$}})+d(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.60129pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$c_{2}(t)$}},\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.13078pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$y$}})=d(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.60129pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$c_{1}(t)$}},\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.60129pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$c_{2}(t)$}}).

On the other hand, by our assumption,

d(c_{1}(t),c_{2}(t))=d(c_{1}(0),c_{2}(0))=d(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 8.29575pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$c_{1}(0)$}},\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 8.29575pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$c_{2}(0)$}})=d(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.60129pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$c_{1}(t)$}},\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.60129pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$c_{2}(t)$}}).

Thus we see that
d(c_{i}(t),y)=d(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.02287pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$c_{i}(t)$}},\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.13078pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$y$}})
for

i=1,2
.
Therefore, by
[
5
, p. 181, 2.10 Exercise (1)]
,

\triangle(c_{1}(0),c_{1}(a),c_{2}(a))
and

\triangle(c_{1}(0),c_{2}(0),c_{2}(a))
are both flat, that is, the convex hull
of these triangles in
Y
are isometric to the convex hull of their
comparison triangles in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
respectively.
In particular, the angles
\angle_{c_{1}(a)}(c_{1}(0),c_{2}(a))
and

\angle_{c_{2}(0)}(c_{1}(0),c_{2}(a))
are equal to the corresponding angles
of the parallelogram in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
above.

Now consider triangles
\triangle(c_{1}(0),c_{1}(a),c_{2}(0))
and

\triangle(c_{2}(a),c_{1}(a),c_{2}(0))
and follow the argument above.
Then we see that both triangles are flat, and hence the angles

\angle_{c_{1}(0)}(c_{1}(a),c_{2}(0))
and

\angle_{c_{2}(a)}(c_{1}(a),c_{2}(0))
are equal to the corresponding angles
of the parallelogram in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
again.
Thus we see that the sum of

\angle_{c_{1}(a)}(c_{1}(0),c_{2}(a))
,
\angle_{c_{2}(0)}(c_{1}(0),c_{2}(a))
,

\angle_{c_{1}(0)}(c_{1}(a),c_{2}(0))
, and
\angle_{c_{2}(a)}(c_{1}(a),c_{2}(0))

is equal to
2\pi
.
Then, by

[
5
, p. 181, 2.11 The flat quadrilateral theorem]
,
we see that the convex hull of
c_{1}([0,a])\cup c_{2}([0,a])
is flat and
isometric to the parallelogram in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
∎

Remark 7.2
.

Since
d(c_{1}(t),c_{2}(t))
need not be equal to
d(c_{1}(t),c_{2}([0,a]))
,
the lemma above is slightly different from the Sandwich lemma

[
5
, p. 182, 2.12 Exercise (2)]
, and we do not
obtain a rectangle as the convex hull in the statement.

Recall that we have often denoted a geodesic starting from

x\in Y
and terminating at
y\in Y\cup\partial Y
by
c_{x}^{y}
; we
will use this notation frequently in what follows.
Also if the convex hull of two geodesic rays

[p,\eta)\cup[p,\xi)
is isometric to a flat sector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
we denote this convex hull by
\mathrm{Sect}_{p}(\eta,\xi)
.
We also denote by
[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]\subset\partial Y
a geodesic
segment joining
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
with respect to the angular metric

d_{\angle}
on
\partial Y
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
Y
be a complete
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space.
Suppose that, for
p_{i}\in Y

(i=1,2)
and

\eta_{j}\in\partial Y

(j=1,2)
with

0<d_{\angle}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})<\pi
,
the convex hull of

[p_{i},\eta_{1})\cup[p_{i},\eta_{2})
is isometric to a flat sector
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Assume further that, for each
p\in[p_{1},p_{2}]
and

\xi\in[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]\subset\partial Y
, a geodesic ray
[p,\xi)

exists and that
\bigcup_{p\in[p_{1},p_{2}]}[p,\xi)
is isometric to a
half-infinite parallelogram in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

(
it may degenerate to a half line
)
,
which we denote by
F^{\xi}
.

(1)
Take
s_{1},s_{2}>0
, and denote by
R_{s_{1}s_{2}}
the convex hull of

[c_{p_{1}}^{\eta_{1}}(s_{1}),c_{p_{1}}^{\eta_{2}}(s_{2})]\cup[c_{p_{2}}^{\eta_{1}}(s_{1}),c_{p_{2}}^{\eta_{2}}(s_{2})]
.
Then
R_{s_{1}s_{2}}
is isometric to a prallelogram in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

(2)
For each
p\in[p_{1},p_{2}]
, the convex hull of

[p,\eta_{1})\cup[p,\eta_{2})
is isometric to
a flat sector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

(3)

Let
c_{p}^{\xi}\colon[0,\infty)\rightarrow Y
be a
geodesic ray joining
p\in Y
and
\xi\in[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]
.
Consider a map

\tau_{s}^{\xi}\colon q\mapsto c_{q}^{\xi}(s)
,
s>0
,
defined on

\bigcup_{p\in[p_{1},p_{2}]}\mathrm{Sect}_{p}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
.
Then
\tau_{s}^{\xi}
maps
R_{s_{1}s_{2}}
isometrically onto its image.

(4)
Take
s_{0}>0
.
For any
p\in[p_{1},p_{2}]
and

q_{j}\in F^{\eta_{j}}\cap R_{s_{0}s_{0}}
,
j=1,2

\triangle(p,q_{1},q_{2})
is a flat triangle.

Proof.

(1)
Let
c\colon[0,a]\rightarrow Y
be a geodesic joining
p_{1}
and

p_{2}
.
First note that, since we have

\angle_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\angle_{p_{2}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=d_{\angle}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})

(see Remark
2.7
),

\mathrm{Sect}_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
and
\mathrm{Sect}_{p_{2}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
are
isometric to each other.
Therefore we have

d(c_{p_{1}}^{\eta_{1}}(s_{1}),c_{p_{1}}^{\eta_{2}}(s_{2}))=d(c_{p_{2}}^{\eta_{1}}(s_{1}),c_{p_{2}}^{\eta_{2}}(s_{2}))
,
and we denote this value by
d
.

Consider a geodesic

\tilde{c}_{i}\colon[0,d]\rightarrow\mathrm{Sect}_{p_{i}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})

with
\tilde{c}_{i}(0)=c_{p_{i}}^{\eta_{1}}(s_{1})
and

\tilde{c}_{i}(d)=c_{p_{i}}^{\eta_{2}}(s_{2})
,

i=1,2
. Then, for any
t\in[0,d]
,

\angle_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\tilde{c}_{1}(t))=\angle_{p_{2}}(\eta_{1},\tilde{c}_{2}(t))
,
and we see that there exists
\xi_{t}\in[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]
such that

\tilde{c}_{i}(t)\in[p_{i},\xi_{t})
for
i=1,2
.
Since we are assuming that there exists
F^{\xi}
for each
\xi
,

[\tilde{c}_{1}(t),\tilde{c}_{2}(t)]
is parallel to

[p_{1},p_{2}]
in
F^{\xi_{t}}

and given by

\tau\mapsto c_{c(\tau)}^{\xi_{t}}(s)
for some
s
.
In particular,

d(\tilde{c}_{1}(t),\tilde{c}_{2}(t))=d(p_{1},p_{2})

for any
t\in[0,d]
.

Therefore, by Lemma
7.1
, we see that
the convex hull of

[\tilde{c}_{1}(0),\tilde{c}_{1}(d)]\cup[\tilde{c}_{2}(0),\tilde{c}_{2}(d)]=[c_{p_{1}}^{\eta_{1}}(s_{1}),c_{p_{1}}^{\eta_{2}}(s_{2})]\cup[c_{p_{2}}^{\eta_{1}}(s_{1}),c_{p_{2}}^{\eta_{2}}(s_{2})]

is isometric to a flat parallelogram, which we denote by
R_{s_{1}s_{2}}
.

(2)
By
(1)
, we see that

d(c_{p}^{\eta_{1}}(s),c_{p}^{\eta_{2}}(s))=d(c_{p_{1}}^{\eta_{1}}(s),c_{p_{1}}^{\eta_{2}}(s))

for any
p\in[p_{1},p_{2}]
and
s\in[0,\infty)
.
In particular, for any
p\in[p_{1},p_{2}]
,

\angle_{p}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\angle_{p_{1}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=d_{\angle}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
.
Therefore, by
[
5
, p. 283, 9.9 Corollary]
,

[p,\eta_{1})\cup[p,\eta_{2})
bounds a flat sector.

(3)
Let
q_{i}^{j}=c_{p_{i}}^{\eta_{j}}(s_{j})
for
i,j=1,2
.
We know that the convex hull of

[q_{1}^{1},q_{1}^{2}]\cup[q_{2}^{1},q_{2}^{2}]
is a flat parallelogram by (1).
Since
\mathrm{Sect}_{p_{i}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
is a flat sector,

c_{q_{i}^{1}}^{\xi}
and
c_{q_{i}^{2}}^{\xi}
are parallel to each other, and
hence
t\mapsto d(c_{q_{i}^{1}}^{\xi}(t),c_{q_{i}^{2}}^{\xi}(t))
is constant.

Fix
t\geq 0
for a while, and let
\tilde{c}_{i}
be a geodesic joining

c_{q_{i}^{1}}^{\xi}(t)
and
c_{q_{i}^{2}}^{\xi}(t)
.
Since
\mathrm{Sect}_{p_{i}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
,
i=1,2
, are isometric to
each other, for each
\tau
and
i=1,2
,

\tilde{c}_{i}(\tau)\in F^{\xi_{\tau}}
for the same

\xi_{\tau}\in[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]
.
Then, by the same argument as we have seen in the proof of
(1)

above, we see that

d(\tilde{c}_{1}(\tau),\tilde{c}_{2}(\tau))=d(p_{1},p_{2})
for any
\tau
,
and hence the convex hull of

[c_{q_{1}^{1}}^{\xi}(t),c_{q_{1}^{2}}^{\xi}(t)]\cup[c_{q_{2}^{1}}^{\xi}(t),c_{q_{2}^{2}}^{\xi}(t)]

is isometric to a flat parallelogram.

Now we vary
t
.
Note that
d(c_{q_{1}^{1}}^{\xi}(t),c_{q_{2}^{2}}^{\xi}(t))
and

d(c_{q_{1}^{2}}^{\xi}(t),c_{q_{2}^{1}}^{\xi}(t))
are bounded convex
function defined on
[0,\infty)
, and hence both are nonincreasing.
These functions give the length of two
diagonal lines of parallelograms with fixed side length. Therefore, if
one decreases, the other must increase. Hence we see that both
functions are constant.
Therefore, for all
t
, the convex hull of

[c_{q_{1}^{1}}^{\xi}(t),c_{q_{1}^{2}}^{\xi}(t)]\cup[c_{q_{2}^{1}}^{\xi}(t),c_{q_{2}^{2}}^{\xi}(t)]

are isometric to the same flat parallelogram.
Since
\tau_{s}^{\xi}
maps

[c_{q_{i}^{1}}^{\xi}(0),c_{q_{i}^{2}}^{\xi}(0)]
to

[c_{q_{i}^{1}}^{\xi}(s),c_{q_{i}^{2}}^{\xi}(s)]
, this shows
(3)
.

(4)
Replacing
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
if neccesary, we may assume

p=c(t_{0})\in[p_{1},p_{2})
.
Let
q_{i}\in F^{\eta_{i}}
,
(i=1,2)
and consider

\triangle(p,q_{1},q_{2})
.
Denote by
c_{i}

(i=1,2)
a geodesic joining
p
and
q_{i}
.
Since
F^{\eta_{i}}
is flat,
c_{i}
is lying in
F^{\eta_{i}}
.
Let us identify
F^{\eta_{i}}
with a half-infinite parallelogram

\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.40971pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$F$}}_{i}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
of the form

\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.40971pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$F$}}_{i}=\{a\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.01561pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$p$}}+b\hskip 0.08855pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$q$}\hss}{\hskip-0.08855pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}}_{i}\mid a\in[0,d(p_{1},p_{2})],\ b\geq 0\},

where we identify
c_{p_{1}}^{p_{2}}(1)
with
\bar{p}
and

c_{p_{1}}^{\eta_{i}}(1)
with
\bar{q}_{i}
.
Note that every geodesic segment in
\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.40971pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$F$}}_{i}
starting from

a_{0}\bar{p}
can be expressed
as
t\mapsto(a_{0}+a_{i}t)\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.01561pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$p$}}+(b_{i}t)\hskip 0.08855pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$q$}\hss}{\hskip-0.08855pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}}_{i}
.
Therefore we see that there exists
a_{i},b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
c_{i}

can be expressed as

c_{i}(t)=c_{c(t_{0}+a_{i}t)}^{\eta_{i}}(b_{i}t)
.
Note that
c_{i}(s/b_{i})
lies in
R_{ss}
.
By considering an isometric translation
\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}
in
(3)
,
we see that, for any
s_{1}>0
,
s>0
, and
t\in[0,a]
,

\angle_{c_{c(t)}^{\eta_{1}}(s_{1})}(c_{p_{2}}^{\eta_{1}}(s_{1}),c_{c(t)}^{\eta_{2}}(s_{1}))=\angle_{c_{c(t)}^{\eta_{1}}(s_{1}+s)}(c_{p_{2}}^{\eta_{1}}(s_{1}+s),c_{c(t)}^{\eta_{2}}(s_{1}+s))

and hence

\alpha(s,t)=\angle_{c_{c(t)}^{\eta_{1}}(s)}(c_{p_{2}}^{\eta_{1}}(s),c_{c(t)}^{\eta_{2}}(s))

is independent of
s
.
Furthermore, since

\left\{[c_{c(t)}^{\eta_{1}}(s),c_{c(t)}^{\eta_{2}}(s)]\mid t\in[0,a]\right\}

forms a family of parallel geodesic segments in
R_{ss}
,

\alpha(s,t)
does not depending on
t
either.
Note that
d(c_{1}(s/b_{1}),c_{2}(s/b_{2}))
is equal to the length of
a diagonal line of a parallelogram
R_{ss}^{\prime}
in
R_{ss}
spanned over
an interval

[c(t_{0}+(a_{1}s/b_{1})),c(t_{0}+(a_{2}s/b_{2}))]\subset[p_{1},p_{2}]
,
which is bounded by

[c_{c(\bar{t}_{1})}^{\eta_{1}}(s),c_{c(\bar{t}_{1})}^{\eta_{2}}(s)]
and

[c_{c(\bar{t}_{2})}^{\eta_{1}}(s),c_{c(\bar{t}_{2})}^{\eta_{2}}(s)]
, where

\bar{t}_{i}=t_{0}+(a_{i}s/b_{i})
,
i=1,2
.

Then we see that the length of two pairs of sides of
R_{ss}^{\prime}
are

d(c_{c(\bar{t}_{1})}^{\eta_{i}}{s},c_{c(\bar{t}_{2})}^{\eta_{i}}(s))=d(c(\bar{t}_{1}),c(\bar{t}_{2}))=|\bar{t}_{1}-\bar{t}_{2}|=\left|\frac{a_{1}}{b_{1}}-\frac{a_{2}}{b_{2}}\right|s,\ (i=1,2),

and

d(c_{c(\bar{t}_{i})}^{\eta_{1}}(s),c_{c(\bar{t}_{i})}^{\eta_{2}}(s))=2s\sin\frac{d_{\angle}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})}{2},\ (i=1,2),

since

\mathrm{CH}([c(\bar{t}_{i}),\eta_{1})\cup[c(\bar{t}_{i}),\eta_{2}))

is a flat sector by
(2)
.
Recalling that
\alpha(s,t)
is constant, we see that
the length of diagonal line of
R_{ss}^{\prime}
is also linear in
s
:

d(c_{c(\bar{t}_{1})}^{\eta_{1}}(s),c_{c(\bar{t}_{2})}^{\eta_{2}}(s))=d(c_{1}(s/b_{1}),c_{2}(s/b_{2}))=Cs

for some constant
C
.
Since
d(p,c_{i}(s/b_{i}))=s/b_{i}
is also linear in
s
,
by considering a comparison triangle and taking

[
5
, p. 181, 2.10 Exercises (1)]
into account,
we conclude that
\triangle(p,c_{1}(s/b_{1}),c_{2}(s/b_{2}))
is flat.
This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

Let
p_{1}=(0,0),p_{2}=(1,0)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and denote

(\infty,0)\in\partial{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by
\xi
.
Take
\eta_{i}\in\partial{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
so that

\angle(\xi,\eta_{i})=\theta<\pi/2
.
Then

\mathrm{CH}([p_{2},\eta_{1}),[p_{2},\eta_{2}))\subset\mathrm{CH}([p_{1},\eta_{1}),[p_{1},\eta_{2}))\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
and
F^{\xi}
degenerates to a half line
[p_{1},\xi)
.
Even in this case, the lemma above is valid.

Remark 7.5
.

Suppose that
d_{\angle}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\pi
in the setting of the
lemma above.
Then we have two half-infinite parallelograms
F^{\eta_{i}}
,
i=1,2
,
and
F^{\eta_{1}}\cup F^{\eta_{2}}
is bounded by a two geodesic lines
joining
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
and passing through
p_{1}
and
p_{2}

respectively.
Then the set
F^{\eta_{1}}\cup F^{\eta_{2}}
consists of parallel geodesic
lines joining
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
.
By
[
5
, p. 182, The Flat Strip Theorem]
, we see
that
F^{\eta_{1}}\cup F^{\eta_{2}}
forms a flat strip in
Y
.
Therefore
(1)
,
(3)
, and
(4)
in the lemma above clearly hold.
For
(2)
,
\mathrm{Sect}_{p}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},
) degenerates to a geodesic
line for each
p\in[p_{1},p_{2}]
, and the assertion is also valid in
this sense.

7.2.
Construction of a cone
C^{(m)}
.

Now we return to the setting of Theorem
6.12
.
Consider
\{f(\gamma_{j})\}\subset f(\Gamma)
with

d(f(e),f(\gamma_{j}))\to\infty
, and let
\xi^{(1)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim f(\gamma_{j})
.
We denote
f(\gamma_{j})
by
x_{j}
, and set
x_{0}=f(e)
.
We also denote
Y^{(1)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim(Y,x_{0})
, and

Y^{(i)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim(Y^{(i-1)},x_{0})
inductively.
We often use the expression
\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(i)}_{j}x_{j}
to emphasize that we are
taking
i
th ultralimit; it should be taken on
Y^{(i-1)}
.
As we will see, if
\{x_{j}\}
does not converge to
\xi^{(1)}
in the cone
topology, then
\xi^{(i)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(i)}x_{j}\not=\xi^{(1)}
for
i>1
, and

\{\xi^{(1)},\dots\xi^{(m)}\}

spans a Euclidean cone over a regular Euclidean
(m-1)
-simplex
(Proposition
7.13
), from which we conclude that
\{x_{j}\}

actually converges to
\xi^{(1)}
and derive
Theorem
6.12
.

Setting
.

A
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
