is either of finite telescopic dimension
or locally finite-dimensional.
And a countable group
\Gamma
with a random walk
\mu
acts
isometrically on
Y
via a homomorphism

\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}({Y})
.
Futhermore, from what we have seen so far, we may assume the following:

•

A symmetric probability measure
\mu
on
\Gamma
satisfies

\mathrm{supp}\,\mu\supset\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
.

•

There exists a
\rho
-equivariant

\mu
-harmonic map
f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
.

•

There is a sequence
\{\gamma_{j}\}\subset\Gamma
such that

d(f(e),f(\gamma_{j}))\to\infty
,
and, as in the proof of
Lemma
6.6
, we have

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim f(\gamma_{j})=\xi^{(1)}\in\partial Y^{(1)}
.
We denote
f(\gamma_{j})
by
x_{j}
, and
\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(i)}x_{j}=\xi^{(i)}
.

•

A function

\varphi_{\xi^{(1)}}\colon\gamma\mapsto b_{\xi^{(1)}}(f(\gamma),f(e))

defined on
\Gamma
is a
\mu
-harmonic function.
The same is true for

\varphi_{\xi^{(i)}}\colon\gamma\mapsto b_{\xi^{(i)}}(f(\gamma),f(e))

for any
i
by Lemma
6.6
and
the proof of Proposition
6.9
.

•

For any
x=f(\gamma),x^{\prime}=f(\gamma^{\prime})\in f(\Gamma)
and
i
,
by applying Lemma
5.1

(2)
to

\rho_{\gamma}
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic map
f_{\gamma}
as
in §
6.4
,
we see that the convex hull
F_{xx^{\prime}}^{(i)}
of

[x,\xi^{(i)})\cup[x,x^{\prime}]\cup[x^{\prime},\xi^{(i)})

is isometric to a half-infinite parallelogram in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Also, in order to prove Theorem
6.12
, we assume that

\theta_{0}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\angle_{f(e)}(x_{j},\xi^{(1)})>0
, which will lead us to a
contradiction in §
7.5
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Under our setting, we have

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\angle_{x_{0}}(x_{j},\xi^{(i)})<\pi
for any
i
.

Proof.

What we have to show is that there exists
\delta>0
such that

\lambda\left(\{j\mid\angle_{x_{0}}(x_{j},\xi^{(i)})\leq\pi-\delta\right\})=1.

Suppose not. Then, since
\lambda
is an ultrafilter, we get

\lambda(A_{\delta})=1
for any
\delta>0
, where

A_{\delta}=\{i\mid\angle_{x_{0}}(x_{j},\xi^{(i)})>\pi-\delta\}.

Take
\delta
so that
\delta<\pi/2
.
Since
f
is
\mu
-harmonic and

\int_{\Gamma}b_{\xi^{(i)}}(f(\gamma),x_{0})d\mu(\gamma)=0
, we have

\int_{\Gamma}\left\langle\log_{x_{0}}(f(\gamma)),\,V_{c}\right\rangle d\mu(\gamma)=0

as in the proof of Lemma
5.1

(2)
, where

V_{c}\in S_{x_{0}}Y^{(i)}
corresponds to a geodesic ray with

c(0)=x_{0}
and
c(\infty)=\xi^{(i)}
.
Since
\left\langle\log_{x_{0}}(x_{j}),\,V_{c}\right\rangle<0
for any

j\in A_{\delta}
by our assumption,
there must be
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
satisfying

\left\langle\log_{x_{0}}(f(\gamma^{\prime})),\,V_{c}\right\rangle>0
.
Let
d=d(x_{0},f(\gamma^{\prime}))
, and
c^{\prime}
a geodesic joining
x_{0}
and

f(\gamma^{\prime})
.
Take a geodesic
c_{j}
joining
x_{0}
and
x_{j}
.
Since we are assuming
d(x_{0},x_{j})\to\infty
,
we may assume that
d(x_{0},x_{j})>d

holds and hence
c_{j}(d)
is defined for any
j\in A_{\delta}
.
Since
\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim c_{j}(t)=c(t)
, we get

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim d(c^{\prime}(t),c_{j}(t))=d(c^{\prime}(t),c(t)),

which means that, by setting

B_{\delta^{\prime}}=\{j\in{\mathbb{N}}\mid|d(c^{\prime}(t),c_{j}(t))-d(c^{\prime}(t),c(t))|<\delta^{\prime}\},

we have
\lambda(B_{\delta^{\prime}})=1
for any
\delta^{\prime}>0
.
Since
\lambda
is an ultrafilter, we get

\lambda(A_{\delta}\cap B_{\delta^{\prime}})=1
.
By the triangle inequality on
(S_{x_{0}}Y^{(i)},\angle_{x_{0}})
,
we get

\angle_{x_{0}}(c_{j}(t),c^{\prime}(t))\geq\angle_{x_{0}}(c_{j}(t),c(t))-\angle_{x_{0}}(c^{\prime}(t),c(t))\geq\pi-\delta-\angle_{x_{0}}(c^{\prime}(t),c(t)),

for any
j\in A_{\delta}
.
While
\left\langle\log_{x_{0}}(f(\gamma^{\prime})),\,V_{c}\right\rangle>0
implies

\angle_{x_{0}}(c^{\prime}(t),c(t))<\pi/2
, we see that
for a sufficiently small
\delta>0
,

\angle_{x_{0}}(c_{j}(t),c^{\prime}(t))\geq\pi/2+\alpha

holds for some
\alpha>0
and any
j\in A_{\delta}
.
Since the comparison angle is nonincreasing as
t\to 0

(
[
5
, p. 184, 3.1 Proposition]
),
this implies that

d(c_{j}(t),c^{\prime}(t))^{2}\geq(2+\alpha^{\prime})t^{2}
for some
\alpha^{\prime}>0
and
any
j\in A_{\delta}
and
t>0
.
Since
\lambda(A_{\delta})=1
, we see that

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim d(c_{j}(t),c^{\prime}(t))^{2}=d(c(t),c^{\prime}(t))^{2}>2t^{2}
.
On the other hand, for sufficiently small
t>0
, we have

d(c^{\prime}(t),c(t))^{2}\leq 2t^{2}

by
\left\langle\log_{x_{0}}(f(\gamma^{\prime})),\,V_{c}\right\rangle>0
.
A contradiction.
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

Under our setting, for any
i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
x\in f(\Gamma)
,
the convex hull of
[x,\xi^{(i)})\cup[x,\xi^{(j)})
is a flat
sector
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{(i)},\xi^{(j)})
with angle
\theta_{0}
.

Proof.

By our assumption, for each
x
and
j
,

[x,\xi^{(1)})\cup[x,x_{j}]\cup[x_{j},\xi^{(1)})

bounds a half inifinite parallelogram,
which we denote by
F_{xx_{j}}^{(1)}
.
Let
c_{x}^{x_{j}}\colon[0,d(x,x_{j})]\rightarrow Y
be a geodesic joining

x
and
x_{j}
, and

c_{x}^{(1)}\colon[0,\infty)\rightarrow Y^{(1)}
a geodesic ray
joining
x
and
\xi^{(1)}
, which is obtained as

c_{x}^{(1)}(t)=\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(1)}_{j}c_{x}^{x_{j}}(t)
.
Set
\theta_{x}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim\angle_{x}(x_{j},\xi^{(1)})
and fix
x
for a while.
Then, since
F_{xx_{j}}^{(1)}
is flat,

\angle_{x}(x_{j},\xi^{(1)})=2\arcsin\left(d(c_{x}^{(1)}(t),c_{x}^{x_{j}}(t))/2t\right)

for any
t>0
,
and thus
\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\angle_{x}(x_{j},\xi^{(1)})=\theta_{x}

implies that

(7.1)

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d(c_{x}^{(1)}(t),c_{x}^{x_{j}}(t))=2t\sin\frac{\theta_{x}}{2}

for any
t>0
.

Now consider
Y^{(2)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim(Y^{(1)},p_{0})
.
Then we have a geodesic ray
c_{x}^{(2)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(2)}_{j}c_{x}^{x_{j}}
as
before.
Then
\xi^{(2)}=c_{x}^{(2)}(\infty)
.
By recalling (
7.1
) with
x=f(e)
and our
assumption
\theta_{0}>0
,
c_{f(e)}^{(2)}
cannot coincide with

c_{f(e)}^{(1)}
, and hence
\xi^{(2)}\not=\xi^{(1)}
.
Note that, for each
t>0
, we have

d(c_{x}^{(1)}(t),c_{x}^{(2)}(t))=2t\sin(\theta_{x}/2)
, hence

\angle_{x}(c_{x}^{(1)},c_{x}^{(2)})=\theta_{x}
by

[
5
, p. 184, 3.1 Proposition]
.
Therefore, each triangle

\triangle(x,c_{x}^{(1)}(t),c_{x}^{(2)}(t))
is isometric to a
Euclidean triangle by
[
5
, p. 180 2.9 Proposition]
,
and hence
[x,\xi^{(1)})\cup[x,\xi^{(2)})
bounds a flat
sector with angle
\theta_{x}
in
Y^{(2)}
.
Thus
\theta_{x}=d_{\angle}(\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)})
, and
we see that
\theta_{x}=\theta_{0}
for all
x\in f(\Gamma)
.
Since we know
\theta_{0}<\pi
by Lemma
7.6
,
these flat sectors do not degenerate to a geodesic line.
By our assumption,

[x,\xi^{(2)})\cup[x,x_{j}]\cup[x_{j},\xi^{(2)})
also bounds
a half inifite parallelogram.
Note that, for fixed
x,x^{\prime}\in f(\Gamma)
, when we take an ultralimit for
the first time and obtain
Y^{(1)}
,
we have

d(c_{x}^{x^{\prime}}(t),c_{x}^{(1)}(t))=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d(c_{x}^{x^{\prime}}(t),c_{x}^{x_{j}}(t))
,
while when we take it for the second time and obtain

Y^{(2)}
, we must have

d(c_{x}^{x^{\prime}}(t),c_{x}^{(2)}(t))=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d(c_{x}^{x^{\prime}}(t),c_{x}^{x_{j}}(t))
.
In particular,

\angle_{x}(x_{j},\xi^{(2)})=\angle_{x}(x_{j},\xi^{(1)})

for all
j
and
x\in f(\Gamma)
, and we have

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\angle_{x}(x_{j},\xi^{(2)})=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\angle_{x}(x_{j},\xi^{(1)})=\theta_{0}
.
Thus, in
Y^{(3)}
, we see that

[x,\xi^{(i)})\cup[x,\xi^{(j)})
bounds
a flat sector with angle
\theta_{0}
for each
i,j=1,2,3
with
i\not=j
.
Repeat this
n
times, and denote by
\xi^{(i)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(i)}x_{j}
,
an ultralimit of
\{x_{j}\}
taken in
Y^{(i-1)}
.
Then we get a flat sector
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{(i)},\xi^{(l)})

bounded by
[x,\xi^{(i)})\cup[x,\xi^{(l)})
with angle

\theta_{0}
as
\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(l)}F_{xx_{j}}^{(i)}
for any
1\leq i<l\leq n
.
∎

Take any
x=f(\gamma)\in f(\Gamma)
,

\eta\in[\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)}]\subset\partial Y^{(2)}
, and

t^{\prime}
so that

c_{x}^{\eta}(t^{\prime})\in[c_{x}^{(1)}(t),c_{x}^{(2)}(t)]
.
Since
\triangle(x,c_{x}^{(1)}(t),c_{x}^{(2)}(t))
lies in a flat
sector
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)})
by
Lemma
7.7
, we have

d(x,c_{x}^{\eta}(t^{\prime}))=(1-s)d(x,c_{x_{k}}^{(1)}(t))+sd(x,c_{x}^{(2)}(t)),

where
s\in[0,1]
is determined by

d(c_{x}^{(1)}(t),c_{x}^{\eta}(t^{\prime}))=sd(c_{x}^{(1)}(t),c_{x}^{(2)}(t))
.
Now take any
p\in Y
.
Then by the conexity of the distance function, we see that

d(p,c_{x}^{\eta}(t^{\prime}))\leq(1-s)d(p,c_{x}^{(1)}(t))+sd(p,c_{x}^{(2)}(t)).

Thus, by letting
t^{\prime},t\to\infty
, we get

\begin{split}&b_{\eta}(p,x)=\lim_{t^{\prime}\to\infty}d(p,c_{x}^{\eta}(t^{\prime}))-d(x,c_{x}^{\eta}(t^{\prime}))\\
\leq&\lim_{t\to\infty}\left(\left((1-s)d(p,c_{x}^{(1)}(t))+sd(p,c_{x}^{(2)}(t))\right)\right.\\
&\left.\phantom{\lim_{t\to\infty}\ \ }-\left((1-s)d(x,c_{x}^{(1)}(t))+sd(x,c_{x}^{(2)}(t))\right)\right)\\
=&\lim_{t\to\infty}\left((1-s)\left(d(p,c_{x}^{(1)}(t))-t\right)+s\left(d(p,c_{x}^{(2)}(t))-t\right)\right)\\
=&(1-s)b_{\xi^{(1)}}(p,x)+sb_{\xi^{(2)}}(p,x).\end{split}

Since
\xi^{(i)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(i)}_{j}x_{j}
, for any
p\in Y
, we have

b_{\xi^{(1)}}(p,x)=b_{\xi^{(2)}}(p,x)
by
Lemma
6.6
, and hence

b_{\eta}(p,x)\leq b_{\xi^{(i)}}(p,x)

for
i=1,2
.

Since
p\mapsto b_{\eta}(p,x)
is a convex function and

f
is a
\mu
-harmonic map, we see that a function

\varphi_{\eta}\colon\gamma^{\prime}\mapsto b_{\eta}(f(\gamma^{\prime}),f(\gamma))

is
\mu
-subharmonic; for any
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
, we have

\Delta\varphi_{\eta}(\gamma^{\prime})=b_{\eta}(f(\gamma^{\prime}),f(\gamma))-\int_{\Gamma}b_{\eta}(f(\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}),f(\gamma))d\mu(\gamma^{\prime\prime})\leq 0.

On the other hand, since we already know that

\gamma^{\prime}\mapsto b_{\xi^{(i)}}(f(\gamma^{\prime}),f(\gamma))
is
\mu
-harmonic,
noting
b_{\xi}(x,x)=0
, we see that

(7.2)

\begin{split}0&\geq b_{\eta}(f(\gamma),f(\gamma))-\int_{\Gamma}b_{\eta}(f(\gamma\gamma^{\prime}),f(\gamma))d\mu(\gamma^{\prime})\\
&\geq b_{\xi^{(i)}}(f(\gamma),f(\gamma))-\int_{\Gamma}b_{\xi^{(i)}}(f(\gamma\gamma^{\prime}),f(\gamma))d\mu(\gamma^{\prime})=0.\end{split}

Noting that the computation above is valid for any

x=f(\gamma)\in f(\Gamma)
, and that

\begin{split}&b_{\eta}(f(\gamma^{\prime}),f(\gamma))-\int_{\Gamma}b_{\eta}(f(\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}),f(\gamma))d\mu(\gamma^{\prime\prime})\\
=&b_{\eta}(f(\gamma^{\prime}),f(\gamma^{\prime}))+b_{\eta}(f(\gamma^{\prime}),f(\gamma))\\
&\ -\int_{\Gamma}b_{\eta}(f(\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}),f(\gamma^{\prime}))+b_{\eta}(f(\gamma^{\prime}),f(\gamma))\ d\mu(\gamma^{\prime\prime})\\
=&b_{\eta}(f(\gamma^{\prime}),f(\gamma^{\prime}))-\int_{\Gamma}b_{\eta}(f(\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}),f(\gamma^{\prime}))d\mu(\gamma^{\prime\prime})\end{split}

holds, we see a that
\gamma^{\prime}\mapsto b_{\eta}(f(\gamma^{\prime}),f(\gamma))

is also
\mu
-harmonic.
Note that, for any
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
, we also obtain

b_{\eta}(f(\gamma^{\prime}),f(\gamma))=b_{\xi^{(i)}}(f(\gamma^{\prime}),f(\gamma))

by the equality in (
7.2
).
In particular, by
Lemma
5.1

(2)
,
for any
x=f(\gamma)
and
x^{\prime}=f(\gamma^{\prime})
, the convex hull of

[x,\eta)\cup[x,x^{\prime}]\cup[x^{\prime},\eta)
is a half-infinite
parallelogram, which can be expressed as

F_{xx^{\prime}}^{\eta}=\bigcup_{p\in[x,x^{\prime}]}c_{p}^{\eta}([0,\infty))
.

Take
p_{j}\in[x,x_{j}]
so that
d(x,p_{j})=d
for fixed
d
.
Then
\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(3)}_{j}c_{p_{j}}^{\eta}
turns out to be a geodesic ray

c_{\infty}
with
c_{\infty}(0)=\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(3)}_{j}p_{j}=q\in[x,\xi^{(3)})
.
Since
d(c_{\infty}(t),c_{x}^{\eta}(t))=d
holds for any
t\geq 0
,

c_{\infty}(\infty)=\eta
and
c_{\infty}
should be denoted by

c_{q}^{\eta}
.
Set

F_{x(3)}^{\eta}=\bigcup_{q\in[x,\xi^{(3)})}c_{q}^{\eta}([0,\infty))
.
Then
F_{x(3)}^{\eta}
can be viewed as an ultralimit of

F_{xx_{j}}^{\eta}
.
Since each
F_{xx_{j}}^{\eta}
is flat,

\triangle(x,c_{x}^{x_{j}}(t),c_{x}^{\eta}(t))\subset F_{xx_{j}}^{\eta}
forms a flat triangle, and hence

\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(3)}_{j}\triangle(x,c_{x}^{x_{j}}(t),c_{x}^{\eta}(t))
is also flat.
Letting
t\to\infty
, we see that

F_{x(3)}^{\eta}
is also flat, and hence we may write

F_{x(3)}^{\eta}=\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(3)})
.
By the same argument as we have just seen, we conclude that, for any

\eta\in[\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)}]
and

\eta^{\prime}\in[\eta,\xi^{(3)}]\subset\partial Y^{(3)}
, the convex hull of

[x,\eta^{\prime})\cup[x,x^{\prime}]\cup[x^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
is a half-infinite
parallelogram. And we get

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta^{\prime},\xi^{(4)})=F_{x(4)}^{\eta^{\prime}}\subset Y^{(4)}
by taking
an ultralimit
\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(4)}_{j}F_{xx_{j}}^{\eta^{\prime}}
.
Repeating this leads us to the following definition:

Let

\begin{split}&C_{x}(i,j)=\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{(i)},\xi^{(j)}),\\
&C_{x}(i,\dots,j,k)=\bigcup_{\eta\in\partial C(i,\dots,j)}\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(k)}),\end{split}

and set

C_{x}^{(m)}=C_{x}(1,\dots,m).

Note that, at this moment,
\angle_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})
could be
equal to
\pi
and
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})
could degenerate to
a geodesic line.

7.3.
Nondegeneracy of a cone
C_{x}^{(m)}

In this subsection, we prepare for the proof of
Proposition
7.13
, which asserts that
C_{x}^{(m)}
is a
Euclidean cone over a Euclidean regular
(m-1)
-simplex; we show that a
cone
C_{x}^{(m)}
does not degenerate under our assumption
\theta_{0}>0
.

Lemma 7.8
.

Under our setting, for any
x\in f(\Gamma)
,
q\in[x,\xi^{(m)})
,
and
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
,

[q,\eta)\cup[q,\eta^{\prime})
bounds a flat sector or a flat
half-plane, which we denote by
\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
.
In particular, for any
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
, a
geodesic
[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]
with respect to
d_{\angle}
is contained in

\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
, namely,
\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
is convex with
respcet to
d_{\angle}
.

Proof.

If
m=2
, then

C_{x}^{(2)}=\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)})
.
For any
q\in C^{(2)}
and
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\partial C_{x}^{(2)}
,
we have

[q,\eta)\cup[q,\eta^{\prime})\subset\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)})
,
and since
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)})
is a flat sector, the convex
hull of
[q,\eta)\cup[q,\eta^{\prime})
must be a flat sector

\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
contained in
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)})
,
and the assertion is obvious.

Suppose that the assertion is true for
C_{x}^{(m-1)}
.
Take any
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
, and take

\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}\in C_{x}^{(m-1)}
so that

\eta\in\partial\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{(m)},\tilde{\eta})
and

\eta^{\prime}\in\partial\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{(m)},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime})
.
(If
\eta=\xi^{(m)}
, then any choice of

\tilde{\eta}\in\partial C_{x}^{(m-1)}
makes sense in what follows.)
Suppose first
d{\angle}(\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime})<\pi
.
Then, by our assumption, for any
x\in f(\Gamma)
,

\mathrm{CH}([x,\tilde{\eta})\cup[x,\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}))
is a nondegenerate
flat sector.
Also, as we have seen in the last paragraph of the preceding
subsection, for any

\xi\in[\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}]\subset\partial C_{x}^{(m-1)}
,
we have a half-infinite parallelogram

F_{xx_{j}}^{\xi}=\bigcup_{q^{\prime}\in[x,x_{j}]}c_{q^{\prime}}^{\xi}([0,\infty))
.
By applying Lemma
7.3

(2)
to

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime})\cup\mathrm{Sect}_{x_{j}}(\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime})
,
namely, by setting
p_{1}=x
,
p_{2}=x_{j}
,
\eta_{1}=\tilde{\eta}
, and

\eta_{2}=\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}
in Lemma
7.3
,
we see that, for any
q_{j}\in[x,x_{j}]
,

\mathrm{CH}([q_{j},\tilde{\eta})\cup[q_{j},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}))
is a flat sector.
By taking an ultralimit, we also see that,
for any
q\in[x,\xi^{(m)})
,

\mathrm{CH}([q,\tilde{\eta})\cup[q,\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}))
is a flat sector.
Furthermore,

F_{xx_{j}}^{\xi}=\bigcup_{q^{\prime}\in[x,x_{j}]}c_{q^{\prime}}^{\xi}([0,\infty))

being a half-infinite parallelogram implies that,
by taking an ultralimit again,
for any
\xi\in[\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}]
,

F_{x(m)}^{\xi}=\bigcup_{q\in[x,\xi^{(m)})}c_{q}^{\xi}([0,\infty))

is a flat sector.
Therefore we can apply Lemma
7.3
to

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime})\cup\mathrm{Sect}_{c_{x}^{(m)}(T)}(\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime})

for any
T>0
.
Note that

F_{x(m)}^{\tilde{\eta}}=\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{(m)},\tilde{\eta}),\quad F_{x(m)}^{\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}}=\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{(m)},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}),

and that, for any
q\in[x,\xi^{(m)})
, we have

c_{q}^{\eta}([0,\infty))\subset F_{x(m)}^{\tilde{\eta}},\quad c_{q}^{\eta^{\prime}}([0,\infty))\subset F_{x(m)}^{\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}}.

Therefore, for
s>0
,
we can take
t(s)
and
t^{\prime}(s)
so that

c_{q}^{\eta}(t(s))\in R_{ss}\cap F_{x(m)}^{\tilde{\eta}},\ \text{ and }\ c_{q}^{\eta^{\prime}}(t^{\prime}(s))\in R_{ss}\cap F_{x(m)}^{\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}},

where
R_{ss}
is the convex hull of

[c_{x}^{\tilde{\eta}}(s),c_{x}^{\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}}(s)]\cup[c_{c_{x}^{(m)}(T)}^{\tilde{\eta}}(s),c_{c_{x}^{(m)}(T)}^{\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}}(s)]

for suitable
T>0
.
Applying Lemma
7.3

(4)
to

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime})\cup\mathrm{Sect}_{c_{x}^{(m)}(T)}(\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime})
,
we see that
\triangle(q,c_{q}^{\eta}(t(s)),c_{q}^{\eta^{\prime}}(t^{\prime}(s)))
is a flat
triangle.
Letting
s\to\infty
shows that
\mathrm{CH}([q,\eta)\cup[q,\eta^{\prime}))

is a flat sector.
Also, since

\mathrm{CH}([q,\eta)\cup[q,\eta^{\prime}))=\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\subset\bigcup_{q\in[x,\xi^{(m)})}\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime})=\bigcup_{\xi\in[\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}]}\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi,\xi^{(m)}),

we see that a geodesic

[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]=\partial\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
lies in

\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
.

Now suppose that
\angle_{x}(\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime})=\pi
.
Then

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\tilde{\eta},\xi^{(m)})\cup\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\tilde{\eta}^{\prime},\xi^{(m)})

is a half-plane that can be expressed as

\bigcup_{q\in[x,\xi^{(m)})}([q,\tilde{\eta})\cup[q,\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}))

as we have seen in Remark
7.5
.
Clearly, this half-plane contains
[x,\eta),[x,\eta^{\prime})
.
Thus the assertion is also clear in this case.
∎

The lemma above tells us that
C_{x_{k}}^{(m)}
is not degenerating in
the following sense.

Proposition 7.9
.

Take any
x\in f(\Gamma)
.
Under our setting, the following are true.

(1)
For any
\eta\in\partial C_{x}^{(m-1)}
,

0<\angle_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})<\pi
.

(2)
For any
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\partial C_{x}^{(m-1)}
with

\eta_{1}\not=\eta_{2}
,

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\xi^{(m)})\cap\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{2},\xi^{(m)})=[x,\xi^{(m)})
.

(3)
For any
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\partial C_{x}^{(m-1)}
and

q,q^{\prime}\in[x,\xi^{(m)})
with
\eta_{1}\not=\eta_{2}
and

q\not=q^{\prime}
,

\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\cap\mathrm{Sect}_{q^{\prime}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\emptyset
.

Proof.

(1)
Suppose
\angle_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})=0
.
Since
\mathrm{CH}([x,\eta)\cup[x,\xi^{(m)}))
is a flat sector by
Lemma
7.8
, this means

[x,\eta)=[x,\xi^{(m)})
.
Thus, if we take
q_{j}=c_{x}^{x_{j}}(1)
, we see that

\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m)}_{j}q_{j}=q^{(m)}=c_{x}^{(m)}(1)\in[x,\eta)\subset Y^{(m-1)}
.
Since
q^{(m)},q_{j}\in Y^{(m-1)}
, this means that

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d(q_{j},q^{(m)})=0
.
Hence
q^{(m+1)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m+1)}_{j}q_{j}=q^{(m)}\in[x,\eta)
also holds,
and we see that

[x,q^{(m)}]\subset[x,\xi^{(m)})\cap[x,\xi^{(m+1)})
.
In particular, we get
\angle_{x}(\xi^{(m)},\xi^{(m+1)})=0
.
This contradicts our assumption
\theta_{0}>0
.
Therefore we conclude that
\angle_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})>0
.

Now we show that
\angle_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})<\pi
.
Take
q^{(m)}
,
q^{(m+1)}
, and
q_{j}
as above.
We have
\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m)}_{j}q_{j}=q^{(m)}
.
Suppose that
\angle_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})=\pi
.
Then, for
\tilde{q}=c_{x}^{\eta}(1)
, we have

[q^{(m)},\tilde{q}]=[q^{(m)},x]\cup[x,\tilde{q}]
and

2=d(q^{(m)},\tilde{q})=d(\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m)}_{j}q_{j},\tilde{q})=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d(q_{j},\tilde{q}).

From this, we see that
q^{(m+1)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m+1)}_{j}q_{j}=c_{x}^{(m+1)}(1)

also satisfies

d(q^{(m+1)},\tilde{q})=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d(q_{j},\tilde{q})=2,

and also we have
d(q^{(m+1)},x)=d(x,\tilde{q})=1
by our choice of
q_{j}

and
\tilde{q}
.
Therefore we see that

[q^{(m+1)},\tilde{q}]=[q^{(m+1)},x]\cup[x,\tilde{q}]
.
In particular,

[x,\tilde{q}]\subset[q^{(m)},\tilde{q}]\cap[q^{(m+1)},\tilde{q}]

and
\triangle(q^{(m)},q^{(m+1)},\tilde{q})
is a half-degenerate triangle.

On the other hand, noting Remark
7.5
,
we see that
F_{xx_{j}}^{\eta}\cup F_{xx_{j}}^{(m)}
forms a flat strip in
this case.
Thus we see that

\triangle(q_{j},\tilde{q},q^{(m)})\subset F_{xx_{j}}^{\eta}\cup F_{xx_{j}}^{(m)}

is flat for each
j
.
Since
d(x,x_{j})\to\infty
and

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\angle_{x}(x_{j},\xi^{(m)})=\theta_{0}>0
, the width of

F_{xx_{j}}^{\eta}\cup F_{xx_{j}}^{(m)}
tends to
\infty
, and hence,
for almost all
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
with respect to
\lambda
, we may assume that

c_{\tilde{q}}^{q_{j}}
is defined for
t=2
, and hence

\triangle(\tilde{q},c_{\tilde{q}}^{(m)}(2),c_{\tilde{q}}^{q_{j}}(2))\subset F_{xx_{j}}^{\eta}\cup F_{xx_{j}}^{(m)}

is a flat triangle.
Thus we have

(7.3)

d(c_{\tilde{q}}^{q^{(m)}}(1),c_{\tilde{q}}^{q_{j}}(1))=\frac{1}{2}d(c_{\tilde{q}}^{q^{(m)}}(2),c_{\tilde{q}}^{q_{j}}(2))=\frac{1}{2}d(q^{(m)},c_{\tilde{q}}^{q_{j}}(2)).

However, we know that

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d(q^{(m)},c_{\tilde{q}}^{q_{j}}(2))=d(q^{(m)},q^{(m+1)})=2\sin\frac{\theta_{0}}{2}>0

and

\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d(c_{\tilde{q}}^{q^{(m)}}(1),c_{\tilde{q}}^{q_{j}}(1))=d(c_{\tilde{q}}^{q^{(m)}}(1),c_{\tilde{q}}^{q^{(m+1)}}(1))=d(x,x)=0

holds. This contradicts (
7.3
).
Therefore
\angle_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})
cannot be equal to
\pi
.

(2)
Suppose that there exists a point

q\in\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\xi^{(m)})\cap\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{2},\xi^{(m)})\setminus[x,\xi^{(m)})
.
Since

F=\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\xi^{(m)})\cap\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{2},\xi^{(m)})
is
convex,
[x,q]\subset F
, and we can find

\eta_{i}^{\prime}\in[\eta_{i},\xi^{(m)}]
such that

[x,q]\subset[x,\eta_{i}^{\prime})
.
If
\eta_{1}^{\prime}\not=\eta_{2}^{\prime}
, then we get

\angle_{x}(\eta_{1}^{\prime},\eta_{2}^{\prime})=0
, and this contradicts
Lemma
7.8
, which says that

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1}^{\prime},\eta_{2}^{\prime})
is a flat sector.
Therefore we see that
\eta_{1}^{\prime}=\eta_{2}^{\prime}
and hence

F
is a flat sector; we can express

F=\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})
.

We may assume

\angle_{x}(\eta_{1},\xi^{(m)})\geq\angle_{x}(\eta_{2},\xi^{(m)})

in what follows.
Suppose that
\eta\not=\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
.
Then
\angle_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})<\angle_{x}(\eta_{i},\xi^{(m)})
for
i=1,2
.
Take
q^{\prime}\in(x,\xi^{(m)})
and consider a geodesic ray

[q^{\prime},\eta_{2})\subset\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{2},\xi^{(m)})
.
Since
[q^{\prime},\eta_{2})
is parallel to
[x,\eta_{2})
and

\angle_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})<\angle_{x}(\eta_{2},\xi^{(m)})
,
it must intersect with
(x,\eta)
at some point in

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})\subset\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{i},\xi^{(m)})
.
Denote this point by
q^{\prime\prime}
.
Since we are assuming

\angle_{x}(\eta_{1},\xi^{(m)})\geq\angle_{x}(\eta_{2},\xi^{(m)})
,
a geodesic segment
[q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}]
can be extended to a geodesic ray in

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\xi^{(m)})
; we denote its endpoint by
\eta_{1}^{\prime\prime}
.
Since
[q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}]\subset[q^{\prime},\eta_{2})\cap[q^{\prime},\eta_{1}^{\prime\prime})
,
we see that
\angle_{q^{\prime}}(\eta_{2},\eta_{1}^{\prime\prime})=0
.
On the other hand, by our choice of
\eta
and our assumption,
we have
\eta_{1}^{\prime\prime}\not=\eta_{2}
, and hence,
by Lemma
7.8
, we see that

\mathrm{CH}([q^{\prime},\eta_{1}^{\prime\prime})\cup[q^{\prime},\eta_{2}))
is a flat sector.
This contradicts
\angle_{q^{\prime}}(\eta_{2},\eta_{1}^{\prime\prime})=0
.
Thus we see that
\eta=\eta_{2}
holds, and we may
assume that

[x,\eta_{2})\subset\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\xi^{(m)})
.

Let
p^{(m)}=c_{x}^{(m)}(1)
and
q_{1}=c_{x}^{\eta_{1}}(1)
.
Then we see that
[q_{1},p^{(m)}]
and
[x,\eta_{2})
intersect at a
point.
Denote this ponit by
q_{2}
.
Then
[q_{1},q_{2}]\subset\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\subset Y^{(m-1)}
.
Take
p_{j}=c_{x}^{x_{j}}(1)
.
Then we see that
\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m)}_{j}p_{j}=p^{(m)}
and

\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m)}_{j}[q_{1},p_{j}]=[q_{1},p^{(m)}]
.
Since
\eta_{1}\not=\eta_{2}
, there exists

\bar{q}\in(q_{1},q_{2})\subset[q_{1},p^{(m)}]
, and
we can take
p_{j}^{\prime}\in(q_{1},p_{j})
so that
\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m)}_{j}p_{j}^{\prime}=\bar{q}
.
Since
p_{j}^{\prime},\bar{q}\in Y^{(m-1)}
,
\{p_{j}^{\prime}\}
actually converges to

\bar{q}
in
Y^{(m-1)}
.
Therefore we also have
\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m+1)}p_{j}^{\prime}=\bar{q}\in Y^{(m-1)}
, in
particular,
[q_{1},\bar{q}]\subset[q_{1},p^{(m)}]\cap[q_{1},p^{(m+1)}]
, where

p^{(m+1)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m+1)}p_{j}
.
By applying Lemma
7.3

(4)
to

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\xi^{(m)})\cup\mathrm{Sect}_{x_{j}}(\eta_{1},\xi^{(m)})
, we see
that
\triangle(q_{1},p^{(m)},p_{j})
is a flat triangle for any
j
.
Thus so is
\triangle(q_{1},p^{(m)},p^{(m+1)})
.
Since
\angle_{x}(\xi^{(m)},\xi^{(m+1)})=\theta_{0}>0
, we know that

p^{(m+1)}\not=p^{(m)}
.
However,
[q_{1},\bar{q}]\subset[q_{1},p^{(m)}]\cap[q_{1},p^{(m+1)}]
implies

\angle_{q_{1}}(p^{(m)},p^{(m+1)})=0
.
Since
d(q_{1},p^{(m)})=d(q_{1},p^{(m+1)})=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d(q_{1},p_{j})
and

\triangle(q_{1},p^{(m)},p^{(m+1)})
is flat, this means that

p^{(m)}=p^{(m+1)}
.
A contradiction.
Therefore we conclude that

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\xi^{(m)})\cap\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{2},\xi^{(m)})=[x,\xi^{(m)})
.

(3)
Suppose that

p\in\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\cap\mathrm{Sect}_{q^{\prime}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})

and
p\not=q
.
Since
\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
is a flat sector, we see that

[p,\eta_{i})\subset\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
for
i=1,2
,
and hence

\mathrm{Sect}_{p}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\subset\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
.
If
p\in[x,\xi^{(m)})
, then
[q,p]\subset[x,\xi^{(m)})
.
Since
\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
is a flat sector, there exists

\eta\in[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]\subset\partial C_{x}^{(m-1)}
such that

[q,p]\subset[q,\eta)
.
Then we see that
\angle_{q}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})=0
.
However, since

\mathrm{CH}([q,\eta)\cup[q,\xi^{(m)}))
is a flat sector
by Lemma
7.8
,
we get
\angle_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})=\angle_{q}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})=0
.
This contradicts
(1)
above.
Thus
p\not\in[x,\xi^{(m)})
.

Then there exists

\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]\subset\partial C_{x}^{(m-1)}

such that
p\in[q,\eta)
and
p\in[q^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
hold.
Suppose that
\eta\not=\eta^{\prime}
.
Then, since
[q,\eta)\subset\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})
and

[q^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\subset\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta^{\prime},\xi^{(m)})
, this means that

p\in\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})\cap\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta^{\prime},\xi^{(m)})\setminus[x,\xi^{(m)})
,
and this contradicts
(2)
above.
Thus we have
\eta=\eta^{\prime}
and

p\in\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})
.
Since
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})
is a flat sector,

[q,\eta)
and
[q^{\prime},\eta)
cannot intersect unless
either
q\in[q^{\prime},\eta)
or
q^{\prime}\in[q,\eta)
.
Since
q,q^{\prime}\in[x,\xi^{(m)})
, this again implies
either
\angle_{q}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})=0
or

\angle_{q^{\prime}}(\eta,\xi^{(m)})=0
.
Since
\mathrm{CH}([q,\xi^{(m)})\cup[q,\eta))
and

\mathrm{CH}([q^{\prime},\xi^{(m)})\cup[q^{\prime},\eta))
are both flat
sectors by Lemma
7.8
, this means that

\eta=\xi^{(m)}
and
p\in[x,\xi^{(m)})
, which is impossible.
Thus we conclude that

\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\cap\mathrm{Sect}_{q^{\prime}}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\emptyset
.
∎

Lemma 7.10
.

For any
x\in f(\Gamma)
,
\xi\in\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
and
s\geq 0
,

\tau_{s}^{\xi}\colon q\mapsto c_{q}^{\xi}(s)
gives an isometric
translation on
C_{x}^{(m)}
, that is,
\tau_{s}^{\xi}
satisfies

d(\tau_{s}^{\xi}(q),\tau_{s}^{\xi}(q^{\prime}))=d(q,q^{\prime})
for any

q,q^{\prime}\in C_{x}^{(m)}
and

\tau_{s}^{\xi}([q,\eta))=[\tau_{s}^{\xi}(q),\eta)
for any

q\in C_{x}^{(m)}
and
\eta\in\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
.

Proof.

We show that
\tau_{s}^{\xi}
defined in
Lemma
7.3
induces an
isometric translation from
C_{x}^{(m)}
into
C_{x}^{(m)}
by
induction on
m
.
If
m=2
, then

C_{x}^{(2)}=\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)})
and the
assertion is clear.

Suppose that the assertion is true for
C_{x}^{(m-1)}
.
First note that, for any flat sector
\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
, by
identifying it with a flat sector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
with vertex at the origin,
we can find a point
\bar{q}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
corresponding to

q^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
.
We also have
\bar{p}
and
\bar{p}^{\prime}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
corresponding to

c_{q}^{\eta}(1)
and
c_{q}^{\eta^{\prime}}(1)
respectively.
Then through this identification, for any
s,t\geq 0
, we can write

(7.4)

\tau_{s}^{\eta^{\prime}}\circ\tau_{t}^{\eta}(q^{\prime})=s\bar{p}^{\prime}+t\bar{p}+\bar{q}^{\prime}=t\bar{p}+s\bar{p}^{\prime}+\bar{q}^{\prime}=\tau_{t}^{\eta}\circ\tau_{s}^{\eta^{\prime}}(q^{\prime}).

Note that this makes sense even if
\angle_{q}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=\pi

and
\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
degenerates to a geodesic line,
although the expression
s\bar{p}^{\prime}+t\bar{p}
is not unique.

Take
\xi\in\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
.
Then there exists
\eta_{1}\in C_{x}^{(m-1)}
such that

\xi\in\partial\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\xi^{(m)})
.
We show that there exists
a_{1},a_{2}\geq 0
such that we can express

\tau_{s}^{\xi}=\tau_{a_{1}s}^{\eta_{1}}\circ\tau_{a_{2}s}^{\xi^{(m)}},

and that two maps on the right-hand side are isometric translations.

Since
[x,\xi)\subset\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\xi^{(m)})
,
we can express as
c_{x}^{\xi}(t)=(a_{1}t)p_{1}+(a_{2}t)p^{(m)}
, where

p_{1}=c_{x}^{\eta_{1}}(1)
and
p^{(m)}=c_{x}^{(m)}(1)
.
By the description (
7.4
),
we see that

\tau_{s}^{\xi}(q)=\tau_{a_{1}s}^{\eta_{1}}\circ\tau_{a_{2}s}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q)

holds for
q\in\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\xi^{(m)})
.
Now take any
q^{\prime}\in C_{x}^{(m)}
. Then there exists

\eta_{2}\in\partial C_{x}^{(m-1)}
such that

q^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{2},\xi^{(m)})
holds, and we can express

q^{\prime}=c_{q}^{\eta_{2}}(t)
for some
q\in[x,\xi^{(m)})
and

t\geq 0
.
Since
q,\tau_{a_{2}s}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q)\in[x,\xi^{(m)})
,
the convex hulls of
[q,\xi)\cup[q,\eta_{2})
,

[\tau_{a_{2}s}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q),\eta_{1})\cup[\tau_{a_{2}s}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q),\eta_{2})
,
and
[q,\eta_{2})\cup[q,\xi^{(m)})

are flat sectors expressed as
\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\xi,\eta_{2})
,

\mathrm{Sect}_{\tau_{a_{2}s}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q)}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
, and

\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta_{2},\xi^{(m)})
, respectively, by
Lemma
7.8
.
Then we have

\begin{split}\tau_{s}^{\xi}(c_{q}^{\eta_{2}}(t))&=\tau_{t}^{\xi}\circ\tau_{t}^{\eta_{2}}(q)\overset{(1)}{=}\tau_{t}^{\eta_{2}}\circ\tau_{s}^{\xi}(q)\overset{(4)}{=}\tau_{t}^{\eta_{2}}\circ\tau_{a_{1}s}^{\eta_{1}}\circ\tau_{a_{2}s}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q)\\
&\overset{(2)}{=}\tau_{a_{1}s}^{\eta_{1}}\circ\tau_{t}^{\eta_{2}}\circ\tau_{a_{2}s}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q)\overset{(3)}{=}\tau_{a_{1}s}^{\eta_{1}}\circ\tau_{a_{2}s}^{\xi^{(m)}}\circ\tau_{t}^{\eta_{2}}(q)\\
&=\tau_{a_{1}s}^{\eta_{1}}\circ\tau_{a_{2}s}^{\xi^{(m)}}(c_{q}^{\eta_{2}}(t)),\end{split}

where we deduce equalities
(1)
,
(2)
, and
(3)

by applying (
7.4
)
on
\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\xi,\eta_{2})
,

\mathrm{Sect}_{\tau_{a_{2}s}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q)}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
, and

\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta_{2},\xi^{(m)})
, respectively, and
(4)
follows
from the fact that

\tau_{s}^{\xi}(q)=\tau_{a_{1}s}^{\eta_{1}}\circ\tau_{a_{2}s}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q)

holds for
q\in[x,\xi^{(m)})\subset\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\xi^{(m)})
,
which we have just seen above.
Thus we see that, for any
q^{\prime}\in C_{x}^{(m)}
,

\tau_{t}^{\xi}(q^{\prime})=\tau_{a_{2}t}^{\eta_{1}}\circ\tau_{a_{1}t}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q^{\prime})

holds.

Next we show that
\tau_{t}^{\xi^{(m)}}
and
\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}
are
both isometries.
First we consider
\tau_{t}^{\xi^{(m)}}
.
Take any
q_{i}\in C_{x}^{(m)}
,
i=1,2
.
Then we have
\eta_{i}^{\prime}\in C_{x}^{(m-1)}
,
i=1,2
, so that

q_{i}\in\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{i}^{\prime},\xi^{(m)})
, and there exists

\hat{\eta}_{i}\in[\eta_{i}^{\prime},\xi^{(m)}]
such that

q_{i}\in[x,\hat{\eta}_{i})\subset\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{i}^{\prime},\xi^{(m)})
.
We can express
q_{i}=c_{x}^{\hat{\eta}_{i}}(t_{i})
for some
t_{i}\geq 0
, and
we know that there exists a flat sector

\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\hat{\eta}_{1},\hat{\eta}_{2})
for each

q\in[x,\xi^{(m)})
by Lemma
7.8
.
Noting that

\tau_{t}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q_{i})=\tau_{t}^{\xi^{(m)}}\circ\tau_{t_{i}}^{\hat{\eta}_{i}}(x)=\tau_{t_{i}}^{\hat{\eta}_{i}}\circ\tau_{t}^{\xi^{(m)}}(x)=\tau_{t_{i}}^{\hat{\eta}_{i}}(c_{x}^{\xi^{(m)}}(t)),

and applying Lemma
7.3

(1)

with
p_{1}=x
,
p_{2}=c_{x}^{\xi^{(m)}}(t)
, and

\eta_{i}=\hat{\eta}_{i}
,
we conclude that the convex hull of

[q_{1},q_{2}]\cup[\tau_{t}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q_{1}),\tau_{t}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q_{2})]

is a flat parallelogram.
In particular, we have

d(q_{1},q_{2})=d(\tau_{t}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q_{1}),\tau_{t}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q_{2}))
.
Therefore
\tau_{t}^{\xi^{(m)}}
preserves the distance.

Now consider
\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}
.
Note that
\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}
is an isometric translation on

C_{x}^{(m-1)}
by our assumption.
Take any
q\in[x,\xi^{(m)})
and choose
q_{j}\in[x,x_{j}]

satisfying
\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m)}_{j}q_{j}=q
.
Then, for any
\eta^{\prime}\in\partial C_{x}^{(m-1)}
, we have

(7.5)

\begin{split}\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m)}_{j}\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(c_{q_{j}}^{\eta^{\prime}}(t))&=\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m)}_{j}c_{c_{q_{j}}^{\eta^{\prime}}(t)}^{\eta_{1}}(s)=c_{c_{q}^{\eta^{\prime}}(t)}^{\eta_{1}}(s)=\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(c_{q}^{\eta^{\prime}}(t))\\
&=\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m)}_{j}c_{q_{j}}^{\eta^{\prime}}(t)).\end{split}

On the other hand, applying
Lemma
7.3

(3)
to

\bigcup_{\tilde{q}\in[x,x_{j}]}\mathrm{Sect}_{\tilde{q}}(\eta_{1},\eta^{\prime})
,
we see that

\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}
maps
R_{0t}\subset F_{xx_{j}}^{\eta^{\prime}}

isometrically onto its image for any
t\geq 0
,
where
R_{0t}
is the convex hull of

[x,x_{j}]\cup[c_{x}^{\eta^{\prime}}(t),c_{x_{j}}^{\eta^{\prime}}(t)]
in this case.
Hence

\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}
maps whole

F_{xx_{j}}^{\eta^{\prime}}=\bigcup_{t\geq 0}R_{0t}
isometrically onto its
image.
By (
7.5
) and

\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m)}_{j}F_{xx_{j}}^{\eta^{\prime}}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(m)}_{j}\bigcup_{\tilde{q}\in[x,x_{j}]}c_{\tilde{q}}^{\eta^{\prime}}([0,\infty))=\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta^{\prime},\xi^{(m)}),

we see that

\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta^{\prime},\xi^{(m)}))=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(F_{xx_{j}}^{\eta^{\prime}}).

Thus we conclude that

\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta^{\prime},\xi^{(m)}))
is a flat sector,
and that
\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}
maps
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta^{\prime},\xi^{(m)})

isometrically onto a flat sector

\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta^{\prime},\xi^{(m)}))
for any

\eta^{\prime}\in\partial C_{x}^{(m-1)}
.
Take any pair of points
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}\in C_{x}^{(m)}
.
Then there exists
q_{i}\in C_{x}^{(m-1)}
,
t_{i}\geq 0
, and

\eta_{i}^{\prime}\in\partial C_{x}^{(m-1)}
,
i=1,2
,
such that

q^{\prime}=c_{q_{1}}^{(m)}(t_{1}),\quad q^{\prime\prime}=c_{q_{2}}^{(m)}(t_{2}),\quad q_{i}\in[x,\eta_{i}^{\prime}),\ i=1,2.

We may assume
t_{1}\leq t_{2}
.
Let
\tilde{q}^{\prime}=c_{q_{2}}^{(m)}(t_{1})
and
\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}=c_{q_{1}}^{(m)}(t_{2})
.
Take
s_{i}
,
i=1,2
, so that

q^{\prime}=c_{c_{x}^{(m)}(t_{1})}^{\eta_{1}^{\prime}}(s_{1})
and

q^{\prime\prime}=c_{c_{x}^{(m)}(t_{2})}^{\eta_{2}^{\prime}}(s_{2})
.
Then we have
\tilde{q}^{\prime}=c_{c_{x}^{(m)}(t_{1})}^{\eta_{2}^{\prime}}(s_{2})

and
\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}=c_{c_{x}^{(m)}(t_{2})}^{\eta_{1}^{\prime}}(s_{1})
, and the
the convex hull of
[q^{\prime},\tilde{q}^{\prime}]\cup[\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime\prime}]
can be
expressed as
R_{s_{1}s_{2}}
.
By applying Lemma
7.3

(1)
to

\bigcup_{q\in[c_{x}^{(m)}(t_{1}),c_{x}^{(m)}(t_{2})]}\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta_{1}^{\prime},\eta_{2}^{\prime})
,
we see that
R_{s_{1}s_{2}}
in this set is a flat
parallelogram and
q^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime\prime}
form a pair of diagonal
vertices.
Another pair of diagonal vertices of
R_{s_{1}s_{2}}
is given by

\tilde{q}^{\prime}
and
\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}
.
The sides of
R_{s_{1}s_{2}}
take the form of

\begin{split}&[q^{\prime},\tilde{q}^{\prime}]=R_{s_{1}s_{2}}\cap\mathrm{Sect}_{c_{x}^{(m)}(t_{1})}(\eta_{1}^{\prime},\eta_{2}^{\prime}),\quad[q^{\prime\prime},\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}]=R_{s_{1}s_{2}}\cap\mathrm{Sect}_{c_{x}^{(m)}(t_{2})}(\eta_{1}^{\prime},\eta_{2}^{\prime}),\\
&[q^{\prime},\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}]=R_{s_{1}s_{2}}\cap\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1}^{\prime},\xi^{(m)}),\quad[\tilde{q}^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}]=R_{s_{1}s_{2}}\cap\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{2}^{\prime},\xi^{(m)}).\end{split}

Since
\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}
is an isometric translation on

C_{x}^{(m-1)}
and
\tau_{s}^{\xi^{(m)}}
is an isometry onto its
image as we have seen, recalling (
7.4
), we
see that, for any
s\geq 0
,

\begin{split}d(\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(q^{\prime}),\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(\tilde{q}^{\prime}))&=d(\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}\circ\tau_{t_{1}}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q_{1}),\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}\circ\tau_{t_{1}}^{\xi^{(m)}}(q_{2}))\\
&=d(\tau_{t_{1}}^{\xi^{(m)}}\circ\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(q_{1}),\tau_{t_{1}}^{\xi^{(m)}}\circ\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(q_{2}))\\
&=d(q_{1},q_{2}).\end{split}

Replacing
t_{1}
with
t_{2}
, we see that

d(\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(q^{\prime\prime}),\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}))=d(q_{1},q_{2}).

As we have seen above, for any
\eta^{\prime}\in\partial C_{x}^{(m-1)}
,

\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}
maps a flat sector
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta^{\prime},\xi^{(m)})

isometrically onto its image.
Hence we also have

\begin{split}d(\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(q^{\prime}),\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}))&=d(q^{\prime},\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime})=d(c_{q_{1}}^{(m)}(t_{1}),c_{q_{1}}^{(m)}(t_{2}))=|t_{2}-t_{1}|,\\
d(\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(\tilde{q}^{\prime}),\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(q^{\prime\prime}))&=d(\tilde{q}^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime})=d(c_{q_{2}}^{(m)}(t_{1}),c_{q_{2}}^{(m)}(t_{2}))=|t_{2}-t_{1}|.\end{split}

Thus we see that
\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(R_{s_{1}s_{2}})
is a
parallelogram having the same side length for any
s\geq 0
.
Looking at

s\mapsto d(\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(q^{\prime}),\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(q^{\prime\prime}))\ \text{ and }s\mapsto d(\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(\tilde{q}^{\prime}),\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime})),

we conlude that both are constant functions as in the proof of
Lemma
7.3

(3)
.
Therefore

d(\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}(q^{\prime}),\tau_{s}^{\eta_{2}}(q^{\prime\prime}))=d(q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime})

for any
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}\in C_{x}^{(m)}
, and hence

\tau_{s}^{\eta_{1}}
maps
C_{x}^{(m)}
isometrically onto its image.

Finally, we show that
\tau_{s}^{\xi}
is a translation.
First note that, by (
7.4
), we see that, for

q\in[x,\xi^{(m)})
and
\eta\in\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
,

\tau_{s}^{\xi}(c_{q}^{\eta}(t))=\tau_{s}^{\xi}\circ\tau_{t}^{\eta}(q)=\tau_{t}^{\eta}\circ\tau_{s}^{\xi}(q)=c_{\tau_{s}^{\xi}(q)}^{\eta}(t).

Varying
t\in[0,\infty)
, we get

\tau_{s}^{\xi}([q,\eta))=[\tau_{s}^{\xi}(q),\eta).

Now take any
q^{\prime}=c_{x}^{\xi^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})\in C_{x}^{(m)}
.
We know that
\tau_{s^{\prime}}^{\xi^{\prime}}
is an isometry, and we have just seen
that it maps
[x,\eta^{\prime})
to
[q^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
for any

\eta^{\prime}\in\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
.
Therefore, for any
\xi,\eta\in\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
,

\tau_{s^{\prime}}^{\xi^{\prime}}
maps a flat sector
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi,\eta)
to a flat
sector bounded by
[q^{\prime},\xi)\cup[q^{\prime},\eta)
, that is,

\mathrm{Sect}_{q^{\prime}}(\xi,\eta)
.
Then, by applying (
7.4
) on

\mathrm{Sect}_{q^{\prime}}(\xi,\eta)
, we see that

\tau_{s}^{\xi}(c_{q^{\prime}}^{\eta}(t))=\tau_{s}^{\xi}\circ\tau_{t}^{\eta}(q^{\prime})=\tau_{t}^{\eta}\circ\tau_{s}^{\xi}(q^{\prime})=c_{\tau_{s}^{\xi}(q^{\prime})}^{\eta}(t),

which means that an isometry
\tau_{s}^{\xi}
maps a geodesic ray
starting from
q^{\prime}\in C_{x}^{(m)}
and terminating at

\eta\in\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
to a geodesic ray terminating at
the same
\eta
, that is,
\tau_{s}^{\xi}
is an isometric
translation.
This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 7.11
.

(1)
For any
x\in f(\Gamma)
,
q\in C_{x}^{(m)}
, and

\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
with
\eta_{1}\not=\eta_{2}
,
the convex hull of
[q,\eta_{1})\cup[q,\eta_{2})
is a flat sector

(
possibly degenerates to a geodesic line
)
.

(2)
Let
\xi,\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
.
Suppose that
0<\angle_{x}(\eta_{i},\xi)<\pi
,
i=1,2
.
Then, for any
p\in[x,\xi)
,

q_{i}\in[x,\eta_{i})
,
\triangle(p,q_{1},q_{2})
is a flat
triangle.

Proof.

(1)
Take any
q\in C_{x}^{(m)}
.
Expressing
q
as
q=c_{x}^{\xi}(s)
, we see that

\tau_{s}^{\xi}\colon C_{x}^{(m)}\rightarrow C_{x}^{(m)}

is an isometric translation with
\tau_{s}^{\xi}(x)=q

by Lemma
7.10
above.
Since

[q,\eta_{1})\cup[q,\eta_{2})\subset\tau_{s}^{\xi}(\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2}))
,
and
\tau_{s}^{\xi}(\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2}))
is a flat sector,
it is clear that

\mathrm{CH}([q,\eta_{1})\cup[q,\eta_{2}))\subset\tau_{s}^{\xi}(\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2}))
.
On the other hand, for any
q_{1}\in[q,\eta_{1})
and

q_{2}\in[q,\eta_{2})
,
[q_{1},q_{2}]
must be contained in

\mathrm{CH}([q,\eta)\cup[q,\eta^{\prime}))
.
Therefore

\tau_{s}^{\xi}(\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2}))\subset\mathrm{CH}([q,\eta)\cup[q,\eta^{\prime}))
.
Thus we conclude that
\mathrm{CH}([q,\eta)\cup[q,\eta^{\prime}))
is a flat
sector.

(2)
If
\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}
, then
\triangle(p,q_{1},q_{2})
lies in

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{1},\xi)
, and hence it is flat.
Suppose that
\eta_{1}\not=\eta_{2}
.
We can write
q_{i}=c_{x}^{\eta_{i}}(s_{i})
, and we may assume that

s_{1}\geq s_{2}
.
By our assumption
\angle_{x}(\eta_{i},\xi)<\pi
,
we have a non-trivial flat sector

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi,\eta_{2})
.
Since
[p,q_{2}]
is a geodesic segment in

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi,\eta_{2})
joining points in

[x,\xi)
and
[x,\eta_{2})
, by sliding it by

\tau_{t}^{\xi}
with
t>0
, we see that

\tau_{t}^{\xi}([p,q_{2}])
can be extended to
a geodesic segment
[\tau_{t}^{\xi}(p),q_{2}^{\prime}]
, where

q_{2}^{\prime}\in[x,\eta_{2})
.
Now we can take
t
so that
q_{2}^{\prime}=c_{x}^{\eta_{2}}(s_{1})
, and
take
p^{\prime}\in[x,\xi)
so that

p,\tau_{t}^{\xi}(p)\in[x,p^{\prime}]
.
By (1), we have a flat sector

\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
for any
q\in[x,\xi)
, and we can
consider
\bigcup_{q\in[x,p^{\prime}]}\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
.

Suppose that
d_{\angle}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})<\pi
.
Since
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi,\eta)
is a flat sector for any

\eta\in[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]\subset\partial C_{x}^{(m)}
,

\bigcup_{q\in[x,p^{\prime}]}[q,\eta)\subset\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi,\eta)
is a
(possibly degenerated) half-infinite parallelogram, and hence
we can apply Lemma
7.3

to
\bigcup_{q\in[x,p^{\prime}]}\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
by setting

p_{1}=x
,
p_{2}=p^{\prime}
.
By our choice of
t
, we see that

\tau_{t}^{\xi}(q_{1}),q_{2}^{\prime}\in R_{s_{1}s_{1}}
,
where
R_{s_{1}s_{1}}
is the convex hull of

[c_{x}^{\eta_{1}}(s_{1}),c_{x}^{\eta_{2}}(s_{1})]\cup[c_{p^{\prime}}^{\eta_{1}}(s_{1}),c_{p^{\prime}}^{\eta_{2}}(s_{1})]
,
and that

\triangle(\tau_{t}^{\xi}(p),\tau_{t}^{\xi}(q_{1}),q_{2}^{\prime})
is
flat by Lemma
7.3

(4)
.
Note that
\tau_{t}^{\xi}(\triangle(p,q_{1},q_{2}))
is a subset of the
convex hull of

\triangle(\tau_{t}^{\xi}(p),\tau_{t}^{\xi}(q_{1}),q_{2}^{\prime})
,
and hence it is flat.
By Lemma
7.10
,
\tau_{t}^{\xi}
is an isometric
translation,
we conclude that
\triangle(p,q_{1},q_{2})
is also flat.

While if
d_{\angle}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\pi
, then, according to
Remark
7.5
,
\triangle(p,q_{1},q_{2})
is lying in
a flat strip, and hence it is flat.
This completes the proof.
∎

7.4.
Existence of a Euclidean simplex

Let
l
be a natural number and consider
C_{x}^{(l)}
.
Take any
q\in C_{x}^{(l)}
.
Then there exists
\eta\in\partial C_{x}^{(l-1)}
such that

q\in\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(l)})
.
Then
q
must lie on
[\hat{q},\eta)
for some

\hat{q}\in[x,\xi^{(l)})
;
we can write
q=c_{\hat{q}}^{\eta}(t)
.
If
q\in C_{x}^{(l-1)}
, we can take
\hat{q}=x
.
If
q\not\in C_{x}^{(l-1)}
, then
take
p_{j}\in[x,x_{j}]
so that
\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(l)}p_{j}=\hat{q}
.
Then
\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(l)}c_{p_{j}}^{\eta}(t)=c_{\hat{q}}^{\eta}(t)=q
.
Keep this notation and define a map on
C_{x}^{(l)}
by

\Phi_{1}(q)=\begin{cases}q&q\in C_{x}^{(l-1)},\\
\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(l+1)}c_{p_{j}}^{\eta}(t)&q=C_{x}^{(l)}\setminus C_{x}^{(l-1)}.\end{cases}

Then, since
\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(l+1)}c_{p_{j}}^{\eta}(t)=c_{q^{\prime}}^{\eta}(t)
for

q^{\prime}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(l+1)}p_{j}\in[x,\xi^{(l+1)})
,
we see that

\Phi_{1}(\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(l)}))=\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(l+1)})
,
and obtain

\Phi_{1}\colon C_{x}^{(l)}\rightarrow C_{x}(1,\dots,l-1,l+1)
.
Take
q^{(1)},q^{(2)}\in C_{x}^{(l)}
and write them as

q^{(i)}=c_{\hat{q}_{i}}^{\eta^{i}}(t_{i})
, where

\hat{q}^{(i)}\in[x,\xi^{(l)})
,

\eta^{i}\in\partial C_{x}^{(l-1)}
and

t_{i}\in[0,\infty)
as above.
Take
p^{(i)}_{j}\in[x,x_{j}]
so that

\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(l)}p_{j}^{(i)}=\hat{q}_{i}
.
Then we have

d(q^{(1)},q^{(2)})=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d(c_{p^{(1)}_{j}}^{\eta^{1}}(t_{1}),c_{p^{(2)}_{j}}^{\eta^{2}}(t_{2})),

and we also have

d(\Phi_{1}(q^{(1)}),\Phi_{1}(q^{(2)}))=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}d(c_{p^{(1)}_{j}}^{\eta^{1}}(t_{1}),c_{p^{(2)}_{j}}^{\eta^{2}}(t_{2})),

since

\Phi_{1}(q^{(i)})=\lambda{\text{-}}\!{\lim}^{(l+1)}_{j}c_{p^{(i)}_{j}}^{\eta^{i}}(t_{i})
.
Therefore
\Phi_{1}
is an isometry.
We extend this map to an isometry defined on whole
C_{x}^{(l+1)}
as
follows.

Note that, for any
\eta\in C_{x}^{(l)}
,

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(l+1)})
can be identified with a flat
sector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Take any point
q\in\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{(l+1)},\eta)
.
Through this identification, we can write

q=ap+bp^{(l+1)}=c_{x}^{\eta}(a)+c_{x}^{(l+1)}(b),

where
p=c_{x}^{\eta}(1)
,
p^{(l+1)}=c_{x}^{(l+1)}(1)
.
Note that this description of
q
is unique,
since
0<\angle(\eta,\xi^{(l+1)})<\pi
for any

\eta\in\partial C_{x}^{(l)}
by
Proposition
7.9

(1)
.
Note also that, by Lemma
7.8
with
m=l+1
, the
convex hull of
[x,\xi^{(l)})\cup\Phi_{1}([x,\eta))
is a
flat sector, which we denote by
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta^{\prime},\xi^{(l)})
.
By Proposition
7.9

(2)
, for each

q\in C_{x}^{(l+1)}\setminus\left(C_{x}^{(l)}\cup[x,\xi^{(l+1)})\right)
,
we have unique
\eta\in C_{x}^{(l)}
such that

q\in\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(l+1)})
.
Therefore setting

\Phi(q)=\Phi(ap+bp^{(l+1)})=a\Phi_{1}(p)+bp^{(l)}

gives us a well-defined map on
C_{x}^{(l+1)}
,
and we see that
\Phi
maps

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(l+1)})
onto
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta^{\prime},\xi^{(l)})
.
In this manner, we get a map defined on whole

C_{x}^{(l+1)}=C_{x}(1,\dots,l+1)=\bigcup_{\eta\in\partial C_{x}^{(l)}}\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(l+1)})
.

We will show that
\Phi
is actually an isometry.
Since

\angle(\xi^{(i)},\xi^{(j)})=\theta_{0}
and

\Phi(\xi^{(i)})=\xi^{(j)}
for any
i

(
j=i
if
i\in\{1,\dots,l-1\}
, and
(i,j)=(l,l+1),(l+1,l)

otherwise),

\Phi
is an isometry on each

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{(i)},\xi^{(l+1)})
.
By applying Lemma
7.3

(4)
to

\bigcup_{q\in[x,p^{(l+1)}]}\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\xi^{(i)},\xi^{(j)})
,

i,j\in\{1,\dots,l\}
,
we see that

\triangle(p^{(l+1)},p^{(i)},p^{(j)})
is a flat triangle, where

p^{(k)}=c_{x}^{(k)}(1)
.
The same is true for
\triangle(p^{(l)},\Phi_{1}(p^{(i)}),\Phi_{1}(p^{(j)}))
, and
these two triangles are regular triangles whose side length is equal to

2\sin(\theta_{0}/2)
, hence they are isometric to each other.
Let
p_{t}=(1-t)p^{(i)}+tp^{(j)}\in[p^{(i)},p^{(j)}]
for
t\in[0,1]
.
Since
p_{t}\in C_{x}^{(l)}
,
\Phi(p_{t})=\Phi_{1}(p_{t})
.
Note that
\Phi_{1}
is an isometry with

\Phi_{1}([x,\xi^{(i)}))=[x,\xi^{(\tilde{i})})
, where

\tilde{i}=i
if
1\leq i\leq l-1
, and
\tilde{i}=l+1
if
i=l
.
Then we see that

\Phi(p_{t})=(1-t)p^{(\tilde{i})}+tp^{(\tilde{j})}
holds for any

i,j\in\{1,\dots,l\}
.
Since
\triangle(p^{(l+1)},p^{(i)},p^{(j)})
and

\triangle(p^{(l)},p^{(\tilde{i})},p^{(\tilde{j})})
are isometric to each
other, we see that
d(p_{t},p^{(l+1)})=d(\Phi(p_{t}),p^{(l)})
.
Also since we know that
\triangle(x,p^{(i)},p^{(j)})
and

\triangle(x,p^{(\tilde{i})},p^{(\tilde{j})})
are flat and isometric
to each other,
d(x,p_{t})=d(x,\Phi(p_{t}))
.
Since

\triangle(p^{(l+1)},x,p_{t})
is lying in
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi(t),\xi^{(l+1)})

for
\xi(t)\in\partial C_{x}^{(l)}
satisfying

p_{t}\in[x,\xi(t))
, it is obviously flat.
The same is true for
\triangle(p^{(l)},x,\Phi(p_{t}))
lying in

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\tilde{\xi}(t),\xi^{l})
, where

\tilde{\xi}(t)\in\partial C_{x}^{(l+1)}
satisfies

\Phi(p_{t})\in[x,\tilde{\xi}(t))
.

Since
d(p_{t},p^{(l+1)})=d(\Phi(p_{t}),p^{(l)})
and

d(x,p_{t})=d(x,\Phi(p_{t}))
hold as we have seen above,
together with
d(x,p^{(l+1)})=d(x,p^{(l)})=1
, we see
that
\triangle(p^{(l+1)},x,p_{t})
and
\triangle(p^{(l)},x,\Phi(p_{t}))

are isometric to each other, and in particular,

\angle_{x}(p_{t},p^{(l+1)})=\angle_{x}(\Phi(p_{t}),p^{(l)})
.
Recalling the defintion of
\Phi
, we conclude that

\Phi\colon\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi(t),\xi^{(l+1)})\rightarrow\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\tilde{\xi}(t),\xi^{(l)})

is an isometry.

Now fix
t\in(0,1)
and let
p^{(t)}=c_{x}^{\xi(t)}(1)
.
By applying Lemma
7.3

(4)
to

\bigcup_{q\in[x,c_{x}^{\xi(t)}(T)]}\mathrm{Sect}_{q}(\xi^{(k)},\xi^{(l+1)})

for
k\in\{1,\dots,l\}
and suitable
T>0
,
we see that
\triangle(p^{(l+1)},p^{(t)},p^{(k)})
is a flat triangle, and the
same is true for
\triangle(p^{(l)},\Phi(p^{(t)}),\Phi(p^{(k)}))
.
Since we already know that

\begin{split}&d(p^{(t)},p^{(l+1)})=d(\Phi(p^{(t)}),p^{(l)}),\quad d(p^{(l+1)},p^{(k)})=d(p^{(l)},\Phi(p^{(k)})),\\
&d(p^{(t)},p^{(k)})=d(\Phi_{1}(p^{(t)}),\Phi_{1}(p^{(k)}))=d(\Phi(p^{(t)}),\Phi(p^{(k)}))\end{split}

hold, by the same argument as above, we see that
\Phi
is an isometry on

\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\xi^{\prime},\xi^{(l+1)})
for any

\xi^{\prime}\in[\xi(t),\xi^{(k)}]\subset\partial C_{x}^{(l)}
with

k\not=i,j
and
t\in[0,1]
.
Repeating this shows that

\Phi
is an isometry on
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(l+1)})
for
any
\eta\in C_{x}^{(l)}
.

Now take any
q_{1},q_{2}\in C_{x}^{(l+1)}
.
If there exists
\eta\in\partial C_{x}^{(l)}
such that

q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta,\xi^{(l+1)})
, then we already know

d(q_{1},q_{2})=d(\Phi(q_{1}),\Phi(q_{2}))
.
Suppose there is no such
\eta
.
Then we have
\eta_{i}\in\partial C_{x}^{(l)}
satisfying

q_{i}\in\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{i},\xi^{(l+1)})
.
By Proposition
7.9

(1)
,
we can find
p\in[x,\xi^{(l+1)})
and

q_{i}^{\prime}\in[x,\eta_{i})
so that
q_{i}\in[p,q_{i}^{\prime}]
,
and we see that
Proposition
7.11

(2)
implies that

\triangle(p,q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})
is flat.
Note that

[p,q_{i}^{\prime}]\subset\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{i},\xi^{(l+1)})
and

[q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime}]\subset C_{x}^{(l)}
.
Therefore we have
d(p,q_{i}^{\prime})=d(\Phi(p),\Phi(q_{i}^{\prime}))
,
i=1,2
, and

d(q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})=d(\Phi(q_{1}^{\prime}),\Phi(q_{2}^{\prime}))
.
Since
\Phi
maps
\mathrm{Sect}_{x}(\eta_{i},\xi^{(l+1)})
isometrically onto its
image, we know that

0<\angle_{x}(\Phi(q_{i}^{\prime}),\Phi(p))=\angle_{x}(\eta_{i},\xi^{(l+1)})<\pi

by Proposition
7.9

(1)
.
Thus, by applying Proposition
7.11

(2)
to

\Phi(p)\in[x,\xi^{(l)})
and

\Phi(q_{i}^{\prime})\in\Phi([x,\eta_{i}))
,
i=1,2
,
we see that

\triangle(\Phi(p),\Phi(q_{1}^{\prime}),\Phi(q_{2}^{\prime}))
is also flat.
Therefore we conclude that
\Phi
maps
\triangle(p,q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})

isometrically onto
\triangle(\Phi(p),\Phi(q_{1}^{\prime}),\Phi(q_{2}^{\prime}))
, and
in particular,
d(\Phi(q_{1}),\Phi(q_{2}))=d(q_{1},q_{2})
.

Repeating this procedure allows us to extend
\Phi
to an isometry
defined on
C_{x}^{(m)}
for
m>l+1
; we get an isometry on

C_{x}^{(m)}
permuting
\xi^{(l)}
and
\xi^{(l+1)}
.
Composing such maps suitably, we obtain the following proposition.

Proposition 7.12
.

Let
s
be a permutation of
\{1,\dots,m\}
.
Then there exists an isometry

\Phi_{s}\colon C_{x}^{(m)}\rightarrow C_{x}^{(m)}
which
satisfies
\Phi_{s}([x,\xi^{(i)}))=[x,\xi^{(s(i))})
.

Proposition 7.13
.

Fix
r>0
, and
define
\sigma^{(j)}
inductively by

\sigma(i)=\{p^{(i)}\},\quad\sigma(i,\dots,j,k)=\bigcup_{p\in\sigma(i,\dots,j)}[p,p^{(k)}],\quad\sigma^{(j)}=\sigma(1,\dots,j),

where
p^{(j)}=c_{x}^{(j)}(r)
.
Then, under our setting, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\sigma^{(n-1)}
is
isometric to a regular Euclidean
(n-1)
-simplex.

Proof.

First note that
\sigma^{(n-1)}
is a slice of
C_{x}^{(n)}

containing
\{p^{(1)},\dots,p^{(n)}\}
and left invariant by an
isometry
\Phi_{s}
in Proposition
7.12
for any
permutation
s
. In particular,
[p^{(i)},p^{(j)}]
has the same length

2r\sin(\theta_{0}/2)
for
i,j=1,\dots,n
with
i\not=j
.
Let
S(1,\dots,n)
be a Euclidean regular

(n-1)
-simplex with vertices
\bar{p}_{1},\dots,\bar{p}_{n}
and

d(p_{i},p_{j})=2r\sin(\theta_{0}/2)
for
i\not=j
.
We also denote, for
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
,

(m-1)
-face with vertices
\{\bar{p}_{i_{1}},\dots,\bar{p}_{i_{m}}\}
by

S(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})
.

We show that there is an isometry from

S(1,\dots,n)
onto

\sigma^{(n-1)}
by induction on
n
.
Note that, for any point
\bar{q}
in
S(1,\dots,m-1)
,
m=1,\dots,n
,
there exists a unique segment

[\bar{q},\bar{p}_{m}]\subset S(1,\dots,m)
,
and we know that

S(1,\dots,m)=\bigcup_{\bar{q}\in S(1,\dots,m-1)}[\bar{q},\bar{p}_{m}].

Furthermore, if

\bar{q}\not=\bar{q}^{\prime}\in S(1,\dots,m-1)
, then

[\bar{q},\bar{p}_{m}]\cup[\bar{q}^{\prime},\bar{p}_{m}]=\{\bar{p}_{m}\}
.
Keeping this in mind, we can define a surjection

\Psi\colon S(1,\dots,n)\rightarrow\sigma^{(n)}
inductively by

\begin{cases}\Psi(\bar{p}_{1})=p^{(1)},&\\
\Psi(\bar{c}_{\bar{q}}^{\bar{p}_{j}}(t))=c_{\Psi(\bar{q})}^{p^{(j)}}(dt),&\bar{q}\in S(1,\dots,j-1),\ j=2,\dots,n,\end{cases}

where
\bar{c}_{\bar{q}}^{\bar{p}_{j}}

is a geodesic joining
\bar{q}
and
\bar{p}_{j}
in
S(1,\dots,j)
,
and
d=d(\Psi(\bar{q}),p^{(j)})/d(\bar{q},\bar{p}_{j})
.

It is obvious that
\Psi
is an isometry on
S(1)
.
Suppose that
\Psi\colon S(1,\dots,n-1)\rightarrow\sigma^{(n-2)}
is
an isometry.
Take
\bar{q}_{1},\bar{q}_{2}\in S(1,\dots,n)
.
We will show that
\Psi
maps

\triangle(\bar{q}_{1},\bar{q}_{2},\bar{p}_{n})
isometrically
into
\sigma^{(n-1)}
.
Note that, for
\bar{q}_{i}\in S(1,\dots,n)
, there exists

\bar{q}_{i}^{\prime}\in S(1,\dots,n-1)
such that

\bar{q}_{i}\in[\bar{q}_{i}^{\prime},\bar{p}_{n}]
. (If
\bar{q}_{i}=\bar{p}_{n}
, then
take any
\bar{q}_{i}^{\prime}\in S(1,\dots,n-1)
.)
Furthermore,
[\bar{q}_{1}^{\prime},\bar{q}_{2}^{\prime}]
can be extended to

[\bar{q}_{1}^{\prime\prime},\bar{q}_{2}^{\prime\prime}]
so that

\bar{q}_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in S(1,\dots,\hat{l}(i),\dots,n-1)
for some

l(i)\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
.
Since
S(1,\dots,n)
is a Euclidean regular

(n-1)
-simplex and
\bar{q}_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in S(1,\dots,\hat{l}(i),\dots,n-1)
,
we know that
d(\bar{q}_{i}^{\prime\prime},\bar{p}_{l(i)})=d(\bar{q}_{i}^{\prime\prime},\bar{p}_{n})
.
By definition, we see that

\Psi(\bar{q}_{i}^{\prime\prime})\in\sigma(1,\dots,\hat{l}(i),\dots,p^{(n-1)})
.
By Proposition
7.12
, we have an isometry

\Phi_{s}
which fixes

\sigma(1,\dots,\hat{l}(i),\dots,n-1)
and switches

p^{(l(i))}
and
p^{(n)}
.
Thus we see that

d(\Psi(\bar{q}_{i}^{\prime\prime}),p^{(n)})=d(\Psi(\bar{q}_{i}^{\prime\prime}),p^{(l(i))})
.
Since
\Psi
restricted to
S(1,\dots,n-1)
is an
isometry, noting that
\Psi(\bar{p}_{l(i)})=p^{(l(i))}
and

\Psi(\bar{p}_{n})=p^{(n)}
, we see that

d(\bar{q}_{i}^{\prime\prime},\bar{p}_{n})=d(\bar{q}_{i}^{\prime\prime},\bar{p}_{l(i)})=d(\Psi(\bar{q}_{i}^{\prime\prime}),\Psi(\bar{p}_{l(i)}))=d(\Psi(\bar{q}_{i}^{\prime\prime}),\Psi(\bar{p}_{n})),

as well as

d(\bar{q}_{1}^{\prime\prime},\bar{q}_{2}^{\prime\prime})=d(\Psi(\bar{q}_{1}^{\prime\prime}),\Psi(\bar{q}_{2}^{\prime\prime}))
.
This shows that
\Psi
maps
[\bar{q}_{1}^{\prime\prime},\bar{q}_{2}^{\prime\prime}]
and

[\bar{q}_{i}^{\prime\prime},\bar{p}_{n}]
,
i=1,2
, isometrically into

\sigma^{(n-1)}
.
Furthermore, by Proposition
7.11

(2)
, we know
that
\triangle(\Psi(\bar{q}_{1}^{\prime\prime}),\Psi(\bar{q}_{2}^{\prime\prime}),p^{(n)})
is flat.
By the definition of
\Psi
, we conlude that
\Psi
maps the convex
hull of
\triangle(\bar{q}_{1}^{\prime\prime},\bar{q}_{2}^{\prime\prime},\bar{p}_{n})
isometrically onto
that of
\triangle(\Psi(\bar{q}_{1}^{\prime\prime}),\Psi(\bar{q}_{2}^{\prime\prime}),p^{(n)})
.
Since
\triangle(\bar{q}_{1},\bar{q}_{2},\bar{p}_{n})
is
contained in the convex hull of

\triangle(\bar{q}_{1}^{\prime\prime},\bar{q}_{2}^{\prime\prime},\bar{p}_{n})
,
we see that

\triangle(\bar{q}_{1},\bar{q}_{2},\bar{p}_{n})
is also isometrically mapped onto

\triangle(\Psi(\bar{q}_{1}),\Psi(\bar{q}_{2}),p^{(n)})
.
In particular,

d(\bar{q}_{1},\bar{q}_{2})=d(\Psi(\bar{q}_{1}),\Psi(\bar{q}_{2}))
, that is,

\Psi
is an isometry. This completes the proof.
∎

7.5.
Proof of Theorem
6.12

Now we give the proof of Theorem
6.12
.

Proof of Theorem
6.12
.

We first show that
\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\angle_{x_{0}}(x_{j},\xi^{(1)})=\theta_{0}=0
, where

x_{0}=f(e)
, by deriving a contradiction under the assumption that

\theta_{0}>0
.

Suppose first that
Y
has locally finite dimension.
Then, for given
R>0
, there exists
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
any
B\subset B(x_{0},R)
satisfies (
6.3
).
On the other hand, by taking ultralimits
m
-times, we see that

B(x_{0},R)\cap C_{x_{0}}^{(m)}\subset Y^{(m)}
contains an arbtrarily small
Euclidean regular

(m-1)
-simplex
\sigma^{(m-1)}
by Proposition
7.13
.
Note that
\sigma^{(m-1)}
(more precisely the set of vertices

\{p^{(1)},\dots,p^{(m)}\}
) satisfies

\mathrm{rad}_{Y^{(m)}}({\sigma^{(m-1)}})=\sqrt{\frac{m-1}{2m}}\mathrm{diam}(\sigma^{(m-1)}).

If we take
m>n+1
, then we have

\sqrt{\frac{n}{2(n+1)}}<\sqrt{\frac{m-1}{2m}},

and this contradicts Proposition
6.10
.

Now suppose
Y
has telescopic dimension at most
n
.
Let
m>n+1
and take
\delta
so that

\delta+\sqrt{\frac{n}{2(n+1)}}<\sqrt{\frac{m}{2(m+1)}}.

By definition, there exists
D>0
such that any
B\subset Y
with

\mathrm{diam}(B)>D
satisfies (
6.4
).
However, taking
\sigma^{(m-1)}\subset C_{x_{0}}^{(m)}
so that

\mathrm{diam}(\sigma^{(m-1)})>D
, we see that
\sigma^{(m-1)}

does not satisfy (
6.4
) as we have seen above.
This contradicts Proposition
6.10
.
Therefore, in either case, we conclude that
\theta_{0}=0
.

Let
\xi=\xi^{(1)}=\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim x_{j}\in\partial Y^{(1)}
.
We show that
\{x_{j}\}
actually converges to
\xi
in the cone
topology.
By our assumption, by setting

A^{\prime}_{M}=\{j\in{\mathbb{N}}\mid d(x_{0},x_{j})\geq M\}

we have
\lambda(A^{\prime}_{M})=1
for any
M>0
.
Also by setting

B^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}=\{j\in{\mathbb{N}}\mid\angle_{x_{0}}(x_{j},\xi)<\varepsilon\},

we see that

\lambda(B^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})=1
for any
\varepsilon>0
,
since we know
\lambda{\text{-}}\!\lim_{j}\angle_{x_{0}}(x_{j},\xi)=0
.
Therefore, for any
M>0
and
\varepsilon>0
,
we have
\lambda(A^{\prime}_{M}\cap B^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})=1
and hence
we can find
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

d(x_{0},x_{j})\geq M
and

\angle_{x_{0}}(x_{j},\xi)<\varepsilon
.
Note that, by Lemma
6.6
and our assumption,
we have
\int_{\Gamma}b_{\xi}(f(\gamma),f(e))d\mu(\gamma)=0
.
Then by noting that
\{x_{j}\}\subset f(\Gamma)
,
we can apply Lemma
5.1

(2)
,
and see that the convex hull of

[x_{0},\xi)\cup[x_{0},x_{j}]\cup[x_{j},\xi)

is a half-infinite parallelogram.
Hence, for any
t\in[0,d(x_{0},x_{j})]
and a geodesic
c_{j}
with

c_{j}(0)=x_{0}
and
c_{j}(d(x_{0},x_{j}))=x_{j}
, we have

d(c_{j}(t),[x_{0},\xi))=t\sin\angle_{x_{0}}(x_{j},\xi)

as long as
\angle_{x_{0}}(x_{j},\xi)\leq\pi/2
.
Therefore, by letting
M\to\infty
and
\varepsilon\to 0
suitably,
we can choose a sequence
\{j^{\prime}\}\subset{\mathbb{N}}
so that
\{c_{j^{\prime}}\}

converges to a geodesic ray joining
x_{0}
and
\xi
on each
bounded interval in
[0,\infty)
.
In particular,
\{x_{j^{\prime}}\}
converges to
\xi
in the
cone topology of
Y_{\infty}\cup\partial Y_{\infty}
.
Since
\{x_{j^{\prime}}\}\subset Y
, we see that
\xi
must lie in

\partial Y
. This completes the proof.
∎

8.
An upper bound of

l_{\rho}(\Gamma,\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}\mu^{n})

Let
\Gamma
be a countable group equipped with a
symmetric probability measure
\mu
whose support generates
\Gamma
.
Suppose that
\Gamma
acts on a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
space
Y
via a homomorphism

\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
.
Suppose further that there exists a
\rho
-equivariant
\mu
-harmonic
map
f\colon\Gamma\rightarrow Y
.
Recall that if
f
is a
\mu
-harmonic map, then

\gamma\mapsto d(f(e),f(\gamma))
is a
\mu
-subharmonic function
on
\Gamma
(Proposition
2.17
).

As in §
3
, we denote by
\mu^{n}
the

n
-fold convolution of
\mu
;
\mu^{n}
describes the distribution of the
position of a random walk generated by
\mu
at time
n
.
Consider a convex combination of
\mu^{n}
, that is,

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}\mu^{n}
, where
a_{n}\geq 0
and

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}=1
. In what follows, we prove that

(8.1)

l_{\rho}\left(\Gamma,\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}\mu^{n}\right)\leq\left(1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}na_{n}\right)l_{\rho}(\Gamma,\mu).

Although it is weaker than the claim of
[
13
, Theorem 4.5]
,
under the assumption
l_{\rho}(\Gamma,\mu)=0
,
this implies
l_{\rho}(\Gamma,\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}\mu^{n})=0

which is sufficient for our purpose.
The proof follows the line suggested in
[
13
]
, except
that we use the existence of a
\mu
-harmonic map
f
at some point.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
, and note that

\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}\mu^{n}\right)^{k}
is again a convex
combination of
\mu^{j}
’s; we can express as

\left(\sum_{i=n}^{\infty}a_{n}\mu^{n}\right)^{k}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{\prime}\mu^{j}
.
Note that, since
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}=1
,

\begin{split}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}ja_{j}^{\prime}&=\sum_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}(n_{1}+\dots+n_{k})a_{n_{1}}\dots a_{n_{k}}\\
&=\sum_{n_{2},\dots,n_{k}}\left(\sum_{n_{1}}(n_{1}+\dots+n_{k})a_{n_{1}}\right)a_{n_{2}}\dots a_{nk}\\
&=\sum_{n_{2},\dots,n_{k}}\left(\left(\sum_{n_{1}}n_{1}a_{n_{1}}\right)+n_{2}+\dots+n_{k}\right)a_{n_{2}}\dots a_{n_{k}}\\
&=\sum_{i_{1}}n_{1}a_{n_{1}}+\dots+\sum_{n_{k}}n_{k}a_{n_{k}}=k\sum_{n}na_{n}\end{split}

holds.
Now set
b_{i}=\sum_{j=k(i-1)+1}^{ki}a_{j}^{\prime}
(
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
) and

(\tilde{\Omega}^{k},\mathbb{P})=(\Gamma\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0},\mu^{k}\times\sum b_{i}\delta_{i})\times(\Gamma\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0},\mu^{k}\times\sum b_{i}\delta_{i})\times\cdots,

where
\delta_{i}
is a Dirac measure supported on
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
.
An element
\tilde{\omega}
in
\tilde{\Omega}^{k}
can be written as

\tilde{\omega}=\left((\omega_{1},i_{1}),(\omega_{2},i_{2}),\dots,(\omega_{n},i_{n}),\dots\right)
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{n}
be a
\sigma
-algebra of subsets of

\tilde{\Omega}^{k}
generated by subsets of the form

\{(\omega_{1},i_{1})\}\times\dots\times\{(\omega_{n},i_{n})\}\times(\Gamma\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0})\times\dots\times(\Gamma\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0})\times\dots.

Then
\mathcal{F}_{n}\subset\mathcal{F}_{n+1}
holds and

\{\mathcal{F}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}}
forms a
filtration
.
We note that a function

L\colon\tilde{\Omega}^{k}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is

\mathcal{F}_{n}
-measurable means that
L
is determined by the
information up to the
n
th component of

\tilde{\omega}=((\omega_{1},i_{1}),(\omega_{2},i_{2}),\dots)
.
Define a function
\tau\colon\tilde{\Omega}^{k}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
by

\tau(\tilde{\omega})=i_{1}
.
Then
\tau
is a
stopping time
(or
Markov time
); that is,

\tau
satisfies, for any
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
,

\{\tilde{\omega}\in\tilde{\Omega}^{k}\mid\tau(\tilde{\omega})\leq m\}\in\mathcal{F}_{m},

since the left-hand side belongs to
\mathcal{F}_{1}
by the definition
of
\tau
.

Let
L_{n}\colon\tilde{\Omega}^{k}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a

\mathcal{F}_{n}
-measurable function and, for
n\geq m
, denote
by
\mathbb{E}(L_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{m})
the conditional
expectation of
L_{n}
with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{m}
, namely

\mathbb{E}(L_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{m})
is
a
\mathcal{F}_{m}
-measurable function written as

\mathbb{E}(L_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{m})\colon\tilde{\Omega}^{k}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}};\ \mathbb{E}(L_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{m})(\tilde{\omega})=\int_{A_{m}^{n}(\tilde{\omega})}L_{n}(\tilde{\omega}^{\prime})d\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\omega}^{\prime}),

where

A_{m}^{n}(\tilde{\omega})=\{\tilde{\omega}^{\prime}\in\tilde{\Omega}^{k}\mid\tilde{\pi}_{i}(\tilde{\omega}^{\prime})=\tilde{\pi}_{i}(\tilde{\omega}),\ i=1,\dots,m\},

and
\tilde{\pi}_{i}\colon\tilde{\Omega}^{k}\rightarrow\Gamma\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}

is the projection onto the
i
th factor.
Note that
A_{m}^{n}(\tilde{\omega})\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
.
We set
L_{n}
to be a function defined by

L_{n}(\tilde{\omega})=n\mathbb{E}(d(f(e),f(\gamma_{1}^{k}(\tilde{\omega}))))-d(f(e),f(\gamma_{n}^{k}(\tilde{\omega}))),

where
\gamma_{n}^{k}(\tilde{\omega})=\omega_{1}\dots\omega_{n}\in\Gamma
.
Then we see that, by the triangle inequality and the

\rho
-equivariance of
f
,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}(L_{n+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n})(\tilde{\omega})\\
=&(n+1)\mathbb{E}(d(f(e),f(\gamma_{1}^{k}(\tilde{\omega}))))-\int_{A_{n}^{n+1}(\tilde{\omega})}d(f(e),f(\gamma_{n+1}^{k}(\tilde{\omega}^{\prime})))d\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\omega}^{\prime})\\
=&(n+1)\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma))d\mu^{k}(\gamma)-\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma_{n}^{k}(\tilde{\omega})\gamma))d\mu^{k}(\gamma)\\
\geq&(n+1)\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma))d\mu^{k}(\gamma)-d(f(e),f(\gamma_{n}^{k}(\tilde{\omega})))\\
&\phantom{==}-\int_{\Gamma}d(f(\gamma_{n}^{k}(\tilde{\omega})),f(\gamma_{n}^{k}(\tilde{\omega})\gamma))d\mu^{k}(\gamma)\\
=&n\mathbb{E}(d(f(e),f(\gamma_{1}^{k}(\tilde{\omega}))))-d(f(e),f(\gamma_{n}^{k}(\tilde{\omega})))=L_{n}(\tilde{\omega})\end{split}

holds; that is,
L_{n}
is a
submartingale
.
Note that, by our definition of
(\tilde{\Omega}^{k},\mathbb{P})
,
the expectation of

\tilde{\omega}\mapsto d(f(e),\gamma_{n}^{k}(\tilde{\omega}))
can be expressed
as

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}(d(f(e),f(\gamma_{n}^{k}(\omega))))&=\int_{\tilde{\Omega}^{k}}d(f(e),f(\gamma_{n}^{k}(\omega)))d\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma))d\mu^{nk}(\gamma)\\
&=L^{nk}(f,\mu)=L^{nk}(f),\end{split}

where
L^{nk}(f)
is defined in §
3
.

Noting that
\tau\wedge n(\tilde{\omega})=\min\{\tau(\tilde{\omega}),n\}

also gives a stopping time and that
\tau\wedge n
is bounded,
by Doob’s optional stopping theorem, we get

\mathbb{E}(L_{\tau\wedge n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{1})(\tilde{\omega})\geq L_{1}(\tilde{\omega})
,
where

L_{\tau\wedge n}(\tilde{\omega})=(\tau\wedge n)(\tilde{\omega})\mathbb{E}(d(f(e),f(\gamma_{1}^{k}(\tilde{\omega}))))-d(f(e),f(\gamma_{(\tau\wedge n)(\tilde{\omega})}^{k}(\tilde{\omega}))).

Since
\mathbb{E}(L_{1})=0
, by integrating the both sides over

\tilde{\Omega}^{k}
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}(\tau\wedge n)\mathbb{E}(d(f(e),f(\gamma_{1}^{k}(\cdot))))\geq\mathbb{E}(d(f(e),f(\gamma_{(\tau\wedge n)(\cdot)}^{k}(\cdot)))).

Since
\tau\geq\tau\wedge n
, we get

\mathbb{E}(\tau)\mathbb{E}(d(f(e),f(\gamma_{1}^{k}(\cdot))))\geq\mathbb{E}(d(f(e),f(\gamma_{(\tau\wedge n)(\cdot)}^{k}(\cdot)))).

Since

d(f(e),f(\gamma_{(\tau\wedge n)(\tilde{\omega})}(\tilde{\omega})))\geq 0

and

\{d(f(e),f(\gamma_{(\tau\wedge n)(\tilde{\omega})}^{k}(\tilde{\omega})))\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}}

converges to
d(f(e),f(\gamma_{\tau(\tilde{\omega})}^{k}(\tilde{\omega})))

as
n\to\infty
, Fatou’s Lemma implies that

(8.2)

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}(\tau)L^{k}(f,\mu)&\geq\int_{\tilde{\Omega}^{k}}d(f(e),f(\gamma_{\tau(\tilde{\omega})}^{k}(\tilde{\omega})))d\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\omega})\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}b_{i}\left(\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma))d\mu^{ik}(\gamma)\right)\end{split}

by our definition of
(\tilde{\Omega}^{k},\mathbb{P})
.
Since
\gamma\mapsto d(f(e),f(\gamma))
is
\mu
-subharmonic,
we have

\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma^{\prime}\gamma))d\mu(\gamma)\geq d(f(e),f(\gamma^{\prime}))
,
and hence we get, for
m\geq l
,

(8.3)

\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma))d\mu^{m}(\gamma)\geq\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma))d\mu^{l}(\gamma).

On the other hand, recalling the definition of
b_{i}
, we get

(8.4)

\mathbb{E}(\tau)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}ib_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\lceil{\frac{j}{k}}\right\rceil a_{j}^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{k}\left(k+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}ja_{j}^{\prime}\right)=1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}na_{n},

where
\lceil s\rceil
denotes the smallest integer greater than or
equal to
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Combining inequalities (
8.2
), (
8.3
) and
(
8.4
), and recalling that

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{\prime}\mu^{j}=(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}\mu^{n})^{k}
,
we conclude that

\begin{split}\left(1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}na_{n}\right)L^{k}(f,\mu)&\geq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}b_{i}\left(\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma))d\mu^{ik}(\gamma)\right)\\
&\geq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{\prime}\left(\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma))d\mu^{j}(\gamma)\right)\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}d(f(e),f(\gamma))d(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}\mu^{n})^{k}.\end{split}

Therefore we get

\left(1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}na_{n}\right)\frac{L^{k}(f,\mu)}{k}\geq\frac{L^{k}(f,\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}\mu^{n})}{k}.

Letting
k\to\infty
completes the proof of (
8.1
).
